Coupling and Tensorization of Kinetic Theory and Graph Theory Thanks: This project has received funding from the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme under the Marie Skłodowska-Curie grant agreement No. 101034255.
Datong Zhou

Abstract
. We study a non-exchangeable multi-agent system and rigorously derive a strong form of the mean-field limit. The convergence of the connection weights and the initial data implies convergence of large-scale dynamics toward a deterministic limit given by the corresponding extended Vlasov PDE, at any later time and any realization of randomness. This is established on what we call a bi-coupling distance defined through a convex optimization problem, which is an interpolation of the optimal transport between measures and the fractional overlay between graphs. The proof relies on a quantitative stability estimate of the so-called observables, which are tensorizations of agent laws and graph homomorphism densities. This reveals a profound relationship between mean-field theory and graph limiting theory, intersecting in the study of non-exchangeable systems.



1.
Introduction

Graph theory and kinetic theory are two prominent disciplines of mathematics, each with its unique methodologies and applications. The purpose of this article is to draw the attention of both communities to a promising intersection that has been less explored. Specifically, the concepts and techniques from graph theory have significant potential for investigating non-exchangeable multi-agent systems, where the connection weights
(w_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{N}
can be naturally interpreted as a
weighted, directed graph
.

A canonical example of non-exchangeable systems is the following:

Consider a domain
\mathbb{D}
, which is either
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
\mathbb{T}^{d}
(the
d
-dimensional torus). Let
N\geq 1
denote the number of agents and
X_{i}(t)\in\mathbb{D}
denote the state of the
i
-th agent. Let
\mu:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the velocity field representing the individual dynamics, and let
\sigma:\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the interaction kernel describing the binary interactions between agents. Furthermore, let
(w_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{N}
be a matrix that quantifies the intensity of the interaction exerted by the
j
-th agent on the
i
-th agent, scaled by a factor of
1/N
. This setup leads to the following system of ordinary differential equations (ODEs):

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}X_{i}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\mu(X_{i})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}w_{i,j}\sigma(X_{i},X_{j}),\quad\forall i\in[N].

Here, for any natural number
N
, we use the notation
[N]=\{1,\dots,N\}
. This notation will be used consistently throughout the article.

The framework can be naturally extended to stochastic multi-agent systems by incorporating additive noise. Specifically, we consider the following system of stochastic differential equations (SDEs):

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{i}=\mu(X_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}w_{i,j}\sigma(X_{i},X_{j})\,\mathrm{d}t+\nu\,\mathrm{d}B_{i},\quad\forall i\in[N].

In this system,
B_{i}(t)
for
i\in[N]
are independent Brownian motions, and the constant
\nu\geq 0
quantifies the intensity of the noise. When
\nu=0
, (
1.1
) reduces to the ODE system.

1.1.
A first result: coupling of mean-field limit and graph limit

We begin by introducing the foundational definitions and presenting our first theorem, which demonstrates the interplay between mean-field theory and graph limit theory concepts in the analysis of multi-agent systems.

Definition 1.1

(
Bi-coupling distances, discrete
)
.

A
(N_{1},N_{2})
-coupling is defined as a matrix
\gamma=(\gamma_{i_{1},i_{2}})_{i_{1}\in[N_{1}],i_{2}\in[N_{2}]}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{1}\times N_{2}}
such that all entries satisfy
\gamma_{i_{1},i_{2}}\geq 0
and

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}\in[N_{1}]}\gamma_{i_{1},i_{2}}=\frac{1}{N_{2}},\quad\forall i_{2}\in[N_{2}],

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{2}\in[N_{2}]}\gamma_{i_{1},i_{2}}=\frac{1}{N_{1}},\quad\forall i_{1}\in[N_{1}].

Let
\Pi(N_{1},N_{2})
denote the set of all
(N_{1},N_{2})
-couplings.

For
n=1,2
, consider

\displaystyle w^{(n)}=(w_{i,j}^{(n)})_{i,j\in[N_{n}]}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{n}\times N_{n}}\text{ and }X^{(n)}=(X_{i}^{(n)})_{i\in[N_{n}]}\in\mathbb{D}^{N_{n}}.

The bi-coupling distance from the
L^{2}\to L^{2}
operator norm and the Wasserstein-1 disntance for the pairs
(w^{(n)},X^{(n)})
is defined as

\displaystyle d_{L^{2}\to L^{2},W_{1}}\big[(w^{(1)},X^{(1)}),(w^{(2)},X^{(2)})\big]

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(N_{1},N_{2})}\bigg[\sum_{i_{1}\in[N_{1}]}\sum_{i_{2}\in[N_{2}]}|X^{(1)}_{i_{1}}-X^{(2)}_{i_{2}}|\,\gamma_{i_{1},i_{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{1}N_{2}}}\Big(\|N_{2}\cdot w^{(1)}\gamma-N_{1}\cdot\gamma w^{(2)}\|_{\ell^{2}\to\ell^{2}}+\|N_{2}\cdot\gamma^{T}w^{(1)}-N_{1}\cdot w^{(2)}\gamma^{T}\|_{\ell^{2}\to\ell^{2}}\Big)\bigg],

where
\gamma^{T}
denotes the transpose of
\gamma
, and the products such as
(w^{(1)}\gamma)\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{1}\times N_{2}}
should be understood as the standard matrix multiplication.

The minimization problem in the definition is remarkably convex.
When
w^{(1)},w^{(2)}
are all-one matrices, the second term vanishes and this bi-coupling distance degenerates to the Wasserstein distance in the Kantorovich formulation between empirical measures
\frac{1}{N_{1}}\sum_{i\in[N_{1}]}\delta_{X^{(1)}_{i}}
and
\frac{1}{N_{2}}\sum_{i\in[N_{2}]}\delta_{X^{(2)}_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D})
, which has been extensively used for decades in the development of mean-field theory
[
9
,
15
,
31
,
37
]
. For Sinkhorn’s matrix scaling algorithm of the approximate solutions, see
[
13
]
. On the other hand, when the entries of
X^{(1)},X^{(2)}
are all identical, the first term vanishes. The remaining part of this bi-coupling distance is minorly modified from the distance studied in
[
5
,
14
,
18
]
that identifies the fractional isomorphism of graphs, whose theoretical foundation traces back to earlier works such as
[
33
,
38
,
39
]
.
As noted in
[
5
]
, approximate solutions can be derived using interior-point methods in convex optimization; for a detailed treatment, see
[
30
]
.
In graph theory,
\gamma\in\Pi(N_{1},N_{2})
is also called a
fractional overlay
.

With these definitions in place, we now present our first theorem.

Theorem 1.2

(
Main result, discrete only
)
.

Let
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{T}
, and let
\mu\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
,
\sigma\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T})
,
\nu\geq 0
. Let
\{w^{(n)}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of connection weight matrices
w^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{n}\times N_{n}}
, and let
\{X^{(n)}_{0}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of initial data
X^{(n)}_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{N_{n}}
. Assume that
N_{n}\to\infty
,
\sup_{n}\sup_{i,j}|w^{(n)}_{i,j}|<\infty
, and that the initial data
X^{(n)}_{0}
are deterministic. Then, the following holds:

•

(Well-posedness). For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a unique solution
X^{(n)}(t)
of the ODE or SDE system (
1.1
) with connection weights
w^{(n)}
and initial data
X^{(n)}_{0}
.
(When
\nu>0
,
X^{(n)}(t)
are stochastic processes depending on the randomness of independent Brownian motions
(B^{(n)}_{i})_{i\in[N_{n}]}
.)

•

(Compactness). There exists a Cauchy subsequence of
(w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})
(which we still index by
n
) in the sense that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq n}d_{L^{2}\to L^{2},W_{1}}\big[(w^{(n_{1})},X^{(n_{1})}_{0}),(w^{(n_{2})},X^{(n_{2})}_{0})\big]=0.

•

(Stability). If the sequence
(w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})
is Cauchy, then at any later time
t>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq n}\mathbb{E}\Big(d_{L^{2}\to L^{2},W_{1}}\big[(w^{(n_{1})},X^{(n_{1})}(t)),(w^{(n_{2})},X^{(n_{2})}(t))\big]\Big)=0.

The full version of our main result is provided later as Theorem
1.7
. Informally, it states that “If
(w^{(n)}_{0},X^{(n)}_{0})
initially converges to a deterministic limit, denoted by
(w,f_{0})
, where
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I},\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{D}))
(with
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{T}
) for some atomless standard probability space
\mathbb{I}
, then there exists a unique distributional solution
f
to an extended Vlasov equation

(1.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(t,x,\xi)+\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(\left[\mu(x)+\int_{\mathbb{I}}w(\xi,\zeta)\int_{\mathbb{D}}\sigma(x,y)f(t,y,\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\zeta\right]f(t,x,\xi)\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\Delta_{x}f(t,x,\xi)=0,

with initial data
f_{0}
; and for any
t\geq 0
,
(w^{(n)},X^{(n)}(t))
converges in expectation to
(w,f(t))
.”
Rigorously defining such convergence to the limit requires significantly more definitions and notation. Thus, we pause here and postpone the statement of Theorem
1.7
until the end of the introduction.

1.2.
Summary of key innovations

We take this opportunity to summarize the key innovations of our work, some of which may involve specific background knowledge that will be provided in later sections. Our hope is that readers from different backgrounds can be inspired by at least part of these ideas before proceeding further on to their first reading.

•

(Bi-coupling).

Our bi-coupling distance takes an interpolative form, aligning two non-exchangeable systems
(w^{(1)},X^{(1)})
and
(w^{(2)},X^{(2)})
by a coupling measure
\gamma
.
This stands in sharp contrast to the existing literature (see, for example,
[
3
,
4
,
12
,
20
,
22
]
) establishing the mean-field limit of the form (
1.2
), which essentially relied on a “bijective” correspondence between agents
a priori
established according to
w^{(1)}
,
w^{(2)}
(see Section
1.5
), and studied the laws of
X^{(1)}
,
X^{(2)}
using the method of characteristics.
Our approach presents several advantages that are related:

–

(Convexity).

The minimization problem associated with the bi-coupling distance is
convex
. This is much more computationally feasible compared to existing results, which typically require performing highly non-convex optimizations to find the best correspondence between
w^{(1)}
,
w^{(2)}
.

–

(Weaker requirements).

The bi-coupling distance provides a much less restrictive condition for identifying similar large-scale behavior.
For example, two multi-agent systems with connection weight graphs, which are merely
fractionally isomorphic
but not isomorphic, are considered very different according to previous results. However, our results suggest that their dynamics can be recognized as identical when having suitable initial data.
For an in-depth discussion of the difference between isomorphism and fractional isomorphism of graphs, we refer the readers to
[
14
,
18
]
and references therein.

–

(Empirical data).

The bi-coupling distance characterizes the mean-field limit on the level of
empirical data
. More precisely, it gives conditions under which two systems
(w^{(1)},X^{(1)})
and
(w^{(2)},X^{(2)})
actually lead to similar dynamics in each realization of randomness.
This is stronger than the previous results on law convergence and, as discussed in Section
1.6
, seems incompatible with any a priori correspondence between the agents. However, it aligns naturally with the
a posteriori correspondence
viewpoint suggested by the interpolative form of the bi-coupling distance.

Remarkably, the asymptotic independence of agents
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}
, which is critical in the context of a priori correspondence, does not appear explicitly in our discussion.
Instead, a much weaker form of independence seems to be integrated into the main result.

•

(Connection to graph limit theory).

The topology induced by the bi-coupling distance is equivalent to the weak-* convergence of an infinite hierarchy of measures, referred to in this article as
observables
. Each observable is a weighted sum of empirical data
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}
, whose weights are carefully chosen according to the connection weights
(w_{i,j})_{i,j\in[N]}
.
Similar observables, defined from the marginal laws of
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}
, have been investigated in
[
19
,
21
]
.
Nevertheless, our bi-coupling distance is novel, and its underlying mathematical structure is noteworthy:

We reformulate the observables as a tensorized extension of
graph homomorphism densities
(see
[
28
]
), by considering a graph whose edge “weights”, depending on both
(w_{i,j})_{i,j\in[N]}
and
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}
, are defined on a certain Hilbert space.
With this interpretation, by properly extending the Counting Lemma and Inverse Counting Lemma
[
5
,
8
,
18
,
28
]
, we reveal a delicate correspondence between the hierarchy of observables and the low-dimensional data
(w_{i,j})_{i,j\in[N]}
and
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}
, which consequently leads to our proposal of the bi-coupling distance.

It should also be noted that our results cannot fully recover those of
[
19
,
21
]
, as they consider potentially sparser connection weights that are not discussed in this article.

We now provide a far-from-complete overview of many-particle and multi-agent systems, focusing as much as possible on the aspects relevant to the contributions of this article.

1.3.
Many-particle systems

The classical and relatively well-understood models are those assuming
w_{i,j}\equiv 1
for all
i\neq j
. Under this assumption, the ODE or SDE system (
1.1
) simplifies to

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{i}=\mu(X_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]\setminus\{i\}}\sigma(X_{i},X_{j})\,\mathrm{d}t+\nu\,\mathrm{d}B_{i},\quad\forall i\in[N].

In this system, we refer to the agents
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}
as “particles” due to their indistinguishable nature. Specifically, any permutation of the indices of
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}
results in a reordered sequence
(X_{\pi(i)})_{i\in[N]}
that also satisfies the ODE or SDE system (
1.3
).

For illustration, consider the deterministic case (
\nu=0
) and also assume
\sigma(x,x)=0
for all
x\in\mathbb{D}
(allowing us to take
w_{i,i}=1/N
without altering the system). With these assumptions, the binary interaction received by
X_{i}
in (
1.1
) is a naive average given by
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\sigma(X_{i},X_{j})
.

Suppose that we have two solutions
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
and
(Y_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
of (
1.1
), possibly with different initial conditions. We define the
coupling distance
between these two solutions as

\displaystyle D_{c}(t)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}|X_{i}(t)-Y_{i}(t)|.

Assuming that both
\mu:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
are bounded and Lipschitz continuous, we can apply Picard’s argument for ODEs (or SDEs when

(X_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
and
(Y_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
depend on the same
(B_{i})_{i\in[N]}
). The Grönwall’s inequality shows that the coupling distance satisfies:

\displaystyle D_{c}(t)\leq D_{c}(0)\,e^{Lt},

where
L
depends on the Lipschitz constant of
\mu
and
\sigma
. This indicates that the difference between the two solutions, in terms of coupling distance, remains controlled over time, provided it starts sufficiently small.

Note that index permutations can be applied to
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
solving (
1.3
), hence the coupling between two solutions
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
and
(Y_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
is not necessarily unique.
Essentially, one can consider the
Wasserstein-1 distance
between the
empirical measures
associated with the particle distributions.
The empirical measure
\rho_{N}
is defined as

\displaystyle\rho_{N}(t,x)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\delta_{X_{i}(t)}(x),

where
\delta_{X_{i}(t)}
is the Dirac delta measure at
X_{i}(t)
.
The Wasserstein-1 distance between two probability distributions
f,g\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D})
can be defined through the following Kantorovich definition:

\displaystyle W_{1}(f,g)\vcentcolon=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(f,g)}\int_{\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D}}|x-y|\,\mathrm{d}\gamma(x,y)=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(f,g)}\mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim\gamma}|X-Y|,

where
\Pi(f,g)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{D})
denotes the set of all couplings (joint probability measures) with marginals
f
and
g
.
In the deterministic case, it is straightforward to check that the empirical measure
\rho_{N}
are distributional solutions of the following Vlasov equation (with
\nu=0
)

(1.4)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(t,x)+\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(\left[\mu(x)+\int_{\mathbb{D}}\sigma(x,y)f(t,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\right]f(t,x)\right)-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\Delta_{x}f(t,x)=0.

One can extend the argument for
D_{c}
to any
\mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim\gamma}|X-Y|
. This essentially results the well-posedness and stability of (
1.4
), that for any initial data
f_{0},g_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(D)
, the unique distributional solutions
f,g\in C([0,T];\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}))
of the Vlasov equation (
1.4
) has Wasserstein-1 distance bounded by

\displaystyle W_{1}(f(t),g(t))\leq W_{1}(f_{0},g_{0})\,e^{Lt}.

When stochasticity is incorporated into the system (
\nu>0
), the empirical measure
\rho_{N}
becomes random due to the presence of stochastic terms
\nu\,\mathrm{d}B_{i}
in particle dynamics. Consequently, these empirical measures do not exactly satisfy the deterministic Vlasov equation (
1.4
). However, as the number of particles
N\to\infty
, one can still expect some form of convergence of the empirical measures to a deterministic limit governed by the Vlasov equation.
We refer to
[
37
]
for the derivation of such convergence when the coefficients are Lipschitz bounded.

A central concept in this analysis is
propagation of chaos
. It asserts that as
N\to\infty
, the particles become asymptotically
independent and identically distributed
(i.i.d.). In other words, the joint distribution of any finite subset of particles converges to a product of identical single-particle distributions. As a consequence, at any later time
t
, the empirical measure
\rho_{N}(t)
converges in expectation with respect to the Wasserstein-1 distance to the deterministic measure that solves the Vlasov equation.

The mean-field limit of many-particle systems with identical connections is an active area of research in its own right, with much still to be understood. When the interaction kernel
\sigma(x,y)
is singular, rigorously establishing the mean-field limit becomes increasingly difficult. Even for models where the limit has been justified, quantifying the convergence rate as
N\to\infty
remains a significant problem.
For a more comprehensive review, we refer the reader to survey articles
[
11
,
10
]
.

1.4.
Non-exchangeable systems

Much less explored are the
non-exchangeable systems, where the connection weights
w_{i,j}
in ODE or SDE systems such as (
1.1
) can all differ. However, there has been a growing interest in such systems over the past decade due to their critical importance in many applications. Traditional definitions of the mean-field limit are often insufficient in this context, as the non-identical connection weights fundamentally alter the structure of the problem.
Conceptually speaking, agents play distinct roles within the system, resulting in diverse interactions and dynamic behaviors. As a result, any attempt to couple agents or average their behavior must account for their inherent differences.

One way to generalize the concept of the mean-field limit, as discussed in
[
12
,
22
]
(and many more recent works, which we will introduce gradually), is by using the theory of graphons
[
29
]
and its extensions, which serve as natural tools to describe the graph limit of the connection weights. In this approach, it is expected that as the number of agents
N\to\infty
, the large-scale behavior of the ODE or SDE system (
1.1
) can be effectively captured by the extended Vlasov equation (
1.2
), restated here:

(1.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(t,x,\xi)+\nabla_{x}\cdot\left(\left[\mu(x)+\int_{\mathbb{I}}w(\xi,\zeta)\int_{\mathbb{D}}\sigma(x,y)f(t,y,\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\zeta\right]f(t,x,\xi)\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\Delta_{x}f(t,x,\xi)=0.

This is an equation about
f(t,x,\xi)
, which we call the extended density function. Here,
x\in\mathbb{D}
represents the state of agents, and
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
is an additional variable introduced to account for the indices
i=1,\dots,N
as the number of agents
N\to\infty
.
Specifically,
\xi
serves as a continuous identifier that distinguishes between different particles in the continuum limit.
For convenience, we mainly consider
\mathbb{I}=[0,1]
equipped with the Lebesgue measure, but other atomless standard probability spaces will be used when necessary. The flexibility in selecting
\mathbb{I}
is due to the fact that the definition relies solely on an integral kernel
w(\xi,\zeta):\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}
, which corresponds to the discrete
w_{i,j}
-weights in the finite system.

One way to illustrate how the extended Vlasov equation provides the right approximation of the multi-agent system is to again consider the deterministic case,
\nu=0
. Let
\mathbb{I}=[0,1]
, and define the extended empirical measure
\rho_{N}
as

\displaystyle\rho_{N}(t,x,\xi)\vcentcolon=\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbbm{1}_{\left[\frac{i-1}{N},\frac{i}{N}\right)}(\xi)\,\delta_{X_{i}(t)}(x).

Next, define the piecewise-extended kernel
w_{N}:[0,1]\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle w_{N}(\xi,\zeta)\vcentcolon=\sum_{i,j\in[N]}\mathbbm{1}_{\left[\frac{i-1}{N},\frac{i}{N}\right)\times\left[\frac{j-1}{N},\frac{j}{N}\right)}(\xi,\zeta)\,w_{i,j}.

It is straightforward to verify that the pair
(w_{i,j})_{i,j\in[N]},(X_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
solving the ODE system (
1.1
) with
\nu=0
is equivalent, in the distributional sense, to having
f(t,x,\xi)=\rho_{N}(t,x,\xi)
and
w(\xi,\zeta)=w_{N}(\xi,\zeta)
solve the Vlasov PDE (
1.5
) with
\nu=0
.

For the case
\nu>0
, one can adapt the classical arguments from
[
37
]
to establish asymptotic independence. This concept is weaker than chaos in exchangeable systems, as the agents’ distributions are not necessarily identical when the equations are not fully symmetric.
We outline this approach in the following and refer to
[
19
]
for further details.

Consider the following McKean-Vlasov SDE system:

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bar{X}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mu(\bar{X}_{i})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}w_{i,j}\sigma(\bar{X}_{i},\bar{X}_{j})\right)\,\mathrm{d}t+\nu\,\mathrm{d}B_{i},\quad\forall i\in[N],

where the binary interaction is defined in terms of the expectation. It is straightforward to verify that if the initial data
(\bar{X}_{i}(0))_{i\in[N]}
are independent, then the states
(\bar{X}_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
remain independent for any later time
t>0
.

When the coefficients
\mu,\sigma
are reasonably smooth,
coupling methods can be used to show that the solution
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
of (
1.1
) is approximated by the solution
(\bar{X}_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
of (
1.6
) in the following sense: Starting with the same
(w_{i,j})_{i,j\in[N]}
and same independent initial data, one can establish an a priori estimate that for any later time
t
, the averaged difference

(1.7)

\mathbb{E}\bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}|X_{i}(t)-\bar{X}_{i}(t)|\bigg)\to 0,\quad\text{ as }N\to\infty.

Define the extended law function
p_{N}(t):[0,1]\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D})
as

p_{N}(t,x,\xi)\vcentcolon=\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbbm{1}_{\left[\frac{i-1}{N},\frac{i}{N}\right)}(\xi)\mathrm{Law}_{\bar{X}_{i}}(x).

It is straightforward that the pair
(w_{i,j})_{i,j\in[N]}
and
(\bar{X}_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
solving (
1.6
) with
\nu\geq 0
is equivalent to having
f(t,x,\xi)=p_{N}(t,x,\xi)
and
w(\xi,\zeta)=w_{N}(\xi,\zeta)
solve the Vlasov PDE (
1.5
) in the distributional sense with
\nu\geq 0
.

However, for non-exchangeable systems, justifying the Vlasov PDE (
1.5
) is only halfway towards establishing the mean-field limit, as it merely considers a fixed connection matrix
(w_{i,j})_{i,j\in[N]}
and compares the difference between
(X_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
in (
1.1
) and
(\bar{X}_{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
in (
1.6
) (or
p_{N}(t,x,\xi)
solving (
1.5
)).
To derive the mean-field limit from a sequence of multi-agent systems satisfying (
1.1
), one must take the
n\to\infty
limit of both
(w_{i,j}^{(n)})_{i,j\in[N_{n}]}
and
(X_{i}^{(n)})_{i\in[N_{n}]}
.

If sufficient a priori knowledge of the connection weights is available, for example, when there exists a predefined kernel
w\in C(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
on a low-dimensional configuration space
\mathbb{I}
, and the discrete connection weights arise from discretizing this kernel (e.g., each agent
i
is associated with some
\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{I}
and
w_{i,j}=w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})
), then the convergence of
(w_{i,j}^{(n)})_{i,j\in[N_{n}]}
is natural to expect. In this scenario, one can effectively reduce the problem to the exchangeable setting by enlarging the phase space to
\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{I}
, where each agent’s state is
(x_{i},\xi_{i})
and there is no dynamics in the
\xi
-dimension.

A more challenging question arises when one only has access to
(w_{i,j}^{(n)})_{i,j\in[N_{n}]}
, with no clear low-dimensional embedding like
w_{i,j}=w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})
.
This is particularly relevant to many models where the existence of a low-dimensional structure is far from evident. To characterize the limit of connection weights without imposing overly strong a priori assumptions, graph limit theories have been widely borrowed in the study of non-exchangeable systems.

1.5.
Graphon theory

A notable example of graph limiting theories is the
graphon
theory, which was initially developed in the seminal work
[
29
]
for
L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2})
kernels. More recently, in
[
6
,
7
]
, it was extended to
L^{p}([0,1]^{2})
kernels, which applies to limits for connection weights that do not necessarily follow the
O(1/N)
scaling discussed in this article.
Here, we present a brief introduction to the graphon theory with the most classical
L^{\infty}
definition.

Definition 1.3
.

For any
w\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2})
, the cut norm is defined as

\displaystyle\|w\|_{\square}:=\sup_{S,T\subseteq[0,1]}\left|\int_{S\times T}w(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\,\mathrm{d}\zeta\right|.

Given a bound
W>0
, the set of graphons is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{W}:=\left\{w\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2}):0\leq w(\xi,\zeta)\leq W,\ \forall\xi,\zeta\in[0,1]\text{ and }w\text{ is symmetric}\right\}.

For any two graphons
w,u\in\mathcal{G}_{W}
, we define the labeled cut distance as

\displaystyle d_{\square}(w,u):=\|w-u\|_{\square},

and the (unlabeled) cut distance as

\displaystyle\delta_{\square}(w,u)=\inf_{\Phi}\|w-u^{\Phi}\|_{\square},

where
\Phi
ranges over all bijective measure-preserving maps
\Phi:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
, and
u^{\Phi}\in\mathcal{G}_{W}
denotes the rearranged graphon, defined by

\displaystyle u^{\Phi}(\xi,\zeta)\vcentcolon=u(\Phi(\xi),\Phi(\zeta)).

The labeled cut distance is equivalent to the
L^{\infty}\to L^{1}
operator norm of the adjacency operators. More precisely, one has

\displaystyle d_{\square}(w^{(n)},w)\leq\|T_{w^{(n)}}-T_{w}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}\leq 4d_{\square}(w^{(n)},w),

where

\displaystyle T_{w}(\psi)(\xi):=\int_{0}^{1}w(\xi,\zeta)\,\psi(\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}\zeta,\quad\xi\in[0,1].

From this, it is clear that

\displaystyle d_{\square}(w^{(n)},w)\leq\|w^{(n)}-w\|_{L^{1}},

and
d_{\square}
induces a strictly weaker topology than the
L^{1}
norm on
\mathcal{G}_{W}
the space of graphons.

The unlabeled cut distance
\delta_{\square}
is not a metric in
\mathcal{G}_{W}
, because a graphon
w
and its rearrangement
w^{\Phi}
have
\delta_{\square}(w,w^{\Phi})
= 0.
Instead, it is a metric in the quotient space
\mathcal{G}_{W}/\sim
, which contains equivalent classes of graphons up to measure-preserving rearrangements.
The space
(\mathcal{G}_{W}/\sim,\delta_{\square})
is remarkably a
compact
metric space, as shown in
[
29
]
(see also
[
6
,
7
]
for the
L^{p}
case).
In other words, for any sequence of graphons
w^{(n)}\in\mathcal{G}_{W}
, there exists a graphon limit
w\in\mathcal{G}_{W}
such that, after suitable rearrangements and passing to a subsequence, the unlabeled cut distance satisfies
d_{\square}(w^{(n)},w)\to 0
.

Let us emphasize that the compactness property does not hold directly on
(\mathcal{G}_{W},d_{\square})
when rearrangements are not allowed, nor can it easily be extended by replacing
d_{\square}(w^{(n)},w)
with stronger distances, such as
\|w^{(n)}-w\|_{L^{1}}
.
This is because we merely assume that the
1/N
-rescaled connection weights are uniformly bounded, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\sup_{i,j}|w_{i,j}^{(n)}|<\infty.

In simple terms, the rearrangement on
[0,1]
(or the index permutation on
[N]
) serves to group agents with “similar connection weight properties” together, which provides a weak yet crucial regularity of
w^{(n)}
and ensures compactness in terms of cut distance.

Such measure-preserving rearrangement
\Phi
in the definition of the unlabeled cut distance
\delta_{\square}
is particularly relevant in the study of non-exchangeable systems. It is straightforward to verify that if
(w,f)
is a solution to the Vlasov PDE (
1.5
) and
\Phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
is a measure-preserving bijection, then the rearranged pair

\displaystyle w^{\Phi}(\xi,\zeta)=w(\Phi(\xi),\Phi(\zeta)),\quad f^{\Phi}(t,x,\xi)=f(t,x,\Phi(\xi))

also forms a solution. This property corresponds to the permutation of indices in the ODE/SDE system (
1.1
), which does not fundamentally alter the underlying equation.
Consequently, by allowing measure-preserving rearrangements on
[0,1]
(and possibly passing to a subsequence), one can assume the a priori convergence of the kernels
d_{\square}(w^{(n)},w)\to 0
in the Vlasov PDE (
1.5
).
If
f^{(n)}\to f
can be established in the appropriate metric (given the convergence of
w^{(n)}
and the initial data), then
f
is justified as the solution to the Vlasov PDE (
1.5
) in the limit of the McKean-Vlasov system (
1.6
). Furthermore, if the propagation of independence (
1.7
) is given,
f
is also justified as the mean-field limit of the multi-agent system (
1.1
).

The first introduction of graphons to the mean-field theory of non-exchangeable systems of the form (
1.1
) was possibly made in
[
12
,
22
]
, where the mean-field limit was established through a quantitative stability estimate of
f^{(n)}\to f
in terms of

(1.8)

\displaystyle d_{F}\left(f^{(n)}(t,\cdot,\cdot),f(t,\cdot,\cdot)\right)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\bigg[\int_{0}^{1}\Big(d_{\mathcal{P}}\big(f^{(n)}(t,\cdot,\xi),f(t,\cdot,\xi)\big)\mathrm{d}\xi\Big)^{p}\bigg]^{1/p},

with
p=1
and the metric
d_{\mathcal{P}}
inside the integral chosen as the bounded Lipschtiz metric (which is a truncated Wasserstein-1) on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D})
, the space of probability measures.
The original results of
[
12
,
22
]
, however, considered
\|w^{(n)}-w\|_{L^{1}}\to 0
instead of
d_{\square}(w^{(n)},w)\to 0
, and thus still required an a priori limiting kernel
w
along with its ad hoc discretization.

The graphon approach has been further developed, for example, by
[
3
,
4
,
20
]
.
With
d_{\square}(w^{(n)},w)\to 0
, the stability of
f^{(n)}\to f
in terms of a metric of the form (
1.8
), with
p=2
and
d_{\mathcal{P}}
chosen as the Wasserstein-2 metric was achieved in both
[
3
,
4
]
.
Remarkable recent work
[
1
]
broadened the graphon framework by using hypergraphons (as seen in
[
16
,
34
]
) and defining mean-field limits where interactions extend beyond pairwise exchanges.

This strategy has also been applied to the graph limits defined in alternative ways. For example,
[
17
]
examined graph-ops, as introduced in
[
2
]
, from a more operator-theoretic perspective, while
[
24
]
developed and analyzed a specialized notion of digraph measures. Both works established the stability of
f^{(n)}\to f
by choosing
d_{\mathcal{P}}
in (
1.8
) as the bounded Lipschitz metric and formally taking
p=\infty
(which corresponds to the essential supremum taken over
\xi
).
While these alternative definitions provide valuable limiting objects for sparser connection weights, convergence
w^{(n)}\to w
can be more intricate, making the compactness property out of reach of current graph limit theories.

Let us also mention that when the connection weights are extremely sparse, for example, induced by graphs with bounded degrees, the averaging effect in binary interactions becomes insufficient and one cannot justify (
1.7
). In fact, since the agents are not asymptotically independent in generic, the dynamics is fundamentally different and should be described using frameworks other than extended Vlasov PDEs like (
1.5
). This topic cannot be adequately discussed in this article, and we refer instead to
[
25
,
26
,
32
]
and the references therein.

1.6.
Empirical data and a posteriori correspondence

The convergence behavior of empirical data has been much less discussed in the context of non-exchangeable systems.

In the
exchangeable
systems, the stability of the Vlasov equation, combined with the propagation of chaos, suggests that the empirical measure
\rho_{N}(t,x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\delta_{X_{i}(t)}(x)
converges to a deterministic limit
f(t,x)
as
N\to\infty
. For instance, for sufficiently smooth dynamics, one can expect the convergence of Wasserstein-1 distance in expectation, i.e.

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[W_{1}\left(\rho_{N}(t,\cdot),f(t,\cdot)\right)\right]=0.

However, in the
non-exchangeable
systems, there is a larger gap between the convergence of laws to the limit and the convergence of empirical data directly to the same limit. To see it, recall our definition of extended law

p_{N}(t,x,\xi)\vcentcolon=\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbbm{1}_{\left[\frac{i-1}{N},\frac{i}{N}\right)}(\xi)\mathrm{Law}_{\bar{X}_{i}}(x),

and extended empirical measure

\displaystyle\rho_{N}(t,x,\xi)\vcentcolon=\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbbm{1}_{\left[\frac{i-1}{N},\frac{i}{N}\right)}(\xi)\delta_{X_{i}(t)}(x).

For a fixed
t\geq 0
and
\xi\in[0,1]
,
\rho_{N}(t,\cdot,\xi)
is a Dirac delta measure centered at
X_{i}(t)
if
\xi
lies within the interval corresponding to the
i
-th particle.
However, when there is randomness in the initial data or in the dynamics, the limit of
p_{N}(t,x,\xi)
, that is,
f(t,x,\xi)
, should not concentrate on a Dirac delta for a fixed
t\geq 0
and
\xi\in[0,1]
.
This implies that when the metric takes the
\xi
-fibered form as in (
1.8
), the convergence of
\rho_{N}
to
f
is typically not allowed.

The issue is fundamental in non-exchangeable systems. Agents play distinct roles due to the connection weights
(w_{i,j}^{(n)})_{i,j\in[N_{n}]}
. To capture this, the aforementioned approach
a priori
establishes a fine correspondence between agents
i\in[N_{n}]
and pieces of density in the continuum limit
\xi\in[0,1]
, independent of time and any specific realization of randomness. However, this would result in insufficient averaging of agents when starting from empirical data.
Hence, even when the laws of the agents are independent, characterizing the convergence of empirical data to the extended Vlasov equation still requires additional effort. One possible approach is to adopt the definition given later in Definition
1.6
, where agents
i\in[N_{n}]
are not
a priori
associated with small portions of
[0,1]
equipped with the Lebesgue measure, but rather more precisely associated with parts of the distribution
f(t,\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}\xi)
.

One approach that considers an a priori correspondence and achieves a stability result directly from empirical data in an SDE system is given in
[
20
]
.
The proof relies on a metric tailored to the limiting
w\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2})
. This metric induces the weak-* topology on
\mathcal{M}([0,1]\times\mathbb{R})
and allows for a form of local averaging among nearby agents, resulting in convergence
\rho_{N}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}f
and mean trajectories of agents like in the exchangeable case.

In this article, our bi-coupling distance as in Definition
1.1
takes an interpolative form, allowing the coupling measure
\gamma
to adjust with time and specific realizations of randomness. In this sense, we refer to it as
a posteriori
correspondence.

The benefit of a posteriori correspondence is evident. Consider a toy example where the connection weights are all identically
1/N
, yet we pretend not to know this and consider the systems as non-exchangeable. We further simplify (
1.1
) by setting
\mu=\nu=0
, so that
\mathrm{d}X_{i}=\nu B_{i}
on the torus
\mathbb{T}
. Suppose
N_{n}\to\infty
and
\rho_{n}(0,x)=\frac{1}{N_{n}}\sum_{i\in[N_{n}]}\delta_{X^{(n)}_{i}(0)}(x)
converging to
f_{0}
in Wasserstein-2. Then, as time evolves, the dynamics should asymptotically follow
\partial_{t}f=\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\Delta f
. This implies that at any later time
t
, we still have the Wasserstein-2 convergence, and since the topologies are equivalent, we also have the Wasserstein-1 convergence. In other words, we can write

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq n}\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(N_{n_{1}},N_{n_{2}})}\left[\sum_{i_{1}\in[N_{n_{1}}]}\sum_{i_{2}\in[N_{n_{2}}]}|X^{(n_{1})}_{i_{1}}(t)-X^{(n_{2})}_{i_{2}}(t)|^{p}\gamma_{i_{1},i_{2}}\right]=0,

which is a degenerate form of our bi-coupling distance (when
p=1
).
However, if we try to fix a particular
\gamma\in\Pi(N_{n_{1}},N_{n_{2}})
a priori and remove the infimum in the above expression, then at any later time
t
, we cannot achieve convergence for either
p=1
or
p=2
.

From the above example, it becomes clear that even the classical mean-field limit for exchangeable systems should be understood as relying on a posteriori correspondence. Our discussion simply makes this perspective explicit.

However, for non-exchangeable systems, adopting a posteriori correspondence becomes much more challenging. With an a priori correspondence, one can first analyze the McKean–Vlasov system (
1.6
) and then establish a loose form of the mean-field limit based on the propagation of independence (
1.7
). The a posteriori correspondence viewpoint does not seem to allow for such a decomposition easily. Specifically,
\mathrm{Law}_{\bar{X}_{i}}
(the law of a particular agent indexed by
i
) becomes very unnatural, and the weights and states of agents become more intricately entangled, resulting in the method of characteristics seemingly inaccessible.

1.7.
Tensorization of agent law and graph homomorphism density

A recent practice, as seen in
[
19
,
21
,
27
]
, aims to extend the classical mean-field theory tool of the BBGKY hierarchy to the non-exchangeable case.
The article
[
27
]
provides a detailed quantification of the propagation of independence in relative entropy for non-exchangeable systems. The two related works
[
19
,
21
]
study the large-scale behavior by defining certain weighted
k
-marginals, which do not rely on any correspondence between different systems at the microscopic level.
We describe the arguments from
[
19
,
21
]
in some detail, as our strategy benefits from these works on multiple levels.

Let us begin with the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
). By integrating it over the variable
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
(thereby not explicitly distinguishing individual agents), one has:

(1.9)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\tau(1,w,f)(t,x_{1})+\nabla_{x}\cdot\Big(\mu(x_{1})\tau(1,w,f)(t,x_{1})\Big)

\displaystyle+\nabla_{x}\cdot\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{D}}\sigma(x_{1},x_{2})\tau(2,w,f)(t,x_{1},x_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\bigg)-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\tau(1,w,f)(t,x_{1})=0,

where the distribution functions
\tau(1,w,f)(t,x)
and
\tau(2,w,f)(t,x_{1},x_{2})
are defined as

\displaystyle\tau(1,w,f)(t,x)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{I}}f(t,x,\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\tau(2,w,f)(t,x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{I}}\int_{\mathbb{I}}w(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})f(t,x_{1},\xi_{1})f(t,x_{2},\xi_{2})\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}.

This is a linear PDE that describes the time evolution of
\tau(1,w,f)
, which represents the average behavior of all agents. In the exchangeable case, the evolution of
f(t,x)
can be expressed as a linear equation involving both
f(t,x)
and its tensorization
f(t,x_{1})f(t,x_{2})
. In the non-exchangeable setting, this relationship extends to
\tau(1,w,f)
depending on
\tau(2,w,f)
, defined as a weighted integral of the tensorized form
f(t,x_{1},\xi_{1})f(t,x_{2},\xi_{2})
. The connection weights
w
are incorporated into
\tau(2,w,f)
in such a way that
w
no longer explicitly appear in the resulting PDE (
1.9
).

One can also derive a linear equation for
\tau(2,w,f)
, which involves weighted integrals of 3-tensorized forms, specifically
f(t,x_{1},\xi_{1})f(t,x_{2},\xi_{2})f(t,x_{3},\xi_{3})
. This recursive process gives rise to an extended family called
observables
, indexed by (directed) trees
T\in\mathcal{T}
and denoted by
\tau(T,w,f)
, which reads

\displaystyle\tau(T,w,f)\big(t,(x_{i})_{i\in\mathsf{v}(T)}\big)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(T)}}\prod_{(k,l)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}w(\xi_{k},\xi_{l})\prod_{m\in\mathsf{v}(T)}\Big(f(t,x_{m},\xi_{m})\mathrm{d}\xi_{m}\Big).

These observables, first identified in
[
19
]
, establish an infinite hierarchy of dependencies among the distribution functions:

\displaystyle\tau(T,w,f)\text{ depends on }\tau(T,w,f)\text{ and all }\tau(T+i,w,f),

where
(T+i)
is obtained from
T
by adding a new vertex and connecting it to the vertex
i\in\mathsf{v}(T)
by a new edge.

This hierarchical structure introduces a significant challenge, which appears in many kinetic theory problems. Quantitative analysis of one
\tau(T,w,f)
requires knowledge of the next-level observables. Although it is possible to establish energy estimates at each level, combining these estimates typically results in a priori bounds that resemble a Taylor expansion with large factorial coefficients. Such coefficients can cause a blow-up in some finite time
t>0
, a phenomenon commonly referred to as
factorial blow-up
.
However, in
[
19
]
, by establishing a priori estimates for the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
) and extending these estimates to all observables in the hierarchy,
L^{2}
-quantitative stability of the observables was achieved for a sufficiently smooth dynamics at all times
t\geq 0
. Without referring to the explicit convergence rate, the critical estimate in
[
19
]
can be restated as follows: If

\displaystyle\tau(T,w^{(n)},f^{(n)}_{0})\to\tau(T)^{(\infty)}_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{D}^{\mathsf{v}(T)}),

then at any later
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\tau(T,w^{(n)},f^{(n)}(t))\to\tau(T)^{(\infty)}(t)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{D}^{\mathsf{v}(T)}).

Notably, still with sufficiently smooth dynamics, the observables were proved to be
W^{1,\infty}
-regular and to have certain moment bounds (which are necessary when
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
). This additional regularity can ensure compactness in terms of the
L^{2}
norm of the observables
\tau(T,w,f)
and guarantee that the limit exists without additional assumptions on the weights
w^{(n)}
.

Investigating the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
) through the observables completely avoids any direct argument of the graph limit.
Thus, this approach can even be extended to sparse kernels
w
lying in the space
L^{\infty}_{\xi}\mathcal{M}_{\zeta}\cap L^{\infty}_{\zeta}\mathcal{M}_{\xi}
, where the interpretation of this notation differs from the conventional definitions of Bochner spaces. This allows connection weights to be sparsely distributed, which we do not discuss in this article. In fact, addressing the compactness and sparsity of the connection weights simultaneously was one of the primary motivations behind the development of the observables in
[
19
]
.

Extending this hierarchical convergence to work directly for multi-agent systems like (
1.1
) was addressed by
[
21
]
, although the rigorous result was merely done on
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{R}
about a different SDE system (integrate-and-fire neuron model).
This approach corresponds to the classical argument that the BBGKY hierarchy converges to the Vlasov hierarchy in the exchangeable case, where the propagation of chaos is not proved separately but is instead integrated into the derivation of the mean-field limit.
The observable
\tau_{N}(T,w,X)
for the multi-agent systems is defined on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{D}^{\mathsf{v}(T)})
for fixed time
t
, as

\displaystyle\tau_{N}(T,w,X)(t,\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(T)|}}\sum_{\forall j\in\mathsf{v}(T),\ i_{j}\in[N]\text{ distinct}}\bigg(\prod_{(l,m)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}w_{i_{l},i_{m}}\bigg)\mathrm{Law}_{(X^{i_{j}}(t))_{j\in\mathsf{v}(T)}}(\mathrm{d}z),

which is a weighted sum of marginal laws with the same number of agents.
From the Liouville equation governing the full joint law of
(X^{i}(t))_{i\in[N]}
, a similar hierarchical structure was derived.
Quantitative estimates on the hierarchy were established using a strategy similar to that of
[
19
]
, but within a tactically chosen negative Sobolev space, specifically a tensorization of
H^{-1}(\mathbb{R})
.
Compared to
L^{2}
, this space is particularly helpful for systems with a finite number of agents, as it includes singular distributions such as the Dirac delta.

Readers who are familiar with graph theory may have noticed that if we take the integration over the entire domain
\mathbb{D}^{\mathsf{v}(T)}
, the observables
\tau(T,w,f)
and
\tau_{N}(T,w,X)
simply give the homomorphism densities for (directed) graphons and graphs. In this sense, the observables are joint densities of graph homomorphism densities tensorized with agent distributions that are independently multiplied
[
19
]
or as marginals of correlated distributions
[
21
]
. This formulation effectively yields a large-scale description of the multi-agent system.

The strategy of constructing observables is highly extensive. For multi-agent systems where there are two types of binary interactions between agents with distinct connection weights
w
and
u
, observables can be defined by assigning two different colors to the tree edges.
Another important extension involves indexing observables by tree graphs that incorporate both “opaque edges” and “transparent edges”. In the definition of such observables, opaque edges correspond to factors
w
, while transparent edges correspond to factors
1
.
The stability argument can be readily applied within this context, as building the hierarchy merely requires the addition of “opaque edges”.
From a graph homomorphism perspective, this definition may seem redundant at first glance because, if a tree includes a “transparent edge”, the homomorphism density can be factored into two independent components. For example, two vertices connected by a transparent edge yield

\displaystyle\tau(\dots)(t,x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}}\mathbbm{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})f(t,x_{1},\xi_{1})f(t,x_{2},\xi_{2})\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

which can be integrated separately to obtain

\displaystyle\tau(\dots)(t,x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{I}}f(t,x_{1},\xi_{1})\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\ \int_{\mathbb{I}}f(t,x_{2},\xi_{2})\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}.

However, for a finite system, this
\tau_{N}
corresponds to the
2
-marginal law of the agents. The convergence of observables essentially implies that the
2
-marginal law tensorizes, that is, it becomes the product of the
1
-marginal laws.
By standard tools from kinetic theory, this extends to that all
k
-marginal laws asymptotically factorize as products of the
1
-marginal laws, which is an alternative formulation of chaos. Consequently, the empirical measure converges to the deterministic
1
-marginal law in expectation. This argument can be generalized to any observable by connecting the corresponding tree to its copy via a “transparent edge”. The convergence essentially implies that the “empirical observables”

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(T)|}}\sum_{\forall j\in\mathsf{v}(T),\ i_{j}\in[N]\text{ distinct}}\bigg(\prod_{(l,m)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}w_{i_{l},i_{m}}\bigg)\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}(T)}\delta_{X^{i_{j}}(t)}(\mathrm{d}z),

which are random distributions derived from individual realizations of the SDE, converge in expectation to
\tau(T,w,f)
.
In this sense,
[
21
]
provided another result, apart from
[
20
]
, on direct convergence from empirical data of the SDE system to a deterministic limit.

1.8.
Counting Lemma and Inverse Counting Lemma

A natural question is whether it is possible to establish a more straightforward metric on the pair
(w,f)
or
(w,X)
that is equivalent to the convergence of a certain family of observables. (As we have illustrated, these interesting families are far from unique.)

The “projection” of this problem to graph theory is relatively well studied.
The celebrated Counting Lemma and Inverse Counting Lemma
[
8
]
establish that convergence in the (unlabeled) cut distance
\delta_{\square}
is equivalent to the convergence of graph homomorphisms for all simple graphs. More recently, it was shown by
[
14
,
18
]
that two graphs (or graphons) having identical graph homomorphisms for all trees is equivalent to the
fractional isomorphism
of the two. In
[
5
]
, metrics were introduced that relax the strict conditions of fractional isomorphism, showing that the convergence of these metrics implies the convergence of graph homomorphisms for all tree graphs.

Our main result is closely related to this problem.
In particular, we prove in the article that the convergence in the bi-coupling distance, as defined in Definition
1.1
and in the following Definition
1.5
), is equivalent to the convergence of observables in a larger family than what has been illustrated here.
Our proof of this equivalence adopts techniques developed from all the aforementioned results about graph limits.
The stability in the bi-coupling distance, as stated in Theorem
1.2
and in the following Theorem
1.7
, actually reflect the stability of these observables, which is proved following the approach in
[
21
]
.

1.9.
Some conventions

Last, before we state the main result. Let us explain a bit about the conventions adopted in this article.

•

In our discussion, we frequently work with vectors in Hilbert spaces. To emphasize this technical key point, we denote
vector-valued quantities in Hilbert spaces using bold fonts
. This convention distinguishes these vectors from other types of variables within our framework.

Consequently, random variables will not be represented in bold fonts. Let us remark here that the only variables that are potentially random in our model are
X
, representing the states, and
B
, representing the Brownian motion. We will remind the readers when these variables are discussed in a random context to avoid any confusion.

•

We also use standard probability spaces (which are measure spaces with total measure
1
) in our definition of the mean-field limit. However, these probability spaces do not represent actual randomness in the model. To emphasize this distinction, we use the notation
\mathbb{I}
instead of the more commonly used
\Omega
for such probability spaces.

•

Throughout the paper, we perform computations involving kernels on
\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}
, which correspond to compositions of adjacency operators. Specifically, consider the kernels
w:\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\gamma:\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. The composition of adjacency operators satisfies
T_{w}T_{\gamma}=T_{w\gamma}
,
where the composed kernel
w\gamma
is defined by

\displaystyle(w\gamma)(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}w(\xi_{1},\zeta)\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\zeta.

In some instances,
\gamma
may be just a measure on
\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}
, and to rigorously define the composition in the distributional sense, we need to write

\displaystyle(w\gamma)(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}w(\xi_{1},\zeta)\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\zeta,\xi_{2}).

However, inserting
\mathrm{d}\zeta
into the middle of the expression can be visually cumbersome for readers. To maintain clarity and ease of notation, when there are integrals on measures or equations that should be understood in the distributional sense, we adopt the slight abuse of notation like
\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\zeta
.

•

Many of our estimates rely on the assumption of uniform bounds. To maintain concise notation, we adopt the following convention: for any Banach space
\mathcal{B}
, we denote by
\mathcal{B}_{\leq 1}
the subset of
\mathcal{B}
consisting of elements with norm bounded by
1
. As will be evident in the proofs, the bound of
1
is chosen for convenience most of the time and can be replaced by any positive constant.

•

Finally, our stability estimates rely on multiple metrics that give equivalent notions of convergence, where compactness arguments yield uniform bounds from one metric to the other. We denote these bounds using
C^{\downarrow}_{a}
, where
a
represents some additional parameter. For any fixed
a
,
C^{\downarrow}_{a}
represents a non-decreasing, continuous function
\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{r\downarrow 0}C^{\downarrow}_{a}(r)=0.

We express the equivalence of convergence given by two metrics
d_{1},d_{2}
as

\displaystyle d_{1}\leq C^{\downarrow}_{a}(d_{2}),\quad d_{2}\leq C^{\downarrow}_{a}(d_{1}).

This notation says that the metric
d_{1},d_{2}
can be controlled by the other metric through a certain function
C^{\downarrow}_{a}
.

1.10.
Main result

Let us begin by rephrasing Definition
1.1
with greater generality.

Definition 1.4

(
Coupling
)
.

Let
(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathscr{B}_{1},P_{1})
and
(\mathbb{I}_{2},\mathscr{B}_{2},P_{2})
be two standard probability spaces.
A coupling of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
is a probability measure
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2})
on the product
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}_{1}\times\mathscr{B}_{2}
such that:

\displaystyle\text{for all measurable sets }A_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{I}_{1},\quad\gamma(A_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2})=P_{1}(A_{1}),

\displaystyle\text{for all measurable sets }A_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{I}_{2},\quad\gamma(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times A_{2})=P_{2}(A_{2}).

We also say that it is a coupling of
\mathbb{I}_{1}
and
\mathbb{I}_{2}
when there is no ambiguity of
P_{1},P_{2}
. We denote by
\Pi(P_{1},P_{2})
or
\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
the set of all couplings.

Note that when
\mathbb{I}_{1}=\{1,\dots,N_{1}\}
and
\mathbb{I}_{2}=\{1,\dots,N_{2}\}
are equipped with a uniform measure of
1/N_{1}
and
1/N_{2}
at each point, this definition coincides with the
\Pi(N_{1},N_{2})
coupling for discrete sets in Definition
1.1
.

Definition 1.5

(
Bi-coupling distances
)
.

For
n=1,2
, let
\mathbb{I}_{n}
be standard probability spaces,
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
and
X^{(n)}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{D}
. Define the
bi-coupling distance from an operator norm
L^{p}\to L^{q}
and Wasserstein-1 distance, as

\displaystyle d_{L^{p}\to L^{q},W_{1}}\big[(w^{(1)},X^{(1)}),(w^{(2)},X^{(2)})\big]

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})

\displaystyle+\|T_{w^{(1)}}\circ T_{\gamma}-T_{\gamma}\circ T_{w^{(2)}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle+\|T_{\gamma^{T}}\circ T_{w^{(1)}}-T_{w^{(2)}}\circ T_{\gamma^{T}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{1})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{2})}\bigg],

where
\gamma^{T}\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{2},\mathbb{I}_{1})
is defined as
\gamma^{T}(\xi,\zeta)=\gamma(\zeta,\xi)
, and
T_{w^{(1)}},T_{w^{(2)}},T_{\gamma},T_{\gamma^{T}}
are the adjacency operators.

The bi-coupling distance from the cut norm and the Wasserstein-1 distance, denoted by
d_{\square,W_{1}}
, is defined analogously, with the
L^{p}\to L^{q}
operator norm in the above formulation replaced by the cut norm
\|\cdot\|_{\square}
of the kernels whose adjacency operators are
(T_{w^{(1)}}\circ T_{\gamma}-T_{\gamma}\circ T_{w^{(2)}})
and
(T_{\gamma^{T}}\circ T_{w^{(1)}}-T_{w^{(2)}}\circ T_{\gamma^{T}})
.

Again, when
p=q=2
, this definition coincides with the discrete definition of
d_{L^{2}\to L^{2},W_{1}}
in Definition
1.1
.

The following definition addresses the solutions of extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
).
Intuitively speaking, what we obtained from (
1.2
) are distributions along the
\xi
-fibers, and we are splitting them back to Dirac deltas.
This definition seems novel and is the conceptual key step that allows us to go beyond a priori correspondence and fiberwise differences in
\xi
, as discussed in Section
1.6
.

Definition 1.6

(
Random variable lifted from law
)
.

Let
\mathbb{I}
be a standard probability space,
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
, and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{D}))
such that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
, one has
f(\mathrm{d}x,\xi)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D})
.
Define probability space
\mathbb{D}\rtimes_{f}\mathbb{I}
as the product space
\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{I}
equipped with
f\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{I})
as its probablity measure, i.e.

\displaystyle\forall\text{ measurable sets }A\subseteq\mathbb{D},B\subseteq\mathbb{I},\quad f(A\times B)=\int_{A\times B}f(x,\xi)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\xi.

Define the
f
-lift of the pair

\displaystyle w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}),\quad f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{D})),

as the pair

\displaystyle w_{f}\in L^{\infty}((\mathbb{D}\rtimes_{f}\mathbb{I})\times(\mathbb{D}\rtimes_{f}\mathbb{I})),\quad X_{f}:\mathbb{D}\rtimes_{f}\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{D},

where

\displaystyle w_{f}(\cdot,\cdot)=w(p_{\mathbb{I}}(\cdot),p_{\mathbb{I}}(\cdot)),\quad X_{f}(\cdot)=p_{\mathbb{D}}(\cdot),

and the mappings
p_{\mathbb{I}}:\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{I}
,
p_{\mathbb{D}}:\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{D}
are the canonical projections for product sets.

To simplify notation, when the domain and function are clear from context, we omit the subscript and refer simply to “
(w,X)
being the lift of
(w,f)
”.

We are now ready to state the full version of Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 1.7

(
Main result
)
.

Let
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{T}
, and let
\mu\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
,
\sigma\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T})
,
\nu\geq 0
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
N_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
and
X^{(n)}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
be either of the following:

–

For some
N_{n}\geq 1
,
\mathbb{I}_{n}=\{1,\dots,N_{n}\}
equipped with uniform atomic measure
1/N_{n}
on each point. The pair
w^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{n}\times N_{n}}
,
X^{(n)}_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{N_{n}}
is connection weight matrices and initial data for the ODE/SDE system (
1.1
).

–

For formally
N_{n}=\infty
,
\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}
is an atomless probability space. The pair
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime})
,
f^{(n)}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}
,

f^{(n)}_{0}(\cdot,\xi)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
, is the weight kernel and initial data for the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
).

Let
\mathbb{I}_{n}=\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}\ltimes_{f^{(n)}_{0}}\mathbb{T}
,
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
and
X^{(n)}_{0}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
be the lift.

Assume that
N_{n}\to\infty
,
\|w^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq w_{\max}<\infty
, and the initial data
X^{(n)}_{0}
are deterministic.
Then the following holds:

•

(Compactness). There exists a subsequence of
(w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})
(which we still index by
n
), an atomless standard probability space
\mathbb{I}_{\infty}
, and a pair
w^{(\infty)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{\infty}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{I}_{\infty}^{\prime})
,
f^{(\infty)}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{\infty}^{\prime};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}_{\infty}^{\prime}
,

f^{(\infty)}_{0}(\cdot,\xi)
concentrate as a Dirac delta. Let
\mathbb{I}_{\infty}=\mathbb{I}_{\infty}^{\prime}\ltimes_{f^{(\infty)}_{0}}\mathbb{T}
,
w^{(\infty)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{\infty}\times\mathbb{I}_{\infty})
and
X^{(\infty)}_{0}:\mathbb{I}_{\infty}\to\mathbb{T}
be the lift. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{L^{2}\to L^{2},W_{1}}\big[(w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0}),(w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}_{0})\big]=0.

•

(Well-posedness). For each
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, there exists either

–

an unique solution
X^{(n)}(t)
of ODE or SDE system (
1.1
) with connection weights
w^{(n)}
and initial data
X^{(n)}_{0}
, for all
t\geq 0
.
(When
\nu>0
,
X^{(n)}(t)
are stochastic processes only depend on the randomness of independent Brownian motions
(B^{n}_{i})_{i\in[N_{n}]}
.)

–

an unique solution
f^{(n)}(t)
of the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
) with weight kernel
w^{(n)}
and initial data
f^{(n)}_{0}
, for all
t\geq 0
,

f^{(n)}(t)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}
,

f(t,\cdot,\xi)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
.

Let
\mathbb{I}_{n}(t)=\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}\ltimes_{f^{(n)}(t)}\mathbb{T}
,
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}(t)\times\mathbb{I}_{n}(t))
and
X^{(n)}(t):\mathbb{I}_{n}(t)\to\mathbb{T}
be the lift. (Note that the definition of
\mathbb{I}_{n}(t)
now depends on time
t
).

•

(Stability).
If the initial data
(w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})
is converging to
(w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}_{0})
in
d_{L^{2}\to L^{2},W_{1}}
, then at any later time
t>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[d_{L^{2}\to L^{2},W_{1}}\big[(w^{(n)},X^{(n)}(t)),(w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}(t))\big]\bigg]=0.

1.11.
Organization of the article

The remainder of this article is organized as follows.

In Sections
2.1
-
2.5
, we introduce all the concepts and definitions necessary for our analysis. Assuming several technical results, we provide a proof of Theorem
1.7
at the end of Section
2.5
. We make a conclusion for this article and discuss further perspectives in Section
2.6
.

Section
3
contains the proofs of most of these technical results, with exceptions for Lemma
2.28
, Lemma
2.29
, Lemma
2.30
and Lemma
2.31
.
This is due to their length and because they are essentially straightforward extensions of results about mean-field theory in
[
19
,
21
]
, results about graphon theory in
[
8
,
29
]
, and results about graph fractional isomorphism in
[
5
,
14
,
18
]
.
For clarity, the proofs of these results are presented respectively in Appendix
A
,
B
and
C
. We make no claim of novelty for any of the proof techniques presented in these sections, which are written in detail solely to establish the necessary results for this article. Where possible, intermediate results are cited directly rather than derived in detail. We encourage readers to consult the original works and the monograph
[
28
]
about graph limits for further motivation and insights.

2.
The whole picture and the proof of Theorem
1.7

2.1.
Uniform metric bound from compactness argument

As explained in the introduction, the proof relies heavily on compactness arguments. Below, we outline the basic lemmas that are used throughout the article. The proofs are postponed to Section
3.1
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
E
be a compact topological space and let
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
be two metrics on
E
that induce this topology. Then, there exists a non-decreasing function
C^{\downarrow}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}C^{\downarrow}(r)=0
such that for all
x,y\in E
,

\displaystyle d_{1}(x,y)\leq C^{\downarrow}(d_{2}(x,y))\quad\text{and}\quad d_{2}(x,y)\leq C^{\downarrow}(d_{1}(x,y)).

The following lemma serves as a complement to Lemma
2.1
, providing a bound to the expectation of distance in the case of random variables.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
be non-negative random variables. Suppose there exists a constant
A>0
and a non-decreasing function
C^{\downarrow}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}C^{\downarrow}(r)=0
such that, almost surely,

\displaystyle D_{1}\leq C^{\downarrow}(D_{2})\quad\text{and}\quad D_{1}\leq A.

Then, there exists a non-decreasing function
C^{\downarrow}_{A}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfying
\lim_{r\downarrow 0}C^{\downarrow}_{A}(r)=0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[D_{1}]\leq C^{\downarrow}_{A}\left(\mathbb{E}[D_{2}]\right).

With these lemmas in hand, and given that we work within compact spaces throughout the article, we will not emphasize the distinction between topological equivalence and uniform bounds across metrics in later statements, and we will use topological equivalence to simplify the statements.

2.2.
Functional-valued graphons

Now we introduce some concepts in graph limit theory generalized to higher dimensions.
The proofs are postponed to Section
3.2
.

We begin with the definition of norms for kernels with values in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, which are extended from the operator norm and cut norm.
When
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}
, this will serve as our definition of the cut norm
\|\cdot\|_{\square}
for kenrels on general
\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}
.

Definition 2.3

(
Norms for Hilbert-valued kernels
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space, and let
\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2}
be standard probability spaces.
For
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
and any
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
, define

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}\vcentcolon=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1},\ f\in L^{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{I}_{2}),\ g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}\bigg\langle\bm{e},\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}f(\xi)\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)g(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

where
q^{\prime}=q/(q-1)
is the Hölder conjugate on
[1,+\infty]
.
In addition, define

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\vcentcolon=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1},\ S\subset\mathbb{I}_{1},\ T\subset\mathbb{I}_{2}}\bigg\langle\bm{e},\int_{S\times T}\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

where
S\subset\mathbb{I}_{1},T\subset\mathbb{I}_{2}
are any measurable subsets.

Note that when
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}
, the norm
\|w\|_{p,q;\mathbb{R}}
corresponds to the
L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})
operator norm of the adjacency operator
T_{w}
. More generally, our definition is equivalent to

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{\square},

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}.

However, when
\mathcal{H}
is infinite-dimensional,
\|\bm{w}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}
represents a weaker notion than the
L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})
norm of
T_{\bm{w}}
.

The following lemma is a direct consequence of interpolation theory for function spaces.

Lemma 2.4

(
Norm interpolations
)
.

For any
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}\leq 4\,\|\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}.

For any
1<p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}^{1-\theta}\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})}^{\theta}.

with
\theta=\max\left\{\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{q}\right\}
.

From the norm introduced here, we define three ways to measure the distance between kernels. Firstly, the “labeled distances” are simply to take the norm of the difference, which of course requires the two kernels to lie in the same space
\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space, and let
\mathbb{I}
be standard probability spaces.
For
\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathcal{H})
, define labeled distances

\displaystyle d_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}},

\displaystyle d_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}},

for all
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
.

Next, we introduce “unlabeled distances” in analogy to the unlabeled cut distance in graph theory. They can be understood as pseudometrics on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathcal{H})
.
However, for the sake of convenience in our arguments, we extend the definition to kernels where the underlying spaces,
\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1}
and
\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}
, differ.

Definition 2.6

(
Unlabeled distances
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space, and let
\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2}
be atomless standard probability spaces.
For
\bm{w}_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})
and
\bm{w}_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
, define unlabeled distances

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\inf_{\Phi}\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}},

\displaystyle\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\inf_{\Phi}\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}},

for all
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
.
Here,
\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi}=\bm{w}_{2}(\Phi(\cdot),\Phi(\cdot))
and
\Phi
ranges on all bijective measure-preserving maps
\Phi:\mathbb{I}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{I}_{2}
.

The necessity of the atomless assumption in the definition of unlabeled distances finds a direct analogy in the classical optimal transport theory.
The presence of atoms can lead to situations where the mass must be “split” to satisfy the push-forward condition, which is not permissible within the Monge definition of the Wasserstein distance.

Lastly, we introduce the concept of “coupling distances”, which allows any coupling between probability spaces.

Definition 2.7

(
Coupling distances
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space, and let
\mathbb{I}_{1}
and
\mathbb{I}_{2}
be standard probability spaces.
For
\bm{w}_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})
and
\bm{w}_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
, define coupling distances

\displaystyle\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})}\bigg(\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}+\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\bigg),

\displaystyle\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})}\bigg(\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}+\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}\bigg),

for all
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
.
Here,
\gamma^{T}\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{2},\mathbb{I}_{1})
is given by
\gamma^{T}(\xi,\zeta)=\gamma(\zeta,\xi)
for all
(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{1}
. The term
(\bm{w}_{1}\gamma)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
is defined as

\displaystyle(\bm{w}\gamma)(\xi,\zeta)=\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\bm{w}(\xi,\iota)\gamma(\iota,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\iota\in\mathcal{H},\quad\forall(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}.

Similarly,
(\gamma\bm{w}_{2})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
, as well as
(\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1})
and
(\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})
, are defined in the natural way.

The following proposition states that the definitions we have introduced are pseudometrics. However, we do not provide a detailed characterization of the resulting space here, as properties such as completeness and compactness remain unclear to us unless we restrict ourselves to specific compact subsets of
\mathcal{H}
.
Even with such assumptions, the proof requires separate techniques arising from graph limit theory. These properties are added later in Lemma
2.29
.

Proposition 2.8
.

The unlabeled distances
\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
,
\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}
and
\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
,
\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}
are pseudometrics, i.e. satisfying non-negativity, symmetry, and the triangle inequality, with the distance from any element to itself being zero.

The following proposition implies that for
L^{\infty}
-bounded kernels, all unlabeled distances induce the same notion of convergence, and similarly, all coupling distances do as well. This result is a straightforward consequence of Lemma
2.4
. Additionally, we show that coupling distances are relaxations of unlabeled distances.

Proposition 2.9
.

The following statements hold for unlabeled distances (“
\delta
”) if
\mathbb{I}_{1}
,
\mathbb{I}_{2}
are atomless standard probability spaces and hold for coupling distances (“
\gamma
”) if
\mathbb{I}_{1}
,
\mathbb{I}_{2}
are standard probability spaces:
For any
\bm{w}_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})
and
\bm{w}_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})\leq\delta_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})\leq 4\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2}),

\displaystyle\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})\leq\gamma_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})\leq 4\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2}).

For all
1<p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})\leq\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})\leq\delta_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})^{1-\theta}w_{\max}^{\theta},

\displaystyle\gamma_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})\leq\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})\leq\gamma_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})^{1-\theta}w_{\max}^{\theta},

with
\theta=\max\left\{\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{q}\right\}
and
w_{\max}=\max\{\|\bm{w}_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\bm{w}_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\}
.

Moreover, if
\mathbb{I}_{1}
,
\mathbb{I}_{2}
are atomless standard probability spaces, then

\displaystyle\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2}),

\displaystyle\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2}),

for all
1<p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
.

2.3.
Bi-coupling distances and functional-valued coupling distances

We now present how our bi-coupling distances are associated with the more abstract coupling distances on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathcal{H})
. The proofs are postponed to Section
3.3
.

To begin with, we clarify our definitions of the Fourier transform and Sobolev spaces to avoid any ambiguity in subsequent calculations.

Definition 2.10

(
Fourier transform and Sobolev spaces on the torus
)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
denote the one-dimensional torus. Specifically, we consider
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, where the points
x
and
(x+n)
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
are identified.

For a function
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
, the Fourier transform (or Fourier series coefficients) of
f
is the sequence
(\hat{f}(m))_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
defined by

\displaystyle\hat{f}(m)\vcentcolon=\mathcal{F}(f)(m)\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)e^{-2\pi imx}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad\text{for all }m\in\mathbb{Z}.

This definition extends to generalized functions such as
f\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})
using the theory of tempered distributions and duality.

The Sobolev norms
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, for
s\in\mathbb{R}
, are defined using the Fourier transform:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T})}\vcentcolon=\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+4\pi^{2}m^{2})^{s}\Big|\hat{f}(m)\Big|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Next, we introduce a family of critical convolutional kernels associated with negative Sobolev spaces, following the notation and definitions in
[
21
]
.

Definition 2.11

(
\Lambda_{s}
kernels
)
.

For any
s>1/4
, define the operator
\Lambda_{s}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
via its Fourier transform. The Fourier coefficients of
\Lambda_{s}
are given by

\displaystyle\hat{\Lambda}_{s}(m)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{(1+4\pi^{2}m^{2})^{s}},\quad\text{for all }m\in\mathbb{Z}.

For simplicity, we denote
\Lambda=\Lambda_{1}
.

Next, we present some results regarding the
\Lambda_{s}
kernels, which are critical to our analysis.

Lemma 2.12
.

The kernels
\Lambda_{s}
satisfy the following properties:

(1)

Function spaces: If
s>1/4
,
\Lambda_{s}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
. Also, if
s>1/2
,
\Lambda_{s}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
.

(2)

Kernel properties: If
s>1/4
, the function
\Lambda_{s}
is positive, symmetric, and satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Lambda_{s}(x)\,dx=1.

(3)

Sobolev norm representations: If
s>1/2
, the Sobolev norm
H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}}f(x)\Lambda_{s}(x-y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\|\Lambda_{s/2}\star f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

(4)

Explicit form of
\Lambda(x)
:
The kernel
\Lambda=\Lambda_{1}
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\Lambda(x)\vcentcolon=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-|x+l|\right).

Define

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\max}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\max_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\Lambda(x)=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\exp(-|l|),

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\min}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\min_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\Lambda(x)=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-\left|l+\tfrac{1}{2}\right|\right).

(5)

Norm of probability distributions:
The
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
norm of the Dirac delta at any
x\in\mathbb{T}
is

\displaystyle\|\delta_{x}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}=\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}},

and for any
f\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
that is not concentrated as a Dirac delta, we have the strict inequality

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}<\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}}.

(6)

Lipschitz estimate for
\Lambda(x)
:
The following Lipschitz-type estimate holds for all
x\in\mathbb{T}
:

\displaystyle 2(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})|x|\leq\Lambda(0)-\Lambda(x)\leq\Lambda_{\max}|x|.

Next, we establish the compactness of negative Sobolev spaces.

Lemma 2.13
.

For any
s>\frac{1}{2}
, the canonical embedding
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})\to H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
is a compact operator. Moreover, on the space
\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{T})
, the
H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
norm induces the weak-* topology of the measures.

We now demonstrate how a pair
(w,X)
from Theorem
1.7
can be reformulated as a kernel with values in the Hilbert space
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
. This is one of the most conceptually important definitions in this article.

Definition 2.14

(
The kernel associated to
(w,X)
)
.

From the pair
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and
X:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
, we define the kernel
\bm{w}_{w,X}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})\oplus\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{w,X}(\xi,\zeta)\vcentcolon=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{X(\zeta)}\\
w(\xi,\zeta)\delta_{X(\zeta)}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})\oplus\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}),\quad\forall(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}.

It is straightforward to see that
\bm{w}_{w,X}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}))
from the inclusion
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})\subset H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
.

The following lemma shows that the bi-coupling distances defined on pairs of
(w,X)
and the coupling distances defined on kernels
\bm{w}_{w,X}
are topologically equivalent.
We avoid stating the lemma as convergence at this point because the closedness and compactness of the topology rely on Lemma
2.29
, which is presented later in this section. Nevertheless, the uniform bound across the metrics, as suggested in Lemma
2.1
, can be directly achieved in the proof.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbb{I}_{n}
be a probability space, let
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
, and let
X^{(n)}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
