be measurable. Assume that there exists a constant
w_{\max}<\infty
such that
\|w^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})}\leq w_{\max}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the following are all equivalent notions of Cauchy sequence:

(1)

under coupling distance from cut norm and
\mathcal{H}=H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq n}\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{w^{(n_{1})},X^{(n_{1})}},\bm{w}_{w^{(n_{2})},X^{(n_{2})}})=0,

(2)

under coupling distance from operator norm (
1<p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
) and
\mathcal{H}=H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq n}\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{w^{(n_{1})},X^{(n_{1})}},\bm{w}_{w^{(n_{2})},X^{(n_{2})}})=0,

(3)

under bi-coupling distance, from the cut norm and the Wasserstein-1 distance

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq n}d_{\square,W_{1}}\big[(w^{(n_{1})},X^{(n_{1})}),(w^{(n_{2})},X^{(n_{2})})\big]=0,

(4)

under bi-coupling distance, from operator norm (
1<p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
) and Wasserstein-1 distance

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq n}d_{L^{p}\to L^{q},W_{1}}\big[(w^{(n_{1})},X^{(n_{1})}),(w^{(n_{2})},X^{(n_{2})})\big]=0.

This equivalence is closely related to Part 5 of Lemma
2.12
, which states that Dirac deltas are the only elements in
\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{T})
that achieve the maximum in the
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
norm.
This ensures that when a Dirac delta is coupled with something different, there is always a positive contribution to the coupling distance
\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
.
While we can define a similar form for any
(w,f)

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{w,f}(\xi,\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}f(\zeta)\\
w(\xi,\zeta)f(\zeta)\end{pmatrix}\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}),\quad\forall(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T},

it is unclear whether the coupling disntace
\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
would still satisfy an assertion analogous to Lemma
2.15
.
This provides technical reassurance for the discussion in Section
1.6
that identifying a
\xi
-fiberwise law of agents
f(t,x,\xi)
is quite unnatural.

2.4.
Functional-valued homomorphism density: Observables

Now we explain how the coupling distances on
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathcal{H})
are related to the observables (graph homomorphism densities).
The proofs are postponed to Section
3.4
.

To begin, we need to clarify several definitions further to avoid ambiguity, starting with the definition on the graph theory side.

Definition 2.16

(
Oriented simple graph
)
.

An oriented simple graph
F=(\mathsf{v}(F),\mathsf{e}(F))
consists of:

•

A set of vertices
\mathsf{v}(F)
.

•

A set of directed edges
\mathsf{e}(F)\subset\mathsf{v}(F)\times\mathsf{v}(F)
such that:

\displaystyle\text{ if }(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),\text{ then }(j,i)\notin\mathsf{e}(F).

Define
\mathcal{G}
as the set of all oriented simple graphs.

Additionally, for an oriented simple graph
F\in\mathcal{G}
, define
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\vcentcolon=\{j\in\mathsf{v}(F):\exists i\in\mathsf{v}(F)\text{ s.t. }(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)\}.

In this context, the notation
(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)
indicates a directed edge from vertex
i
to vertex
j
. Therefore, the set
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)
represents all vertices in
F
that serve as the starting points (i.e., sources) of at least one directed edge.

Remark 2.17
.

The notion of an
oriented simple graph
here is more restrictive than the definitions of a
directed simple graph
commonly found in the literature, where symmetric pairs of directed edges (bi-directed edges) are allowed.
This broader notion, however, does not play a role in our analysis.

We also need to clarify the following definitions from functional analysis.

Definition 2.18

(
Product and direct/tensor products
)
.

Let
S
be a finite set. We define the following constructs:

•

Given a topological space or measure space
\mathbb{D}
, the product space indexed by
S
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{S}\coloneqq\prod_{i\in S}\mathbb{D}.

For functions defined on the product space
\mathbb{D}^{S}
, the tensorization is defined by

\displaystyle f^{\otimes S}(x)\coloneqq\prod_{i\in S}f(x_{i}),

where
x=(x_{i})_{i\in S}\in\mathbb{D}^{S}
.

•

Given a Banach space
\mathcal{B}
, we define the direct sum and tensor product spaces indexed by
S
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\oplus S}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in S}\mathcal{B},\quad\mathcal{B}^{\otimes S}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i\in S}\mathcal{B}.

Here,
\mathcal{B}^{\oplus S}
denotes the direct sum of copies of
\mathcal{B}
indexed by
S
, and
\mathcal{B}^{\otimes S}
denotes the tensor product of copies of
\mathcal{B}
indexed by
S
. (The tensor product should be understood as the projective tensor product, or equivalently, when
\mathcal{B}
is a Hilbert space, the Hilbert tensor product.)

•

When the index set
S
is the set
[k]=\{1,\dots,k\}
for some positive integer
k
, we adopt the more conventional notations:

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}^{k}\coloneqq\mathbb{D}^{[k]},\quad f^{\otimes k}\coloneqq f^{\otimes[k]},\quad\mathcal{B}^{\oplus k}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}^{\oplus[k]},\quad\mathcal{B}^{\otimes k}\coloneqq\mathcal{B}^{\otimes[k]}.

Throughout the article, the index sets
S
we will mainly use are
\mathsf{v}(F)
,
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)
and
\mathsf{e}(F)
.

In the following, we summarize a few key results regarding the tensorization of
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})\subset H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
that are required for our analysis.

Proposition 2.19
.

The following properties are true:

(1)

Sobolev norm representations: For any
s>1/2
, the norm of the tensorized Hilbert space
(H^{-s}(\mathbb{T}))^{\otimes k}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\|f\|_{(H^{-s}(\mathbb{T}))^{\otimes k}}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigg(\prod_{i\in[k]}(1+4\pi^{2}m_{i}^{2})^{-s}\bigg)\Big|\hat{f}(m_{1},\dots,m_{k})\Big|^{2}\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}\times\mathbb{T}^{k}}f(x)\Lambda_{s}^{\otimes k}(x-y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\|\Lambda_{s/2}^{\otimes k}\star f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{k})}.

(2)

Equivalent of topologies: For any
s>\frac{1}{2}
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the canonical embedding

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{k})\simeq(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))^{\otimes k}\to(H^{-s}(\mathbb{T}))^{\otimes k}

is a compact operator. Moreover, on the space
\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
, the
(H^{-s}(\mathbb{T}))^{\otimes k}
norm induces the weak-* topology of the measures.

The next definition extends the classical notion of graphon homomorphism density using tensorized Hilbert spaces.

Definition 2.20

(
Tensorized graph homomorphism
)
.

For any
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathcal{H})
and
F\in\mathcal{G}
, define the tensorized graph homomorphism density
\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}
as

\displaystyle\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

We would like to investigate the tensorized graph homomorphism of the kernel
\bm{w}_{w,X}
associated with
(w,X)
as defined in Definition
2.14
, restated here:

Definition 2.21

(
The kernel associated to
(w,X)
, restate
)
.

For any
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and any
X:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
, define the kernel
\bm{w}_{w,X}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}})
as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{w,X}(\xi,\zeta)\vcentcolon=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{X(\zeta)}\\
w(\xi,\zeta)\delta_{X(\zeta)}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}},\quad\forall(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}.

For simplicity in subsequent calculations, we take the index set of the direct sum to be
\{0,1\}
in the restated definition.
This kernel
\bm{w}_{w,X}
remarkably satisfies the following proposition:

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and
X:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
. Take the kernel
\bm{w}_{w,X}
as defined in Definition
2.21
.
Then by Definition
2.20
,

\displaystyle\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X})

\displaystyle\in(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}\simeq(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}\simeq\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}.

For each
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
, the component
\big(\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X})\big)_{s}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})
is given by

\displaystyle\big(\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X})\big)_{s}=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\delta_{X(\xi_{j})}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i},

and is supported in the subspace

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}}\vcentcolon=\big\{(x_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\in\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}:x_{i_{1},j}=x_{i_{2},j},\ \forall(i_{1},j),(i_{2},j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)\big\}.

The proposition suggests that
\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X})
lies in a specific subspace of
(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}
, namely

\displaystyle\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X})\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}.

Consequently, we introduce two types of definitions for observables to reflect this structure.

Definition 2.23
.

We define the following constructs:

•

For any
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
, any
X:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
and any
F\in\mathcal{G}
, define the normal observable
\bm{t}(F,w,X)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}
as

\displaystyle\bm{t}(F,w,X)\vcentcolon=\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X}).

•

For any
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
, any
X:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
and any
F\in\mathcal{G}
, define the plain observable
\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\in(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}\simeq\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}
by the following:
On each
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
, the component
(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X))_{s}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})
is given by

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\delta_{X(\xi_{j})}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

•

Moreover, for any
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
, any
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
,

f(\xi,\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
and any
F\in\mathcal{G}
, define the plain observable
\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)\in(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}\simeq\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}
by the following:
On each
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
, the component
(\bm{\tau}(F,w,f))_{s}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})
is given by

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)\big)_{s}\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}f(\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

These definitions are related to the following proposition.

Proposition 2.24
.

The following canonical map is bijective

\displaystyle\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}:\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}}

\displaystyle(x_{j})_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}

\displaystyle\mapsto(x_{i,j}=x_{j})_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}.

For any
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and
X:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
,

\displaystyle(\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}^{\#})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X))=\bm{t}(F,w,X),

where
(\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}^{\#})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}
is the pushforward map of measures defined on

\displaystyle\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}^{\#}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}})

\displaystyle(\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}^{\#})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}

In addition, for any

w:L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
,

f(\xi,\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
.
Let the pair

\displaystyle w\in L^{\infty}((\mathbb{I}\ltimes_{f}\mathbb{T})\times(\mathbb{I}\ltimes_{f}\mathbb{T})),\quad X:\mathbb{I}\ltimes_{f}\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{T}

be the lift of
(w,f)
. Then

\displaystyle\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)=\bm{\tau}(F,w,X).

While either definition of observables would suffice to determine the other, introducing both offers distinct advantages. The normal observables
\bm{t}(F,w,X)
are more aligned with the graph limit theory, as we obtain it directly through the tensorized homomorphism densities and can extend almost all the arguments from
[
8
,
29
]
and
[
5
,
14
,
18
]
.
In contrast, the plain observables
\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)
align more closely with the mean-field theory, which simplifies the associated hierarchies of PDEs when the arguments of
[
19
,
21
]
are reproduced. Moreover, the plain observables
\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)
have the unique advantage of being directly defined from
(w,f)
. Using a tensorized density approach to define normal observables
\bm{t}(F,w,X)
from
(w,f)
would lead to correlations within densities on each
\xi
-fiber. This would result in the support of the measure falling outside of the
\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}}
subspace, causing the subsequent arguments to break down entirely.

We now summarize all equivalent notions of convergence for these definitions that will be used throughout the article. Unlike in Lemma
2.15
, we state the lemma here in terms of convergence, as the weak-* compactness of Borel measures on compact sets is a well-known classical result.

Proposition 2.25
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For any sequence of standard probability spaces
\mathbb{I}_{l}
, kernels
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
, states
X^{(n)}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
, and any subset
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
,
the following notions of convergence are all equivalent:

(1)

under weak-* convergence of all normal observables:

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\bm{t}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\bm{t}(F)_{\infty}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})},

(2)

under weak-* convergence of all components of plain observables:

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\forall s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)},\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})\big)_{s}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\big(\bm{\tau}(F)_{\infty}\big)_{s}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}),

(3)

under tensorized negative Sobolev convergence of all normal observables:

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{t}(F)_{\infty}\|_{(H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}}=0,

(4)

under tensorized negative Sobolev convergence of all components of plain observables:

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\forall s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)},

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}(F)_{\infty}\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}=0,

(5)

under the following metrization of normal observables by weighted supremum:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{F\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\bigg[(4(1+w_{\max})\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{-|\mathsf{e}(F)|}

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{t}(F)_{\infty}\|_{(H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}}\bigg]=0,

(6)

under the following metrization of plain observables by weighted supremum:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{F\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime},s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}}\bigg[(4(1+w_{\max})\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{-|\mathsf{e}(F)|}

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}(F)_{\infty}\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\bigg]=0.

An example of a subset
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
from the above definition, which is of particular interest to us, is defined below.

Definition 2.26

(
Oriented tree
)
.

An oriented tree
T=(\mathsf{v}(T),\mathsf{e}(T))
is an oriented simple graph (i.e.,
T\in\mathcal{G}
) satisfying the following conditions:

•

There are no undirected cycles in
T
. That is, there does not exist a sequence of distinct vertices
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\in\mathsf{v}(T)
with
k\geq 2
such that by taking
v_{k+1}=v_{1}
,

\displaystyle(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in\mathsf{e}(T)\quad\text{or}\quad(v_{i+1},v_{i})\in\mathsf{e}(T),\quad\forall i\in[k]

•

The underlying undirected graph of
T
is connected. That is, for any two vertices
u,v\in\mathsf{v}(T)
, there exists a sequence of vertices
u=w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{k}=v
such that

\displaystyle(w_{i},w_{i+1})\in\mathsf{e}(T)\quad\text{or}\quad(w_{i+1},w_{i})\in\mathsf{e}(T),\quad\forall i\in[k-1].

Define
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{G}
as the set of all oriented trees.

Remark 2.27
.

Our notion of oriented trees is broader than the definitions of directed trees commonly found in the literature, where edge directions are often strictly specified. Specifically, our definition gives a larger family of trees than that presented in
[
19
,
21
]
.

2.5.
Extending existing literature and proving Theorem
1.7

The following lemmas extend key results from the existing literature. Proofs for these lemmas will be provided in subsequent sections.

The first lemma establishes well-posedness and stability under an alternative metric.

Lemma 2.28

(
Main result in observables
)
.

Let
\mu\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
,
\sigma\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T})
,
\nu\geq 0
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
N_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
and
X^{(n)}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
be either of the following:

•

For some
N_{n}\geq 1
,
\mathbb{I}_{n}=\{1,\dots,N_{n}\}
equipped with uniform atomic measure
1/N_{n}
on each point, and
w^{(n)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{n}\times N_{n}}
and
X^{(n)}_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{N_{n}}
, be connection weight matrices and initial data for the ODE/SDE system (
1.1
).

•

For formally
N_{n}=\infty
,
\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}
is an atomless probability space and
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime})
,
f^{(n)}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}
,

f^{(n)}_{0}(\cdot,\xi)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
, be the weight kernel and initial data for the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
).

Let
\mathbb{I}_{n}=\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}\ltimes_{f^{(n)}_{0}}\mathbb{T}
,
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
and
X^{(n)}_{0}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
be the lift.

Assume that
N_{n}\to\infty
,
\|w^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq w_{\max}<\infty
, and the initial data
X^{(n)}_{0}
are deterministic.
Then the following holds:

•

(Well-posedness). For each
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, there exists either

–

an unique solution
X^{(n)}(t)
of ODE or SDE system (
1.1
) with connection weights
w^{(n)}
and initial data
X^{(n)}_{0}
, for all
t\geq 0
.
(When
\nu>0
,
X^{(n)}(t)
are stochastic processes only depend on the randomness of independent Brownian motions
(B^{n}_{i})_{i\in[N_{n}]}
.)

–

an unique solution
f^{(n)}(t)
of the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
) with weight kernel
w^{(n)}
and initial data
f^{(n)}_{0}
, for all
t\geq 0
,

f^{(n)}(t)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}
,

f(t,\cdot,\xi)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
.

Let
\mathbb{I}_{n}(t)=\mathbb{I}_{n}^{\prime}\ltimes_{f^{(n)}(t)}\mathbb{T}
,
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}(t)\times\mathbb{I}_{n}(t))
and
X^{(n)}(t):\mathbb{I}_{n}(t)\to\mathbb{T}
be the lift. (Note that the definition of
\mathbb{I}_{n}(t)
now depends on time
t
).

•

(Stability).
If the initial data
(w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})
is converging to
(w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}_{0})
in the sense of

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{T\in\mathcal{T},s\in{\{0,1\}}^{\mathsf{e}(T)}}\bigg[(4(1+w_{\max})\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{-|\mathsf{e}(T)|}

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(T,w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}(T,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}_{0})\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(T)}}\bigg]=0,

then at any later time
t>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sup_{T\in\mathcal{T},s\in{\{0,1\}}^{\mathsf{e}(T)}}\bigg[(4(1+w_{\max})\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{-|\mathsf{e}(T)|}

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(T,w^{(n)},X^{(n)}(t))\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}(T,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}(t))\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(T)}}\bigg]\Bigg]=0.

The full proof of this lemma is provided in Appendix
A
, where we apply techniques from
[
19
,
21
]
.

The next lemma complements the previous one by addressing the compactness. Although it is stated in terms of the unlabeled distance
\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
, Proposition
2.9
implies that
\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\leq\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
, thereby ensuring compactness with respect to the weaker coupling distance
\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
as well.

Lemma 2.29

(
Compactness lemma
)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a Banach space compactly embedded into a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
For any sequence
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n};\mathcal{B})
that each
\mathbb{I}_{n}
is an atomless probability space and satisfying uniform bound that

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|\bm{w}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n};\mathcal{B})}<w_{\max}<\infty,

up to an extraction of subsequence (which we still index by
n
), there exists
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathcal{B})
that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0.

Moreover, if the sequence
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is defined from
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
and
X^{(n)}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
, and
\bm{w}_{n}=\bm{w}_{w^{(n)},X^{(n)}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n};H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}})
defined as in Definition
2.21
,
there exist limiting
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and
X:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
, such that
\bm{w}=\bm{w}_{w,X}
.

The full proof of this lemma is presented in Appendix
B
, extending the results from
[
29
]
, but following the proof outlined in the textbook
[
28
]
.

The next lemma is an extension of the celebrated Counting Lemma and Inverse Counting Lemma
[
8
]
.

Lemma 2.30

(
Counting and Inverse Counting Lemma I
)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a compact Banach space embedded into a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
If
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\cup\{\bm{w}\}\subset L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
satisfy uniform bound that

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|\bm{w}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})}<w_{\max}<\infty

Then the following notions of convergence are equivalent:

•

under tensorized negative Sobolev convergence of all graph homomorphism of all oriented simple graphs:

\displaystyle\forall F\in\mathcal{G},\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=0,

•

under unlabeled distance from cut norm and
\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0.

The complete proof of this lemma is presented in Appendix
B
, following the approach outlined in the textbook
[
28
]
. While this lemma is not directly applied in the main proof, it is a critical component of the subsequent lemma, which demonstrates that all equivalent notions of convergence described in Lemma
2.15
and Proposition
2.25
are mutually equivalent across both definitions.
In particular, this implies that the bi-coupling distance used in Theorem
1.7
and the metric used in Lemma
2.28
are equivalent.

Lemma 2.31

(
Counting and inverse counting lemma II
)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a compact Banach space embedded into a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
If
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\cup\{\bm{w}\}\subset L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
satisfy uniform bound that

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|\bm{w}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})}<w_{\max}<\infty

Then the following notions of convergence are equivalent,

•

under tensorized negative Sobolev convergence of all graph homomorphism of all oriented trees:

\displaystyle\forall T\in\mathcal{T},\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes|\mathsf{e}(F)|}}=0,

•

under coupling distance from cut norm and
\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0.

The complete proof of this lemma is presented in Appendix
C
, which extends the results from
[
5
,
14
,
18
]
. Our proof mainly follows the arguments in
[
18
]
.

Remark 2.32
.

The proof of Lemma
2.31
is the only part where we do not yet know how to replace the compactness argument with an explicit uniform bound. If such a replacement could be achieved, it would yield a quantitative stability estimate with an explicit rate.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
1.7
, assuming that all the lemmas hold. In fact, the lemmas have already completed most of the proof. It only remains to assemble them in the appropriate order.

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Take
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}}
and
\mathcal{H}=H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}}
.
By extending
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
and
X^{(n)}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
to
(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times[0,1])
we may assume the underlying spaces are atomless without changing the sequence
\bm{w}_{w^{(n)},X^{(n)}}
in
\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
.
The compactness in
\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
follows directly from Lemma
2.29
. By Proposition
2.9
, this also implies the compactness in
\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
and
\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}
for all
1<p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
.
By Lemma
2.15
, Proposition
2.25
(choosing
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{T}
), and Lemma
2.31
, we find that the compactness in such
\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}
is further equivalent to the compactness under bi-coupling distances
d_{L^{p}\to L^{q},W_{1}}
(for all
1<p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
) and under the maximum over all observables in Lemma
2.28
.

By Lemma
2.28
, we obtain well-posedness and stability in expectation with respect to the maximum over all observables.
Applying Lemma
2.15
, Proposition
2.25
, and Lemma
2.31
again, along with Lemma
2.1
and Lemma
2.2
, we conclude stability in expectation with respect to bi-coupling distances
d_{L^{p}\to L^{q},W_{1}}
for all
1<p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q<\infty
.

∎

2.6.
Conclusions and perspectives

In this article, we rigorously derived the convergence of
empirical data
of a
non-exchangeable
system (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{T}
toward a deterministic limit solving the corresponding extended Vlasov equation (
1.2
). This was established on a distance that is defined through a
convex
optimization problem, which is an interpolation of the optimal transport between measures and the fractional overlay between graphs.

However, proving this result requires a long detour, delving deeply into more concepts of kinetic theory and graph theory. In particular, most of the technical parts of the article were written in terms of
observables
, which can be understood as a tensorization of agent laws and graph homomorphism density.
This work perhaps suggests that the two fields should not only pay attention to each other’s results but also strive for deeper exchanges of ideas.

There are several directions that we wish to explore further, starting from this first result.

•

Firstly, there is the straightforward problem of explicit convergence rate. Let us remark that the stability in Theorem
1.7
can be made quantitative in terms of observables. However, this a priori bound is, roughly speaking, double-exponential in time (see
[
19
,
21
]
for a more detailed discussion).
The absence of convergence rate in the main theorem is due to the significant role compactness arguments played in at least one part of our proofs, namely the proof of Lemma
2.31
.
We acknowledge that fully adapting the compactness arguments within our analysis to explicitly quantitative bounds may present challenges.
Moreover, if the goal is to improve the convergence rate, the prospects of the current proof remain uncertain. Even when “projected onto the two components”, that is, by considering the particle system and graph limit techniques in the proof separately, the best convergence rates available in the existing literature are quite poor. Achieving a practical convergence rate may require entirely different approaches.
It might also be more practical to begin with problems that impose stronger assumptions on connection weights, even at the expense of reduced generality.

•

A second interesting open question is to what extent our results can be adapted to the various alternative definitions of graph limits found in the existing literature. We expect (though not necessarily correctly) that establishing an analogous result based on
L^{p}
graphons with
p>1
would be technically highly challenging but could follow essentially the same roadmap as outlined in this article. Also, it could be that the resulting convergence is only valid for the bi-coupling distance with a weaker operator norm (such as
L^{\infty}\to L^{1}
), which might result in less favorable properties from an optimization perspective.
A more non-trivial extension is toward the extended kernels discussed in
[
19
,
21
]
, which corresponds to very sparse connection weights. While all our definitions are at least well-defined (in the sense that no integrability issue during the definition process), the current proof relies on the inverse counting lemma of standard graphon, which cannot be directly defined under the conditions in
[
19
,
21
]
. To reproduce the results, the minimum requirement would be a counting lemma for tree homomorphisms and fractional overlay that does not depend on the standard inverse counting lemma. However, additional challenges are difficult to foresee without further exploration.

•

A third concern, shared with
[
21
]
, is how to extend the results to other types of dynamics. Our proof relies heavily on
H^{-1}
estimates in one dimension. Extending this to higher dimensions is conceptually straightforward, as long as a sufficiently large exponent
s
is chosen,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is included by
H^{-s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by Sobolev embedding.
However, applying the Leibniz rule in such
H^{-s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
would require greater differentiability of
\mu
and
\sigma
compared to what is assumed in this article. To establish results under weaker differentiability, more advanced techniques from commutator estimates and harmonic analysis may be necessary.

•

A final interest lies on the application side. Even without convergence rates, our results are compelling from a computational perspective and may warrant numerical experiments. Moreover, both the bi-coupling distance and observables may hold some significance from a data perspective. The optimization problem associated with bi-coupling distance can be interpreted as revealing the correspondence between different parts of two multi-agent systems. Meanwhile, the observables project the system into a high-dimensional space, which could be helpful in studying the intrinsic properties of a single multi-agent system, which are often hidden behind permutation invariance.

3.
Proof of the technical results

We present in this section the proofs of the technical results from Section
2
.

3.1.
Uniform metric bound from compactness argument

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

We only prove
d_{1}(x,y)\leq C^{\downarrow}(d_{2}(x,y))
because the two inequalities are obviously symmetric in the argument.
Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that there exists
r>0
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist points
x_{n},y_{n}\in E
with
d_{2}(x_{n},y_{n})<\frac{1}{n}
but
d_{1}(x_{n},y_{n})>r
.

Since
E
is compact under the topology induced by
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, the sequence
\{x_{n}\}
has a convergent subsequence (which we still denote by
\{x_{n}\}
for convenience) converging to some
x\in E
.
Given that
d_{2}(x_{n},y_{n})<\frac{1}{n}
, it follows that
d_{2}(y_{n},x)\to 0
. But since
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
induce the same topology,
d_{1}(y_{n},x)\to 0
as well.

Using the triangle inequality for
d_{1}
:

\displaystyle d_{1}(x_{n},y_{n})\leq d_{1}(x_{n},x)+d_{1}(x,y_{n}).

Since
x_{n}\to x
and
y_{n}\to x
, we have
d_{1}(x_{n},x)\to 0
and
d_{1}(x,y_{n})\to 0
. Therefore,
d_{1}(x_{n},y_{n})\to 0
.

This contradicts the assumption that
d_{1}(x_{n},y_{n})>r>0
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Consider an arbitrary threshold
r>0
. We can decompose the expectation of
D_{1}
based on the event
\{D_{2}\leq r\}
and its complement
\{D_{2}>r\}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[D_{1}]=\mathbb{E}\left[D_{1}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{D_{2}\leq r\}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[D_{1}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{D_{2}>r\}}\right].

Using the given almost sure bounds, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[D_{1}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[C^{\downarrow}(D_{2})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{D_{2}\leq r\}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[A\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{D_{2}>r\}}\right].

\displaystyle=C^{\downarrow}(r)\cdot\mathbb{P}(D_{2}\leq r)+A\cdot\mathbb{P}(D_{2}>r).

Noting that
\mathbb{P}(D_{2}>r)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[D_{2}]}{r}
by Markov’s inequality, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[D_{1}]\leq C^{\downarrow}(r)+A\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}[D_{2}]}{r}.

Thus, by optimizing the choice of
r
, we obtain the desired function
C^{\downarrow}_{A}
.

∎

3.2.
Functional-valued graphons

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

We begin with the first line.
For any fixed vector
\bm{e}
with
\|\bm{e}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq 1
, standard arguments from graphon theory (see
[
28
]
Chapter 8.2.4) imply that
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}),g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2})
are saturated only when
f,g=\pm 1
almost everywhere. This yields, for any
\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\ f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}),\ g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}\bigg\langle\bm{e},\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}f(\xi)\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)g(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq 4\|\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{\square}\leq 4\|\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}

Taking the supremum over
\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}
, we conclude the first line.

For the second line, note that
\mathbb{I}_{1}
and
\mathbb{I}_{2}
are probability spaces (that is, each has total measure 1). It is straightforward to apply Hölder arguments to get

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})},\quad\forall 1\leq p,q\leq\infty.

For the reverse direction, consider again any fixed
\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}
, which gives

\displaystyle\sup_{\ f\in L^{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{I}_{2}),\ g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}\bigg\langle\bm{e},\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}f(\xi)\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)g(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{q}}

Applying the Riesz-Thorin or Marcinkiewicz interpolation theorem with
\theta=\max\left\{\frac{1}{p},1-\frac{1}{q}\right\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{q}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}^{1-\theta}\|\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{\text{Second Endpoint}}^{\theta}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{\infty,1;\mathcal{H}}^{1-\theta}\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})}^{\theta},

Taking the supremum over
\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}
completes the second line.

∎

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

The non-negativity and the property that the distance from any element to itself is zero are straightforward. It remains to prove the symmetry and the triangle inequality.
Our arguments are written in the
\|\cdot\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}
-norms; however, it is straightforward to extend to
\|\cdot\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
-norms.

Note that any bijective measure-preserving map preserves the
L^{p}
norms, i.e. for such
\Phi:\mathbb{I}_{1}\to\mathbb{I}_{2}
, and for any function
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{1})
, the composition satisfies

\displaystyle\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}=\|f\circ\Phi^{-1}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{2})},

by the change of variables formula.
This essentialy results that for any
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
and any bijective measure-preserving
\Phi:\mathbb{I}_{1}\to\mathbb{I}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}^{\Phi}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1},\ f\in L^{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{I}_{1}),\ g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}\bigg\langle\bm{e},\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1}}f(\xi)\bm{w}(\Phi(\xi),\Phi(\zeta))g(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1},\ f\in L^{q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{I}_{1}),\ g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}\bigg\langle\bm{e},\int_{\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}f(\Phi^{-1}(\xi))\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)g(\Phi^{-1}(\zeta))\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

Apply the argument to the opposite direction with
\Phi^{-1}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}^{\Phi}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}=\|\bm{w}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

Hence, for
\bm{w}_{1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathcal{H})
,
\bm{w}_{2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathcal{H})
, and bijective measure-preserving map
\Phi:\mathbb{I}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{I}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}=\|\bm{w}_{1}^{\Phi^{-1}}-\bm{w}_{2}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}},

which symmetrizes the optimization problem in unlabeled distances.
For the coupling distances, the optimization of

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}+\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

can be symmetrized by simple interchanging the roles of
\gamma
and its transpose
\gamma^{T}
.
This completes the proof of symmetry.

To establish the triangle inequality, consider arbitrary elements
\bm{w}_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n};\mathcal{H})
,
n=1,2,3
along with mappings
\Phi_{n+1,n}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{I}_{n+1}
,
n=1,2
. We then have the following inequality:

\displaystyle\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{3})

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{3}^{\Phi_{3,2}\circ\Phi_{2,1}}\big\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi_{2,1}}\big\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}+\big\|\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi_{2,1}}-\bm{w}_{3}^{\Phi_{3,2}\circ\Phi_{2,1}}\big\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi_{2,1}}\big\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}+\big\|\bm{w}_{2}-\bm{w}_{3}^{\Phi_{3,2}}\big\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

Taking the infimum over
\Phi_{2,1}
and
\Phi_{3,2}
, we obtain the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{3})\leq\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})+\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{2},\bm{w}_{3}).

Also, consider arbitrary elements
\bm{w}_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n};\mathcal{H})
,
n=1,2,3
along with coupling
\gamma_{1,2}\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2}),\gamma_{2,3}\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{2},\mathbb{I}_{3})
. We then have

\displaystyle\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{3})

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma_{12}\gamma_{23}-\gamma_{12}\gamma_{23}\bm{w}_{3}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle+\|(\gamma_{12}\gamma_{23})^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{3}(\gamma_{12}\gamma_{23})^{T}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

The first term can, for example, be expanded as

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma_{12}\gamma_{23}-\gamma_{12}\gamma_{23}\bm{w}_{3}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\circ T_{\gamma_{12}}\circ T_{\gamma_{23}}-T_{\gamma_{12}}\circ T_{\gamma_{23}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{3}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{3})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\circ T_{\gamma_{12}}\circ T_{\gamma_{23}}-T_{\gamma_{12}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\circ T_{\gamma_{23}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{3})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|T_{\gamma_{12}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\circ T_{\gamma_{23}}-T_{\gamma_{12}}\circ T_{\gamma_{23}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{3}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{3})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\circ T_{\gamma_{12}}-T_{\gamma_{12}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle+\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\circ T_{\gamma_{23}}-T_{\gamma_{23}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{3}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{3})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle=\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma_{12}-\gamma_{12}\bm{w}_{2}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}+\|\bm{w}_{2}\gamma_{23}-\gamma_{23}\bm{w}_{3}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

Here, in the second inequality, we use the observation that
T_{\gamma_{12}}
,
T_{\gamma_{23}}
are doubly stochastic operators yet are contractions on
L^{p}
spaces.
This argument can also be applied to the second term, which gives

\displaystyle\|(\gamma_{12}\gamma_{23})^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{3}(\gamma_{12}\gamma_{23})^{T}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\gamma_{12})^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}(\gamma_{12})^{T}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}+\|(\gamma_{23})^{T}\bm{w}_{2}-\bm{w}_{3}(\gamma_{23})^{T}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

Take the infinum over all
\gamma_{12},\gamma_{23}
we obtain the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{3})\leq\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})+\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{2},\bm{w}_{3}).

∎

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

The first part of the proof is to use the following argument repetitiously:
“If
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is continuous and monotonic and
x_{\alpha}\leq f(y_{\alpha})
for all
\alpha
, take the infimum over all
\alpha
yields
\inf x\leq\inf f(y_{\alpha})
, this leads to
\inf x\leq f(\inf y)
, allowing us to conclude
\inf x\leq\inf y
.”
The
\alpha
in the above argument would be either the bijective measure preserving map
\Phi
or the coupling
\gamma
.

For the last part of the proof, we use the following standard arguments from optimal transport.
Starting with a bijective measure preserving map
\Phi
, we can construct a specific coupling measure
\gamma
concentrated on the graph of
\Phi
.
This means
\gamma
is defined via:
For measurable sets
A_{1}\subset\mathbb{I}_{1}
and
A_{2}\subset\mathbb{I}_{2}
.

\displaystyle\gamma(A_{1}\times A_{2})=\big|\{x\in A_{1}\,|\,\Phi(x)\in A_{2}\}\big|.

This is the pushforward
(\text{Id}\times\Phi)_{\#}
of the probability measure on
\mathbb{I}_{1}
. It is straightforward to verify that
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
and that

\displaystyle T_{\gamma}=\Phi^{-1},\quad T_{\gamma^{T}}=\Phi.

Taking

\displaystyle\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}+\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

It is straightforward to verify that

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\circ\Phi^{-1}-\Phi^{-1}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}_{\leq 1}}\|T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}-\Phi^{-1}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}}\circ\Phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{I}_{1})\to L^{q}(\mathbb{I}_{1})}

\displaystyle=\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

Apply a similar argument to the second term we have

\displaystyle\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}=\|\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}^{\Phi}\|_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}.

Take the summation and take the infimum over
\Phi
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\gamma_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta_{p,q;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2}).

∎

3.3.
Bi-coupling distances and functional-valued coupling distances

Proof of Lemma
2.12
.

Parts 1, 2, 3, and 4 of the lemma are straightforward exercises in Fourier analysis. As a hint for Part 4, note that on the real line
\mathbb{R}
, when
s=1
, the inverse Fourier transform of
1/(1+4\pi^{2}\xi^{2})
is
\exp(-|x|)/2
.

To compute the norm of the Dirac delta, we substitute it directly into the definition of the
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
norm, resulting in
\|\delta_{0}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}=\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}}
.
For the norm of other probability measures, observe that
\Lambda_{\max}
is reached only at
x=0
. Therefore, for any probability measure
f
that is not concentrated as a Dirac delta, a part of the integral defining the
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
norm will be strictly less than
\Lambda_{\max}
. This results in the strict inequality
\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}<\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}}
.

Regarding the Lipschitz estimate, we first note that
|\Lambda^{\prime}(x)|\leq\Lambda(x)
, which is more easily observed when considering
\Lambda(x)
extended to the whole real line
\mathbb{R}
. For the lower bound, it is straightforward to verify that the function
\Lambda(x)
is convex when viewed on the interval
[0,1]
and reaches its minimum at
x=\tfrac{1}{2}
. The remainder of the estimate follows from the standard arguments in convex analysis and the properties of
\Lambda(x)
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.13
.

To proof the compactness of the embedding
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})\to H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
, we first observe that the embedding
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})\to H^{-\left(\frac{s}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)}(\mathbb{T})
is bounded. Additionally, the embedding
H^{-\left(\frac{s}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\right)}(\mathbb{T})\to H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
is compact according to the Rellich-Kondrachov compactness theorem. Consequently, the composition of these embeddings ensures that
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})\to H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
is compact.

Next, we establish that the
H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
norm induces the weak-* topology on
\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{T})
. Consider a sequence
\{f_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{T})
such that
\|f_{n}-f\|_{H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})}\to 0
. For any test function
\varphi\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
, the dual pairing satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi(f_{n}-f)\,dx\to 0.

Since
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
is dense in
C(\mathbb{T})
, this convergence extends to all continuous test functions, implying that
f_{n}
converges to
f
in the weak-* topology of measures.

Conversely, suppose that
\{f_{n}\}
is a sequence in
\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{T})
that converges to
f
in the weak-* topology. Then, for each
x\in\mathbb{T}
, the convolution
\Lambda_{s}\ast(f_{n}-f)(x)
satisfies

\displaystyle\Lambda_{s}\ast(f_{n}-f)(x)\to 0.

Since
\Lambda_{s}\in C^{\alpha}(\mathbb{T})
for any
0<\alpha<s-\frac{1}{2}
(which can be verified either directly or via Morrey’s inequality), this convergence is uniform over
x\in\mathbb{T}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{s}\ast(f_{n}-f)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\to 0.

By the alternative formulation of the
H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
norm, this implies that

\displaystyle\|f_{n}-f\|_{H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})}=\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}}(f_{n}-f)(x)\Lambda_{s}(x-y)(f_{n}-f)(y)\,dx\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\to 0.

Thus, convergence in the
H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
norm coincides with weak-* convergence of measures. This completes the proof that the
H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})
norm induces the weak-* topology on
\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{T})
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
2.15
.

To be concise, we prove only the equivalence of (1) and (3), as including (2) and (4) follows straightforwardly from Proposition
2.9
.

In the proof, we establish uniform bounds across the two distances directly.
We begin by that the bi-coupling distance is controlled by the coupling distance.
Specifically, for
n=1,2
, any kernels
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}_{n}\times\mathbb{I}_{n})
, and any states
X^{(n)}:\mathbb{I}_{n}\to\mathbb{T}
, we would like to show that for any coupling
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
,

\displaystyle d_{\square,W_{1}}\big[(w^{(1)},X^{(1)}),(w^{(2)},X^{(2)})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})

\displaystyle+\|w^{(1)}\gamma-\gamma w^{(2)}\|_{\square}+\|\gamma^{T}w^{(1)}-w^{(2)}\gamma^{T}\|_{\square}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\downarrow}_{w_{\max}}(\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}+\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}),

where we take
\bm{w}_{n}=\bm{w}_{w^{(n_{1})},X^{(n_{1})}}
to make the notation simpler.
Once this inequality is completed, taking the infimum over all
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
yields a uniform bound on one side.

For any
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
,

\displaystyle(\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2})(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\\
\\
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}w^{(1)}(\xi_{1},\zeta)\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\int_{\mathbb{I}_{2}}\gamma(\xi_{1},\zeta)w^{(2)}(\zeta,\xi_{2})\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\ \mathrm{d}\zeta\end{pmatrix}.

It is relatively easy to bound the cut distance terms:
Notice that
\mathbbm{1}(x)=1,\forall x\in\mathbb{T}
is a unit vector in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
. Hence by taking

\displaystyle\bm{e}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\mathbbm{1}\end{pmatrix},\text{ which is a unit vector in }\mathcal{H}=H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}),

it is straightforward that

\displaystyle\|w_{1}\gamma-\gamma w_{2}\|_{\square}=\|\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{\square}\leq\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}},

\displaystyle\|\gamma^{T}w_{1}-w_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{\square}\leq\|\langle\bm{e},\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{\square}\leq\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}.

Bounding the Wasserstein-1 term is the most technical part of the proof.
Take

\displaystyle\epsilon=\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})

Now, choose the subset
A_{2}\subset\mathbb{I}_{2}
as

\displaystyle A_{2}=\bigg\{\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{I}_{2}:\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\ \gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\geq\epsilon/2\bigg\}.

By the definition of
A_{2}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times A_{2}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}-\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times(\mathbb{I}_{2}\setminus A_{2})}\bigg)|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})

\displaystyle\geq(\epsilon-\epsilon/2)=\epsilon/2.

For any
\xi_{2}\in A_{2}
,

\displaystyle\bigg\langle-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})},\ \int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\bigg\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle=\Lambda\big(X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})\big)-\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\Lambda\big(X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)\big)\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle\geq 2(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}|X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta

For any
\xi_{2}\in A_{2}
, any
r>0
and any
X_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
, such that
|X_{0}-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\leq r
,

\displaystyle\bigg\langle-\delta_{X_{0}},\ \int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\bigg\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\geq 2(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}|X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle-\|\delta_{X_{0}}-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}\bigg\|\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\bigg\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\geq 2(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}|X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2\Lambda_{\max}r}\sqrt{2\Lambda_{\max}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}|X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta}

\displaystyle\geq\Big(2(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})-2\sqrt{2}\Lambda_{\max}\sqrt{r}\epsilon^{-1/2}\Big)\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}|X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta,

where the last inequality is by the definition of
A_{2}
.
By choosing
r<\epsilon(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})^{2}/8\Lambda_{\max}^{2}
, we ensure that the coefficient in the last line is greater than
(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})
.
Next, select
m>4\Lambda_{\max}^{2}/\epsilon(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})^{2}
, which implies
1/2m<r
, and take
X_{i}=\frac{i-1}{m}
for all
i=0,\dots,(m-1)
. Let the subset

A_{2,i}\subset A_{2}\subset\mathbb{I}_{2}
be defined as

\displaystyle A_{2,i}=\bigg\{\xi_{2}\in A_{2}:X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})\in\Big[\frac{i-1}{m}-\frac{1}{2m},\frac{i-1}{m}+\frac{1}{2m}\Big)\bigg\}.

Using the inclusion-exclusion principle, there exists at least one
i=0,\dots,(m-1)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times A_{2,i}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})\geq\epsilon/2m.

Now, notice that
(-\delta_{X_{i}}/\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})
is a unit vector in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
and
\mathbb{I}_{1},A_{2,i}
are measurable set on
\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2}
respectively.
By the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\geq\bigg\langle\begin{pmatrix}-\delta_{X_{i}}/\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}}\\
0\end{pmatrix},\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times A_{2,i}}(\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2})(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle-\delta_{X_{i}}/\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}},\ \int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times A_{2,i}}\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}-\int_{A_{2,i}}\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\ \mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\bigg\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\geq(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})/\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times A_{2,i}}|X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\geq(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})\epsilon/2\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}}\Big\lceil 4\Lambda_{\max}^{2}/\epsilon(\Lambda_{\max}-\Lambda_{\min})^{2}\Big\rceil.

Recalling that we took
\epsilon=\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})
, this essentially provides a bound that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})\lesssim\sqrt{\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}}

This completes one direction of the proof.

Now we address the other direction. We aim to show that for any coupling
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}+\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\downarrow}_{w_{\max}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}|X^{(1)}(\xi_{1})-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\,\gamma(\mathrm{d}\xi_{1},\mathrm{d}\xi_{2})

\displaystyle+\|w^{(1)}\gamma-\gamma w^{(2)}\|_{\square}+\|\gamma^{T}w^{(1)}-w^{(2)}\gamma^{T}\|_{\square}\bigg).

Again, once this inequality is completed, taking the infimum over all
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
yields a uniform bound to the other direction.

Recall that for any
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
,

\displaystyle(\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2})(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\\
\\
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}w^{(1)}(\xi_{1},\zeta)\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\int_{\mathbb{I}_{2}}\gamma(\xi_{1},\zeta)w^{(2)}(\zeta,\xi_{2})\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\ \mathrm{d}\zeta\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore for any
S\subset\mathbb{I}_{1},\ T\subset\mathbb{I}_{2}
and

\displaystyle\bm{e}=\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{0}\\
\bm{e}_{1}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{H}=H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})\oplus H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}).

that is a unit vector,

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bm{e},\int_{S\times T}(\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2})(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\bm{e}_{0},\int_{S\times T}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\bigg]\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle+\bigg\langle\bm{e}_{1},\int_{S\times T}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}w^{(1)}(\xi_{1},\zeta)\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{I}_{2}}\gamma(\xi_{1},\zeta)w^{(2)}(\zeta,\xi_{2})\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\ \mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg]\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\vcentcolon L_{0}+L_{1}.

The first line
L_{0}
is bounded by

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{I}_{2}}\bigg\|\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\bigg\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}\ \mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{I}_{2}}\sqrt{2\Lambda_{\max}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}|X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta}\ \mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(2\Lambda_{\max}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}|X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})-X^{(1)}(\zeta)|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\ \mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The second line
L_{1}
can be further decomposed as
L_{1}=L_{11}+L_{12}
where

\displaystyle L_{11}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\bm{e}_{1},\int_{S\times T}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}w^{(1)}(\xi_{1},\zeta)\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\ \mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{I}_{2}}\gamma(\xi_{1},\zeta)w^{(2)}(\zeta,\xi_{2})\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\ \mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg]\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{S}(\xi_{1})(w^{(1)}\gamma-\gamma w^{(2)})(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\langle\bm{e}_{1},\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\rangle\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}}\|w^{(1)}\gamma-\gamma w^{(2)}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}}\|w^{(1)}\gamma-\gamma w^{(2)}\|_{\square},

and
L_{2}
is the commutator term

\displaystyle L_{12}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\bm{e}_{1},\int_{S\times T}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}}w^{(1)}(\xi_{1},\zeta)\big[\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})-\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\big]\ \mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg]\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|w^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}\|\delta_{X^{(1)}(\zeta)}-\delta_{X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|w^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}\sqrt{2\Lambda_{\max}|X^{(1)}(\zeta)-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|}\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|w^{(1)}\|_{L^{\infty}}\sqrt{2\Lambda_{\max}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{2}}|X^{(1)}(\zeta)-X^{(2)}(\xi_{2})|\gamma(\zeta,\xi_{2})\mathrm{d}\zeta\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Combining the estimates for
L_{0}
,
L_{11}
, and
L_{12}
provides the desired bound for
\|\bm{w}_{1}\gamma-\gamma\bm{w}_{2}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
. A similar estimate can be applied to
\|\gamma^{T}\bm{w}_{1}-\bm{w}_{2}\gamma^{T}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
. This completes the proof of the second direction.

∎

3.4.
Functional-valued homomorphism density: Observables

Proof of Proposition
2.19
.

The proof essentially involves verifying the definitions of the multi-dimensional Fourier transform and tensorization space and reproducing the argument used in the proof of Lemma
2.13
.

To outline the key ideas, note that
(H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))^{\otimes k}
includes
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{k})
, which is dense in
C(\mathbb{T}^{k})
. Thus, the dual pairing of
(H^{-s}(\mathbb{T}))^{\otimes k}
and
(H^{s}(\mathbb{T}))^{\otimes k}
results in the weak-* topology of the measures. Additionally,
\Lambda_{s}^{\otimes k}
is Hölder continuous on
\mathbb{T}^{k}
, allowing us to reproduce the uniform convergence argument.

∎

Proof of Proposition
2.22
.

In Definition
2.21
we can rewrite that

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{w,X}(\xi,\zeta)\vcentcolon=\begin{pmatrix}\big(w(\xi,\zeta)\big)^{0}\delta_{X(\zeta)}\\
\big(w(\xi,\zeta)\big)^{1}\delta_{X(\zeta)}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}},\quad\forall(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}.

Hence we have

\displaystyle\big(\bm{w}_{w,X}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)_{s_{i,j}}=\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\delta_{X(\xi_{j})}

and

\displaystyle\big(\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X})\big)_{s}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{w}_{w,X}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg)_{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(\bm{w}_{w,X}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)_{s_{i,j}}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\delta_{X(\xi_{j})}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

To see that it is supported in the subspace
\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}}
, notice that

\displaystyle\big(X(\xi_{j})\big)_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\in\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}},\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)},

hence the above integral should be supported in
\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}}
as well.

∎

Proof of Proposition
2.25
.

The bijectivity of
\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}
is straightforward. The equivalence of
\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)
and
\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X})
up to the pushforward map can be verified more easily by checking each component individually. For each
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}^{\#}\Big(\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}^{\#}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\delta_{X(\xi_{j})}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}^{\#}\bigg(\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\delta_{X(\xi_{j})}\bigg)\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\delta_{X(\xi_{j})}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\big(\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{w,X})\big)_{s}.

Finally, when
(w,X)
is lifted from
(w,f)
, a componentwise calculation yields

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{(\mathbb{I}\ltimes_{f}\mathbb{T})^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\delta_{x_{j}}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}(\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\mathrm{d}x_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{T})^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\delta_{x_{j}}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}(f(x_{i},\xi_{i})\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\mathrm{d}x_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\delta_{x_{j}}f(x_{j},\xi_{j})\mathrm{d}x_{j}\bigg)

\displaystyle\prod_{j\in\mathsf{v}(F)\setminus\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x_{j},\xi_{j})\mathrm{d}x_{j}\bigg)\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big)^{s_{i,j}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}f(\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)\big)_{s}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
2.25
.

The equivalence of (1) and (2) follows directly from the fact that
\mathsf{i}_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\to\mathsf{e}(F)}:\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\to\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}|_{\textnormal{diag}}
is an invertible linear map.

The equivalences of (1) and (3), as well as (2) and (4), are direct consequences of Part 2 of Proposition
2.19
. To incorporate (5) and (6), consider (5) as an example. Note that we have an a priori bound:

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{t}(F)_{\infty}\|_{(H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}}

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|}\|\bm{t}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{t}(F)_{\infty}\|_{(\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}}

\displaystyle\leq(2(1+w_{\max})\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|}

where the first inequality follows from Part 5 of Lemma
2.12
. This implies that

\displaystyle(4(1+w_{\max})\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{-|\mathsf{e}(F)|}\|\bm{t}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{t}(F)_{\infty}\|_{(H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)})^{\oplus(\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)})}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-|\mathsf{e}(F)|}.

Thus, by standard arguments about the metrization of the product topology on countably many metric spaces, we conclude that the metric induces the same topology.

∎

Appendix A
BBGKY hierarchy for non-exchangeable systems

The objective of this section is to prove Lemma
2.28
, focusing specifically on the stability part of the lemma. Given that all coefficients in our system are Lipschitz continuous, the well-posedness of both the ODE/SDE system and the Vlasov PDE can be established using the standard Picard iteration method. For readers who are not familiar with these techniques, we refer them to
[
19
,
21
]
, which provide comprehensive treatments.

A.1.
Hierarchy of Linear PDEs

We begin by establishing a system of linear PDEs, where each observable depends on a finite number of other observables within the family.
This is related to the algebraic/combinatorial operation of adding a leaf to an oriented tree.

Definition A.1

(
Adding a leaf
)
.

Without loss of generality, assume the vertex set of the oriented simple graph
F=(\mathsf{v}(F),\mathsf{e}(F))
is labeled as

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}(F)=\{1,2,\dots,|\mathsf{v}(F)|\}.

For any
j\in\mathsf{v}(F)
, we define a new graph
(F+j)\in\mathcal{G}
by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}(F+j)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathsf{v}(F)\cup\{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1\}=\{1,2,\dots,|\mathsf{v}(F)|,|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}(F+j)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathsf{e}(F)\cup\{(j,|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1)\}.

Let
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
be an assignment of values to the edges of
F
. We define
(s+j)\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F+j)}
as an assignment of values to the edges of
(F+j)
given by:

\displaystyle(s+j)_{j_{1},j_{2}}=s_{j_{1},j_{2}},\quad\text{for all }(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F),

\displaystyle(s+j)_{j,|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}=1.

In other words, we add a new vertex labeled
|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1
to
F
and connect it with a directed edge from vertex
j
to the new vertex
|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1
. This effectively adds a leaf to
F
at the vertex
j
.
For
s
an assignment of values to the edges,
s+j
retains the same edge values as
s
for all edges in
F
and assigns the value
1
to the new edge
(j,|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1)
.

Let us first consider the case of the PDE. Recall the extended Vlasov PDE from (
1.2
), which we restate here with
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{T}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(t,x,\xi)+\partial_{x}\left(\left[\mu(x)+\int_{\mathbb{I}}w(\xi,\zeta)\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(x,y)f(t,y,\zeta)\,\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\zeta\right]f(t,x,\xi)\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}f(t,x,\xi)=0.

Also, recall the definition of the plain observables from Definition
2.23
. We restate it here in a more concrete form. For each component
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
, the definition is as follows for
z\in\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
in the distributional sense:

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)\big)_{s}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{j_{1}},\xi_{j_{2}})\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\prod_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}f(z_{j},\xi_{j})\prod_{j\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{j}.

With the above definitions, the following computation is presented formally, but it is straightforward to justify rigorously in the distributional sense. The time derivative of
\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)\big)_{s}(t,z)
,
z\in\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
involves taking the derivative of each factor
f(z_{j},\xi_{j})
in the product. For any oriented simple graph
F\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)\big)_{s}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w(\xi_{j_{1}},\xi_{j_{2}})\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\prod_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\setminus\{j^{\prime}\}}f(z_{j},\xi_{j})

\displaystyle\Bigg\{-\partial_{z_{j^{\prime}}}\bigg(\bigg[\mu(z_{j^{\prime}})+\int_{\mathbb{I}}w(\xi_{j^{\prime}},\xi_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\int_{\mathbb{D}}\sigma(z_{j^{\prime}}-z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})

\displaystyle f(t,z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1},\xi_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\,\mathrm{d}z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\bigg]f(t,z_{j^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})\bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{z_{j^{\prime}}}^{2}f(t,z_{j^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})\Bigg\}\prod_{j\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\bigg[-\partial_{z_{j^{\prime}}}\Big(\mu(z_{j^{\prime}})\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)\big)_{s}(z)\Big)+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{z_{j^{\prime}}}^{2}\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,f)\big)_{s}(z)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j^{\prime}}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{D}}\sigma(z_{j^{\prime}},z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\big(\bm{\tau}(F+j^{\prime},w,f)\big)_{s+j^{\prime}}(z,z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\ \mathrm{d}z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\bigg)\bigg].

A critical observation is that for an oriented tree
T\in\mathcal{T}
, we have
(T+j)\in\mathcal{T}
. Thus, the hierarchy above is closed on the set of all oriented trees.

To extend this argument to the ODE/SDE system with finitely many agents, additional definitions are needed.
We can consider either the law of agents (always deterministic) or the empirical measure (a possibly random measure represented as a sum of Dirac measures). Furthermore, we can either allow repeated indices for agents or enforce unique indices. This orthogonal combination yields four possible definitions for our discussion, each with its own advantages.

Definition A.2

(
Several definition of observables
)
.

Define
[N]=\{1,\dots,N\}
. Define
[N]^{k}=\{1,\dots,N\}^{k}
product set, define
[N]^{\wedge k}

\displaystyle{}^{\wedge k}\vcentcolon=\{(l_{1},\dots,l_{k})\in[N]^{k}:l_{1},\dots,l_{k}\text{ are all distinct }\}

When
\mathbb{I}
in Definition
2.23
is
[N]
equipped with uniform atomic measure
1/N
, we define the following constructs for each component
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
:

•

Re-define “plain observables”
\bm{\tau}
(empirical, allowing repetition) as

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}=\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\delta_{X_{l_{j}}}.

•

Define “probability distribution observables”
\bm{\tau}_{p}
(law, allowing repetition) as

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}_{p}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\mathrm{Law}_{(X_{l_{j}})_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}.

•

Define “empirical observables”
\bm{\tau}_{e}
(empirical, no repetition) as

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\wedge\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\delta_{X_{l_{j}}}.

•

Define “marginal observables”
\bm{\tau}_{m}
(law, no repetition) as

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big(\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\wedge\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\mathrm{Law}_{(X_{l_{j}})_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}.

When
\mathbb{I}
in Definition
2.23
is an atomless standard probability space, and
(w,X)
is a lift from some
(w,f)
, we formally define
\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)=\bm{\tau}_{p}(F,w,X)=\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)=\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w,X)
.

The following proposition states that excluding repetitive indices in the definition results in a negligible remainder as
N\to\infty
.

Proposition A.3
.

The following a priori bounds hold: For any
T\in\mathcal{G}
and on each component
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
,

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})}

\displaystyle\leq(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|}

\displaystyle\bigg(1-\prod_{k=1}^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}\frac{N-k+1}{N}\bigg),

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|}(\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|}

\displaystyle\bigg(1-\prod_{k=1}^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}\frac{N-k+1}{N}\bigg).

The same estimate holds if, in the inequalities above, we replace
\bm{\tau}
and
\bm{\tau}_{e}
with
\bm{\tau}_{p}
and
\bm{\tau}_{m}
, respectively.

In particular, these uniform bounds vanish as
N\to\infty
. Since we are working with sequences satisfying
N_{n}\to\infty
, the convergence of
\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})
and
\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})
are equivalent, and similarly, the convergence of
\bm{\tau}_{p}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})
and
\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})
are equivalent.

Proof of Proposition
A.3
.

There is nothing to prove if
\mathbb{I}
in Definition
2.23
is an atomless standard probability space and
N=\infty
is taken formally.
When
\mathbb{I}
is
[N]
equipped with uniform atomic measure
1/N
, for each component
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
,

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\mathsf{v}(F)}\setminus[N]^{\wedge\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\bigotimes_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\delta_{X_{l_{j}}}.

Hence

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\mathsf{v}(F)}\setminus[N]^{\wedge\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big|w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big|^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|}\frac{\big|[N]^{\mathsf{v}(F)}\setminus[N]^{\wedge\mathsf{v}(F)}\big|}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|}\bigg(1-\prod_{k=1}^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}\frac{N-k+1}{N}\bigg),

which is the first inequality.

The second inequality can be derived by combining the first inequality with the following estimate

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|}\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{e}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\big\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})},

which is obtained from Part 1 of Proposition
2.19
.

∎

For simplicity in later calculations, we introduce the following notation for the law of agents:
For any multi-index
l\in[N]^{k}
, let the joint law of the agents be denoted by

\displaystyle f_{l}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\vcentcolon=\mathrm{Law}_{(X_{l_{j}})_{j=1}^{k}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\delta_{X_{l_{j}}}(z_{j})\bigg].

In particular, for the joint law of all agents, we use the simpler notation

\displaystyle f_{[N]}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\vcentcolon=\mathrm{Law}_{(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigotimes_{i\in[N]}\delta_{X_{i}}(x_{i})\bigg].

The Liouville equation governing the joint law of all agents is given by

(A.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{[N]}(t,x)=\sum_{i\in[N]}\bigg[-\partial_{x_{i}}\Big(\mu(x_{i})f_{[N]}(t,x)\Big)+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{x_{i}}^{2}f_{[N]}(t,x)

\displaystyle-\partial_{x_{i}}\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}w_{i,i^{\prime}}\sigma(x_{i},x_{i^{\prime}})f_{[N]}(t,x)\Big)\bigg]

For any multi-index
l\in[N]^{\wedge k}
, we integrate out all dimensions except
l_{1},\dots,l_{k}
in the Liouville equation (
A.1
). This yields

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{l}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\bigg[-\partial_{z_{j}}\Big(\mu(z_{j})f_{l}(t,z)\Big)+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{z_{j}}^{2}f_{l}(t,z)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l_{k+1}\in[N]\setminus\{l_{1},\dots,l_{k}\}}w_{l_{j},l_{k+1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(z_{j},z_{k+1})f_{l,l_{k+1}}(t,z,z_{k+1})\mathrm{d}z_{k+1}\bigg)
