\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k}w_{l_{j},l_{j^{\prime}}}\sigma(z_{j},z_{j^{\prime}})f_{l}(t,z)\Big)\bigg]

where the last two terms correspond to binary interactions, one involving agents other than
l_{1},\dots,l_{k}
and the other involving one of
l_{1},\dots,l_{k}
.

Next, we take the weighted summation in the definition of
\bm{\tau}_{m}
(see Definition
A.2
), using multi-indices
l_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}=(l_{j})_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
and
z=(z_{j})_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
. This gives

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\wedge\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}f_{l_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}}(t,z)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\bigg[-\partial_{z_{j}}\Big(\mu(z_{j})\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}(t,z)\Big)+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{z_{j}}^{2}\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}(t,z)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(z_{j},z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F+j,w,X)\big)_{s+j}(t,z,z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\mathrm{d}z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\bigg)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}(t,z)\bigg].

where the remainder term is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}(t,z)\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\wedge\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}w_{l_{j},l_{j^{\prime}}}\sigma(z_{j},z_{j^{\prime}})f_{l_{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}}}(t,z)\bigg).

Note that the components of
z
are indexed by
\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)\subset\mathsf{v}(F)
, but we still index the new variable as
z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}
for consistency in indexing calculations.

Following the proof of Proposition
A.3
, it is straightforward to verify the a priori bound

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)})}

\displaystyle\leq(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|+1}\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}}|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|/N,

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|+1}(\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|}\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}}|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|/N.

Again, note that these uniform bounds vanish as
N\to\infty
.
For the Vlasov PDE, we formally take
N=\infty
, in which case the remainder term becomes zero.

We summarize the above arguments in the following proposition.

Proposition A.4
.

Let
\mu\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
,
\sigma\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T})
,
\nu\geq 0
.
Let
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and
X_{0}:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
be either of the following:

•

For some
N\geq 1
,
\mathbb{I}=\{1,\dots,N\}
equipped with uniform atomic measure
1/N
on each point, and
w\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
and
X_{0}\in\mathbb{T}^{N_{n}}
, be connection weight matrices and initial data for the ODE/SDE system (
1.1
).

•

For formally
N=\infty
,
\mathbb{I}^{\prime}
is an atomless probability space and
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{I}^{\prime})
,
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}^{\prime};\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}))
that for a.e.
\xi\in\mathbb{I}^{\prime}
,

f_{0}(\cdot,\xi)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{T})
, be the weight kernel and initial data for the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
).

Let
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{I}^{\prime}\ltimes_{f_{0}}\mathbb{T}
,
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
and
X_{0}:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{T}
be the lift.

Assume that
\|w\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq w_{\max}<\infty
. Then both the ODE/SDE system (
1.1
) and the extended Vlasov PDE (
1.2
) have unique solutions. Moreover, the resulting pair
(w,X)
has observables satisfying the following hierarchy of PDEs: For any
F\in\mathcal{G}
and any
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\bigg[-\partial_{z_{j}}\Big(\mu(z_{j})\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}(t,z)\Big)+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{z_{j}}^{2}\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}(t,z)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(z_{j},z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F+j,w,X)\big)_{s+j}(t,z,z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\mathrm{d}z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\bigg)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}(t,z)\bigg]

with the remainder term bounded a priori by

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|+1}(\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|}\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}}|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|/N.

Proof of Proposition
A.4
.

Since all coefficients are at least Lipschitz continuous and bounded, the formal computations illustrated previously can be made rigorous in the distributional sense in a straightforward manner.

∎

A.2.
Energy estimate and the proof of Lemma
2.28

We now demonstrate how to perform an energy estimate on the BBGKY-like hierarchy, as summarized in Proposition
A.4
. For preparation, we review the following lemmas, which are essentially drawn from Section 3 of
[
21
]
. These provide tools for commutator estimates in negative Sobolev spaces.

Lemma A.5
.

Consider
\mu_{m}
of form

\displaystyle\mu_{m}=1\otimes\dots\otimes\mu\otimes\dots\otimes 1,

where
\mu\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
appears in the
m
-th coordinate, i.e.
\mu_{m}(z)=\mu(z_{m})
. Then for any
f\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes k}
, the following inequality holds

\displaystyle\|\mu_{m}f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes k}}\leq 2\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes k}},

Proof of Lemma
A.5
.

We begin with the one-dimensional case. Start with the duality formula

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{-s}(\mathbb{T})}=\sup_{\|g\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 1}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)g(x)\;\mathrm{d}x\bigg|,

and apply the inequality derived from Leibniz’s rule for
s=1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mu f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\|g\|_{H^{1}}\leq 1}\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(x)\mu(x)f(x)\;\mathrm{d}x\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\|g\|_{H^{1}}\leq 1}\|g\mu\|_{H^{1}}\|f\|_{H^{-1}}\leq 2\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}.

Now, consider the higher-dimensional case. WLOG assume that
\mu_{k}
is non-constant in the
k
-th dimension. We introduce the Fourier transform on the first
k-1
dimensions,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\otimes k-1}\otimes I:\mathbb{T}^{k-1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}\times\mathbb{R}.

For clarity, we temporarily denote
m=(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}
. This gives

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{F}^{\otimes k-1}\otimes I\big)\big(\Lambda_{1/2}^{\otimes k}\star(\mu_{k}f)\big)(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1},z_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+4\pi^{2}m_{i}^{2}}}\bigg)\;\big(\Lambda_{1/2}\star_{k}(\mu_{k}\mathcal{F}^{\otimes k-1}f)\big)(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1},z_{k}).

Recall that the definition of
\Lambda_{1/2}
is given in Definition
2.11
.
By Plancherel’s identity,

\displaystyle\|\mu_{k}f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}}\int

\displaystyle\bigg|\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+4\pi^{2}m_{i}^{2}}}\bigg)\;\big(\Lambda_{1/2}\star_{k}(\mu_{k}\mathcal{F}^{\otimes k-1}f)\big)(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1},z_{k})\bigg|^{2}\;\mathrm{d}z_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}}\hskip-5.69046pt\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+4\pi^{2}m_{i}^{2}}}\bigg)\Big\|\big(\mu_{k}\mathcal{F}^{\otimes k-1}f\big)(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1},\cdot)\Big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}.

Since
\mu\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
, apply the result in dimension
1
gives

\displaystyle\Big\|\big(\mu_{k}\mathcal{F}^{\otimes k-1}f\big)(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1},\cdot)\Big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{\otimes k-1}f(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1},\cdot)\Big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\|\mu_{k}f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes k}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\sum_{(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}}\hskip-5.69046pt\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+4\pi^{2}m_{i}^{2}}}\bigg)\Big\|\mathcal{F}^{\otimes k-1}f(m_{1},\dots,m_{k-1},\cdot)\Big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}

\displaystyle=4\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes k}}^{2},

which completes the proof by taking the square root of both sides.
∎

The next lemma addresses the treatment of the binary interaction term.

Lemma A.6
.

For
k\geq 1
, let
f\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes(k+1)}
and
\sigma\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Define
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})
. Then the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\bigg[\bigg(\Lambda_{1/2}^{\otimes k}\star\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(\cdot_{k},z_{k+1})f(\cdot,z_{k+1})\;\mathrm{d}z_{k+1}\bigg)(z)\bigg]^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\;\mathrm{d}z_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\Lambda_{1/2}(z_{j}-u_{j})

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(u_{k},z_{k+1})f(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},z_{k+1})\;\mathrm{d}z_{k+1}\ \mathrm{d}u_{1},\dots,\mathrm{d}u_{k}\bigg]^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\;\mathrm{d}z_{j}

\displaystyle\leq 16\|\sigma\|_{W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes k+1}}^{2}.

The same estimate holds in an analogous manner when the two components in
\sigma
are general indices rather than specifically
k
and
k+1
.

Proof of Lemma
A.6
.

We begin with the lowest possible dimension. When
\sigma\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
and
f\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes 2}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigg[\bigg(\Lambda_{1/2}\star\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(\cdot,z_{2})f(\cdot,z_{2})\;\mathrm{d}z_{2}\bigg)(z_{1})\bigg]^{2}\mathrm{d}z_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Lambda_{1/2}^{\otimes 2}\star(\sigma f)(z_{1},z_{2})\;\mathrm{d}z_{2}\bigg]^{2}\mathrm{d}z_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\big[\Lambda_{1/2}^{\otimes 2}\star(\sigma f)(z_{1},z_{2})\big]^{2}\;\mathrm{d}z_{2}\mathrm{d}z_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 16\|\sigma\|_{W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes 2}}.

In the general case, by the result in dimension
2
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\bigg[\bigg(\Lambda_{1/2}^{\otimes k}\star\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(\cdot_{k},z_{k+1})f(\cdot,z_{k+1})\;\mathrm{d}z_{k+1}\bigg)(z)\bigg]^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\;\mathrm{d}z_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Lambda_{1/2}^{\otimes k+1}\star(\sigma f)(z,z_{k+1})\;\mathrm{d}z_{k+1}\bigg]^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\;\mathrm{d}z_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{k+1}}\big[\Lambda_{1/2}^{\otimes k+1}\star(\sigma f)(z,z_{k+1})\big]^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}\;\mathrm{d}z_{j}

\displaystyle\leq 16\|\sigma\|_{W^{2,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes k+1}}^{2}.

∎

With the two lemmas, we now proceed to prove Lemma
2.28
with the energy estimates.

Proof of Lemma
2.28
.

Let us begin with the case
\nu>0
and denote

(A.2)

\displaystyle\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}\vcentcolon=\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}_{0})\big)_{s},

for any
F\in\mathcal{G}
and any
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
.
From Proposition
A.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\bigg[-\partial_{z_{j}}\Big(\mu(z_{j})\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)\Big)+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{z_{j}}^{2}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(z_{j},z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\Delta^{(n)}_{F+j,s+j}(t,z,z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\mathrm{d}z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\bigg)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}^{(n)}(t,z)\bigg],

where the a priori
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
bound of the remainder term
\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}^{(n)}
vanishes as
n\to\infty
.

The energy estimate in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
from the above PDE reads

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)\big(\Lambda^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\star\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}\big)(z)\mathrm{d}z\bigg)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}2\partial_{t}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)\big(\Lambda^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\star\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}\big)(z)\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}2\big(\Lambda^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\star\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}\big)(z)

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\sum_{j\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\bigg[-\partial_{z_{j}}\Big(\mu(z_{j})\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)\Big)+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\partial_{z_{j}}^{2}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(z_{j},z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\Delta^{(n)}_{F+j,s+j}(t,z,z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\mathrm{d}z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\bigg)

\displaystyle-\partial_{z_{j}}\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}^{(n)}(t,z)\bigg]\Bigg\}\mathrm{d}z.

Integration by parts yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\Bigg\{-\nu^{2}\big(\Lambda^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\star\partial_{z_{j}}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}\big)(t,z)\partial_{z_{j}}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)

\displaystyle+2\big(\Lambda^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\star\partial_{z_{j}}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}\big)(t,z)\bigg[\Big(\mu(z_{j})\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,z)\Big)

\displaystyle+\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(z_{j},z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\Delta^{(n)}_{F+j,s+j}(t,z,z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\mathrm{d}z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\bigg)+\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}^{(n)}(t,z)\bigg]\Bigg\}\mathrm{d}z.

As a standard argument, the positive diffusion term introduces a strictly negative contribution to the summation. Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\bigg[\bigg(-\nu^{2}+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\bigg)\big\|\partial_{z_{j}}\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{\nu^{2}}(L_{\mu;F,s,j}^{(n)}+L_{\sigma;F,s,j}^{(n)}+R_{F,s,j}^{(n)})\bigg]

where, by Lemma
A.5
,

\displaystyle L_{\mu;F,s,j}^{(n)}=\big\|\mu(\cdot_{j})\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}\leq 4\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}}^{2}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2},

and by Lemma
A.6
,

\displaystyle L_{\sigma;F,s,j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\bigg[\Lambda_{1/2}^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}\star

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sigma(\cdot_{j},z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\Delta^{(n)}_{F+j,s+j}(t,\cdot,z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1})\mathrm{d}z_{|\mathsf{v}(F)|+1}\bigg)(z)\bigg]^{2}\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq 16\|\sigma\|_{W^{2,\infty}}^{2}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{F+j,s+j}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F+j)}}^{2},

and by Proposition
A.4
,

\displaystyle R_{F,s,j}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\|\mathscr{R}_{F,s,j}^{(n)}(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\big[(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|+1}(\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|}\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}}|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|/N_{n}\big]^{2}.

In conclusion,

(A.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\bigg(\frac{12}{\nu^{2}}\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}}^{2}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{48}{\nu^{2}}\|\sigma\|_{W^{2,\infty}}^{2}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{F+j,s+j}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F+j)}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{\nu^{2}}\big[(1+w_{\max})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|+1}(\sqrt{\Lambda_{\max}})^{|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|}\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}}|\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)|/N_{n}\big]^{2}\bigg).

We now consider
\mathcal{T}
, the set of all oriented trees. We take the maximum over all observables of oriented trees with an upper bound on the number of vertices:

\displaystyle M^{(n)}_{k}(t)\vcentcolon=\sup_{T\in\mathcal{T},\ |\mathsf{v}(T)|\leq k,\ s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(T)}}\big\|\Delta^{(n)}_{T,s}(t,\cdot)\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(T)}}^{2}.

Since we are considering trees, we have
|\mathsf{e}(T)|+1=|\mathsf{v}(T)|\leq k
. Hence by taking the maximum for (
A.3
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}M^{(n)}_{k}(t)\leq kC_{1}M^{(n)}_{k+1}(t)+C_{2}C_{3}^{k}k^{3}/N_{n}^{2},

where

\displaystyle C_{1}=\frac{12}{\nu^{2}}\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}}^{2}+\frac{48}{\nu^{2}}\|\sigma\|_{W^{2,\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle C_{2}=\frac{3}{\nu^{2}}\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2},\quad C_{3}=(1+w_{\max})^{2}\Lambda_{\max}.

In integral form, by iteration, this becomes:

\displaystyle M^{(n)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle\leq M^{(n)}_{k}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(kC_{1}M^{(n)}_{k+1}(\tau_{1})+C_{2}C_{3}^{k}k^{3}/N_{n}^{2}\bigg)\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\leq M^{(n)}_{k}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\bigg(kC_{1}M^{(n)}_{k+1}(0)+C_{2}C_{3}^{k}k^{3}/N_{n}^{2}\bigg)\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}kC_{1}\int_{0}^{\tau_{1}}\bigg((k+1)C_{1}M^{(n)}_{k+2}(\tau_{2})+C_{2}C_{3}^{k+1}(k+1)^{3}/N_{n}^{2}\bigg)\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\dots

\displaystyle\leq M^{(n)}_{k}(0)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{m-1}\bigg[\bigg((k-1+l)C_{1}M^{(n)}_{k+l}(0)+C_{2}C_{3}^{k-1+l}(k-1+l)^{3}/N_{n}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{l-1}\frac{k-1+i}{1+i}\bigg)t^{l}\bigg]

\displaystyle+\bigg((k-1+m)C_{1}\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq t}M^{(n)}_{k+m}(\tau)+C_{2}C_{3}^{k-1+m}(k-1+m)^{3}/N_{n}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{k-1+i}{1+i}\bigg)t^{m}.

Now, taking the limit as
n\to\infty
, and using our assumptions of initial data convergence and that
N_{n}\to\infty
, all terms vanish except
\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq t}M^{(n)}_{k+m}(\tau)
. For this term, we have an a priori bound

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq t}M^{(n)}_{k+m}(\tau)\leq C_{3}^{k+m},\quad C_{3}=(1+w_{\max})^{2}\Lambda_{\max},

which is derived from the definition of
\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}
(
A.2
) and Part 1 of Lemma
2.19
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}M^{(n)}_{k}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\bigg((k-1+m)C_{1}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq t}M^{(n)}_{k+m}(\tau)\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{k-1+i}{1+i}\bigg)t^{m}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg((k-1+m)C_{1}C_{3}^{k}\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{k-1+i}{1+i}\bigg)C_{3}^{m}t^{m}.

When
0<t<\frac{1}{2C_{3}}
, we let
m\to 0
and conclude that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}M^{(n)}_{k}(t)=0,\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\ \forall 0<t<1/2C_{3}.

We can take any
0<t<1/2C_{3}
as our new starting time and repeat the argument, which gives
\lim_{n\to\infty}M^{(n)}_{k}(t)=0
for any
k
in a longer interval. This essentially gives

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}M^{(n)}_{k}(t)=0,\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\ \forall t\geq 0,

and completes the stability in terms of

\displaystyle\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}\vcentcolon=\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}_{0})\big)_{s},

for
\nu>0
.

Next, consider the case where
\nu=0
. Let
(w,X^{0})
be the solution of (
1.1
) and
(w,X^{\nu})
be a solution with artificial viscosity
\nu
added, both with the same initial data. It is straightforward to verify that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}|X_{i}^{0}(t)-X_{i}^{\nu}(t)|\leq C^{\downarrow}_{w_{\max},\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}},\|\sigma\|_{W^{1,\infty}},t}(\nu).

Similarly, let
(w,f^{0})
be the solution of (
1.2
) and
(w,f^{\nu})
be a solution with artificial viscosity
\nu
added, also with the same initial data,

\displaystyle\int\Big[d_{W_{2}}\big(f^{0}(t,\cdot,\xi),f^{\nu}(t,\cdot,\xi)\big)\Big]^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\leq C^{\downarrow}_{w_{\max},\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}},\|\sigma\|_{W^{1,\infty}},t}(\nu).

where
d_{W_{2}}
denotes the Wasserstein-2 distance.
These metrics induce the weak-* topology of measures. Hence the above bounds essentially imply the following: for all
T\in\mathcal{T}
and
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(T)}
,

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(T,w,X^{0}(t))\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(T,w,X^{\nu}(t))\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(T)}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\downarrow}_{w_{\max},\|\mu\|_{W^{1,\infty}},\|\sigma\|_{W^{1,\infty}},T,t}(\nu).

Now, for a sequence of pairs
(w^{(n)},X^{(n)})
with converging initial data, we have convergence for any artificial viscosity
\nu>0
added at any later time. Using the a priori bound for the solutions (with and without artificial viscosity) and passing the limit
\nu\to 0
, we obtain the convergence of

\displaystyle\Delta^{(n)}_{F,s}\vcentcolon=\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)}_{0})\big)_{s}-\big(\bm{\tau}_{m}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)}_{0})\big)_{s},

in the case
\nu=0
.

It remains to establish the stability estimate (in expectation) for
\bm{\tau}
, using the stability already obtained for
\bm{\tau}_{m}
.
We omit the time variable in the notation, as there is no dynamics in this part.

First, consider
\bm{\tau}_{p}
(which allows repetitive agents in the law) instead of
\bm{\tau}_{m}
. Recall that

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}_{p}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w,X)\big)_{s}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{|\mathsf{v}(F)|}}\sum_{l\in[N]^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big(w_{l_{j_{1}},l_{j_{2}}}\big)^{s_{j_{1},j_{2}}}\mathrm{Law}_{(X_{l_{j}})_{j\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}.

For any oriented tree
T\in\mathcal{T}
and
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(T)}
, the convergence of
\bm{\tau}_{m}
extends to

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}_{p}(T,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})\big)_{s}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\big(\bm{\tau}_{p}(T,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)})\big)_{s},

as the difference between the two sequences
\bm{\tau}_{p}
,
\bm{\tau}_{m}
in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
norm vanishes by Proposition
A.3
.

For any oriented tree
T\in\mathcal{T}
, using the index
\mathsf{v}(T)=\{1,\dots,|\mathsf{v}(T)|\}
, define
T+T
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}(T+T)

\displaystyle=\{1,\dots,2|\mathsf{v}(T)|\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}(T+T)

\displaystyle=\{(1,1+|\mathsf{v}(T)|)\}\cup

\displaystyle\{(i,j)\in\mathsf{v}(T+T)^{2}:(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T)\text{ or }(i-\mathsf{v}(T),j-\mathsf{v}(T))\in\mathsf{e}(T)\}.

In other words, we define
(T+T)
by drawing an additional edge between two copies of
T
.
For any
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathsf{e}(F)}
, define
s+s
by

(s+s)_{i,j}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0&&\text{ if }(i,j)=(1,1+|\mathsf{v}(T)|),\\
&s_{i,j}&&\text{ if }(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T),\\
&s_{i-\mathsf{v}(T),j-\mathsf{v}(T)}&&\text{ if }(i-\mathsf{v}(T),j-\mathsf{v}(T))\in\mathsf{e}(T).\end{aligned}\right.

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)})\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-2}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\Lambda_{2}^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}(z-u)\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)})\big)_{s}(z)

\displaystyle\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)})\big)_{s}(u)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}u\bigg]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\Lambda_{2}^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}(z-u)\bar{\Delta}^{(n)}_{F+F,s+s}(z,u)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}u,

where

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{(n)}_{F+F,s+s}(z,u)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\big(\bm{\tau}_{p}(F+F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})\big)_{s+s}(z,u)

\displaystyle-2\big(\bm{\tau}_{p}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})\big)_{s}(z)\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)})\big)_{s}(u)

\displaystyle+\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)})\big)_{s}(z)\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)})\big)_{s}(u).

From the previous estimate, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\big\|\bar{\Delta}^{(n)}_{F+F,s+s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F+F)}}=0.

By a duality argument,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big\|\big(\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})-\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(\infty)},X^{(\infty)})\big)_{s}\big\|_{H^{-2}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\big\|\bar{\Delta}^{(n)}_{F+F,s+s}\big\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F+F)}}

\displaystyle\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}}\Lambda_{2}^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}(z-u)

\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}(1-\partial_{z_{i}}^{2}-\partial_{u_{i}}^{2}+\partial_{z_{i}}^{2}\partial_{u_{i}}^{2})\bigg)\Lambda_{2}^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}(z-u)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}u\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Here, the second factor is bounded, and the first factor converges to zero as
n\to\infty
. This establishes the convergence of
\bm{\tau}(F,w^{(n)},X^{(n)})
in expectation in the
H^{-2}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
norm. By Proposition
2.19
, the
H^{-2}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
norm induces the weak-* topology for measures. By Lemma
2.1
and Lemma
2.2
, this convergence in expectation can be expressed in terms of any other metric that induces the same weak-* topology on measures, such as the
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\otimes\mathsf{v}^{\prime}(F)}
norm.

Recall that by Proposition
2.25
, the convergence of all observables can be further expressed as the convergence in a single metric by taking the maximum. This completes the proof of Lemma
2.28
.

∎

Appendix B
Graphon isomorphism, cut distance and homomorphism densities

The objective of this section is to prove Lemma
2.29
and Lemma
2.30
. These results are extensions of those of
[
29
]
and
[
8
]
, but we follow the approach outlined in the textbook
[
28
]
.

In our treatment, we have omitted explicit calculations of convergence rates in Counting Lemma and Inverse Counting Lemma. This allows us to present a relatively simpler argument that highlights the conceptual key parts of the proof.

Since in this section we consider only atomless standard probability spaces and do not require any lifting operations, we will keep the discussion throughout on
\mathbb{I}=[0,1]
, in alignment with the classical literature.

Additionally, in this section, we need to consider unlabeled distances for finite graphs. We adopt the usual convention that the cut distance of a finite graph is defined via its piecewise constant extension to
[0,1]^{2}
as follows.

Definition B.1

(
Piecewise constant extension of a finite graph
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space.
Given
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([N]\times[N];\mathcal{H})
, we define the piecewise constant function
\bar{\bm{w}}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
by

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{w}}(\xi,\zeta)\vcentcolon=\sum_{i,j\in[N]}\mathbbm{1}_{\left[\frac{i-1}{N},\frac{i}{N}\right)\times\left[\frac{j-1}{N},\frac{j}{N}\right)}(\xi,\zeta)\,\bm{w}(i,j),\quad\forall(\xi,\zeta)\in[0,1]^{2}.

If one or both of
\bm{w}_{1}
and
\bm{w}_{2}
are defined on a finite set, the unlabeled distance
\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{1},\bm{w}_{2})
should be understood as the cut distance between their piecewise constant extensions
\bar{\bm{w}}_{1}
and
\bar{\bm{w}}_{2}
.

B.1.
Regularity Lemma

In this subsection, we reproduce Szemerédi’s Regularity Lemma
[
35
,
36
]
for
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
.

Definition B.2

(
k
-measurable partition and piecewise projection
)
.

A
k
-measurable partition
P
of the interval
[0,1]
is a collection of
k
measurable subsets
V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{k}
of
[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle V_{i}\cap V_{j}=\varnothing\quad\text{for all }i\neq j.

and

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}V_{i}=[0,1].

Each
V_{i}
is called a class of partition
P
.

Given a function
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
, where
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space, and a
k
-measurable partition
P=\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
, the
piecewise projection

\bm{w}_{P}
is defined as the function in
L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
that is constant on each rectangle
V_{i}\times V_{j}
and equals the average of
\bm{w}
on that rectangle. Formally, for
\xi,\zeta\in[0,1]
:

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{P}(\xi,\zeta)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{V_{i}}(\xi)\,\mathbbm{1}_{V_{j}}(\zeta)\,\bm{w}_{ij},

where

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{ij}=\frac{1}{|V_{i}||V_{j}|}\int_{V_{i}\times V_{j}}\bm{w}(\xi^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}\zeta^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\xi^{\prime},

Remark B.3
.

Alternatively,
\bm{w}_{P}
can be viewed as the conditional expectation of
\bm{w}
with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
P\times P
:

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{P}=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{w}|P\times P\right].

The following results are classical in the scalar-valued case; we simply extend them to the Hilbert space setting.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space, and
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
. Then

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}.

In addition, let
P
be a
k
-measurable partition of
[0,1]
. Then there exists a partition
Q
refining
P
with at most
4k
classes such that the piecewise projection

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}=\|\bm{w}_{Q}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}.

Proof of Lemma
B.4
.

We begin by proving the first inequality. By definition,

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{S,T\subset[0,1]}\bigg\|\int_{S\times T}\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\|_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{S,T\subset[0,1]}\int_{S\times T}\big\|\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\big\|\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\big\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}.

Next, we proceed to prove the second identity.
Notice that for any refinement
Q
of
P
, we have
\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\geq\|\bm{w}_{Q}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
by contractivity. For the inverse direction, let
\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}
with
\|\bm{e}\|_{\mathcal{H}}=1
, and let
S
and
T
be measurable subsets of
[0,1]
that achieve the optimum, i.e.,

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}=\bigg\langle\bm{e},\int_{S\times T}(\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P})(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta\bigg\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.

Let
Q
denote the partition generated by
P
,
S
, and
T
, which has at most
4k
classes. Then

\displaystyle\int_{S\times T}(\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P})(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P})(\xi,\zeta)\mathbbm{1}_{S\times T}(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P})(\xi,\zeta)(\mathbbm{1}_{S\times T})_{Q}(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P})_{Q}(\xi,\zeta)\mathbbm{1}_{S\times T}(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(\bm{w}_{Q}-\bm{w}_{P})(\xi,\zeta)\mathbbm{1}_{S\times T}(\xi,\zeta)\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\zeta.

Hence,
\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\leq\|\bm{w}_{Q}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}
, and we conclude.
∎

With the results above, we can now derive the weak regularity lemma for
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
.

Lemma B.5

(Weak regularity lemma in Hilbert space)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hilbert space.
For every kernel
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
and
k\geq 1
, there exists a partition
P
of
[0,1]
into at most
k
sets with positive measure for which

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\log k}}\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}.

In addition, for every
m
-partition
Q
of
[0,1]
, there is an equipartition
P
with
k
classes that

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\leq 2\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{Q}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}+\frac{2m}{k}\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}.

Proof.

Given any partition
P
, we can choose a refinement
Q
as in Lemma
B.4
. It is easy to check that
(\bm{w}_{Q}-\bm{w}_{P})
is orthogonal to
\bm{w}_{P}
in
L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\bm{w}_{Q}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\|\bm{w}_{Q}-\bm{w}_{P}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\bm{w}_{Q}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}-\|\bm{w}_{P}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}.

Starting with the initial partition
P_{0}=\{\varnothing,[0,1]\}
, let each
P_{m}
be a refinement of
P_{m-1}
as in Lemma
B.4
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}_{P_{m}}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|\bm{w}_{P_{m-1}}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}+\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P_{m-1}}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\dots\geq\|\bm{w}_{P_{0}}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}+\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P_{i}}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

Since
\|\bm{w}_{P_{k}}\|_{L^{2}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}\leq\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}^{2}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}-\bm{w}_{P_{m}}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}.

By our construction, each
P_{m}
has at most
4^{m}
sets. Optimizing
m
for any given
k
, we conclude the first inequality in the lemma.

To prove the last inequality, we partition each class of
Q
into classes of measure
1/k
, with at most one exceptional class of size less than
1/k
. Keeping all classes of size
1/k
, let us take the union of exceptional classes and repartition it into classes of size
1/k
to get a partition
P
.

∎

B.2.
Compactness in unlabeled distance

As a standard approach in graph limit theory, we use the regularity lemma to further prove Lemma
2.29
on the compactness of kernels in the unlabeled distance, which we restate here with
\mathbb{I}=[0,1]
:

Lemma B.6

(
Compactness lemma, restated
)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a Banach space compactly embedded into a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
For any sequence
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
satisfying uniform bound that

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|\bm{w}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})}<w_{\max}<\infty.

up to an extraction of subsequence (which we still index by
n
), there exists
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0.

Moreover, if the sequence
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is defined from
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2})
and
X^{(n)}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{T}
, and
\bm{w}_{n}=\bm{w}_{w^{(n)},X^{(n)}}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})^{\oplus\{0,1\}})
defined as in Definition
2.21
,
there exist limiting
w\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2})
and
X:[0,1]\to\mathbb{T}
that
\bm{w}=\bm{w}_{w,X}
.

Proof of Lemma
B.6
.

For every
n\geq 1
, we can construct partitions
P_{n,m}
of
[0,1]
using the weak regularity lemma such that the corresponding piecewise constant projections
(\bm{w}_{n})_{P_{n,m}}=\vcentcolon\bm{w}_{n,m}
satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

\|\bm{w}_{n}-\bm{w}_{n,m}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\leq\frac{1}{m},

(2)

The partition
P_{n,m+1}
refines
P_{n,m}
,

(3)

|P_{n,m}|\leq k_{m}
, where
k_{m}
depends only on
m
.

Once we have such partitions, we can rearrange the points of
[0,1]
for each fixed
n
by a measure-preserving bijection so that every partition class in every
P_{n,k}
is an interval.

Since
\bm{w}
takes values in
\mathcal{B}_{\leq w_{\max}}
, which is compact in
\mathcal{H}
, we can, by extracting a subsequence indexed by
n
, assume that as
n\to\infty
, the length of the
i
-th interval of
\bm{w}_{n,m}
converges for each
1\leq i\leq k_{m}
and that the values on the product of the
i
-th and
j
-th intervals converge in
\mathcal{H}
for each
1\leq i,j\leq k_{m}
. It follows that as
n\to\infty
,
\bm{w}_{n,m}
converges almost everywhere to a step function
\bm{u}_{m}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
with
k_{m}
steps.

Let
P_{m}
denote the partition of
[0,1]
into the steps of
\bm{u}_{m}
. For every
m<l
, the partition
P_{n,l}
is a refinement of
P_{n,m}
, and therefore
\bm{w}_{n,m}=(\bm{w}_{n,l})_{P_{n,m}}
. It is straightforward that this relation is inherited by the limiting step functions, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{m}=(\bm{u}_{l})_{P_{m}}.

Assuming
\xi,\zeta\in[0,1]
are uniform random variables, the sequence

\displaystyle\big(\langle\bm{e},\bm{u}_{m}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\big)_{m=1}^{\infty}

is a martingale for any
\bm{e}\in\mathcal{H}
. Since these random variables remain bounded as
m\to\infty
, the martingale convergence theorem implies that this sequence converges with probability
1
(almost everywhere on
(\xi,\zeta)\in[0,1]^{2}
).

Then, by the separability of
\mathcal{H}
and the compact embedding
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
, we conclude that
\bm{u}_{m}(\xi,\zeta)
converges almost everywhere on
(\xi,\zeta)\in[0,1]^{2}
. Let
\bm{u}
denote this limit. It satisfies

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{u},\bm{w}_{n})\leq\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{u},\bm{u}_{m})+\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{u}_{m},\bm{w}_{n,m})+\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n,m},\bm{w}_{n}).

Taking the limit as
n\to\infty
and then
m\to\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{u},\bm{w}_{n})=0,

which completes the proof of the limit.

Finally, suppose that the sequence
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is defined from
w^{(n)}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2})
and
X^{(n)}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{T}
. Using a similar argument using the martingale convergence theorem, we can identify the desired limits
w
and
X
such that
\bm{w}=\bm{w}_{w,X}
.

∎

B.3.
Counting Lemma

From this point forward, our estimates will rely more heavily on the compact embedding
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
. We will begin by considering the finite-dimensional case, i.e.
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and then extend to the general case.

This approach considerably simplifies the analysis. However, let us emphasize that in the previous proofs, we intentionally avoided this approach. Our goal is not only to establish the equivalence of convergence but also to identify the limit, so at least one compactness lemma must avoid techniques that rely on approximating compact operators in finite dimensions.

We begin with the extension of Counting Lemma, which is one direction of Lemma
2.30
.

Lemma B.7

(
Counting lemma on compact subspace
)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a Banach space compactly embedded into a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
If
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\cup\{\bm{w}\}\subset L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
satisfy uniform bound that

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|\bm{w}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})}<w_{\max}<\infty

and convergence in unlabeled distance that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0,

then, for all oriented simple graph
F\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=0.

Proof of Lemma
B.7
.

First, note that any measure-preserving rearrangement
\Phi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
does not change the tensorized homomorphism densities. The following identity can be straightforwardly verified:

\displaystyle\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{w}(\Phi(\zeta_{i}),\Phi(\zeta_{j}))\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\Phi(\zeta_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{w}(\Phi(\zeta_{i}),\Phi(\zeta_{j}))\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\zeta_{i}

\displaystyle=\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}^{\Phi}).

Hence, by applying rearrangements, we may assume
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{w}_{n}-\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}=0
, and from this, we proceed to prove the convergence of all
\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})
.

For any
F\in\mathcal{G}
, we assign an order to the edge set
\mathsf{e}(F)
(any order will suffice for our argument). Then, by editing the edges one by one,

\displaystyle\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigg[\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})-\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\bigg]\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigg(\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\bigg)\otimes\big[\bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})-\bm{w}(\xi_{i^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})\big]

\displaystyle\otimes\bigg(\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)}\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\bigg)\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

Let
\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then

\displaystyle\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\quad l\in[d]^{\mathsf{e}(F)}

forms an orthonormal basis of
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}
. For any basis element,

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\ \bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigg(\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\rangle\bigg)

\displaystyle\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}},\bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})-\bm{w}(\xi_{i^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})\rangle

\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)}\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\rangle\bigg)\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

For fixed
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\mathsf{e}(F)
, it is easy to verify that

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)\setminus\{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\}}}\bigg(\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\rangle\bigg)

\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)}\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\rangle\bigg)\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)\setminus\{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq(w_{\max}L_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{H}})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|-1},

where
L_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{H}}
is the operator norm of the canonical embedding
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{H}
. Moreover, this function is of the form
f(\xi_{i^{\prime}})g(\xi_{j^{\prime}})
, so we can use the
L^{\infty}\to L^{1}
operator norm (or equivalently the cut norm, up to a constant
4
) to obtain

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigg(\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\rangle\bigg)

\displaystyle\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}},\bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})-\bm{w}(\xi_{i^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})\rangle

\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)}\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\rangle\bigg)\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 4(w_{\max}L_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{H}})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|-1}\|\bm{w}_{n}-\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}.

Apply the triangle inequality and let
n\to\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\ \bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 4|\mathsf{e}(F)|(w_{\max}L_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{H}})^{|\mathsf{e}(F)|-1}\|\bm{w}_{n}-\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathcal{H}}\to 0,

for any basis element in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}
.
Since it is a space of finite dimension,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=0.

Now, consider the general case of a compact embedding
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
.
By compactness, there exists a sequence of finite-dimensional subspaces
\mathcal{H}_{d}
, with
d\to\infty
, such that for any
\bm{u}\in\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
, the projection
P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{u}-\bm{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}}(d)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Consequently, when
\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})}\leq w_{\max}
,

\displaystyle\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}-\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})}\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H},w_{\max}}(d).

Using the previous triangle editing argument, for all
F\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\bm{t}(F,P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigg(\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i,j)<(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\bigg)

\displaystyle\otimes\big[P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}(\xi_{i^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})-\bm{w}(\xi_{i^{\prime}},\xi_{j^{\prime}})\big]

\displaystyle\otimes\bigg(\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)}\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\bigg)\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

This time we have the stronger norm
L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
bound, so there is no need to take the dual pairing. We can directly conclude that

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(F,P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H},w_{\max},F}(d).

By contractivity, we have, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}_{n},P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})\leq\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})\to 0,

hence the convergence of the tensorized homomorphism densities projected onto finite-dimensional subspaces. By the a priori bound just established (up to a constant
2
that we included in
C^{\downarrow}
),

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H},w_{\max},F}(d).

Taking the limit as
d\to\infty
, we conclude.
∎

B.4.
Inverse Counting Lemma

Next, we extend the inverse counting lemma, which provides the other direction of Lemma
2.30
.

As before, we start with the case
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The convenience of this choice is obvious, as illustrated in the following lemma.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\leq\sum_{l\in[d]}\|\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle\|_{\square}\leq d\|\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}.

Proof of Lemma
B.8
.

The proof follows directly from the definitions and the bounds between the
\ell^{1}
and
\ell^{2}
norms in the finite-dimensional space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

∎

Following the approach of
[
28
]
, we use several probabilistic techniques by interpreting
\xi\in[0,1]
as a uniform random variable. The aim is not to reproduce rates comparable to those in the literature. To this end, we introduce the following notion.

Definition B.9
.

If there exists a priori
\epsilon(k),r(k)\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, such that for a sequence of random variables
Y_{k}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(|Y_{k}|\leq r(k)\big)\geq 1-\epsilon(k),

we say that

\displaystyle Y_{k}\to 0

in probability with a priori rates
\epsilon(k)
and
r(k)
.

This notion will be used to replace the explicit rate computations in
[
28
]
, as the most important consideration (when we do not need to compute the exact rate) is the existence of some uniform rate of convergence.

The following definition of sampling is the starting point of the probabilistic argument.

Definition B.10

(
Sampling graphs from kernels
)
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{H})
, where
\mathcal{H}
is a Hilbert space. For a tuple
\xi^{\oplus k}=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k})\in[0,1]^{k}
, define the
k\times k
matrix
\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}]
with entries in
\mathcal{H}
as

\displaystyle\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}](i,j)\vcentcolon=\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j}),\quad\forall\,i,j=1,\dots,k.

When considering
\xi^{\oplus k}
as i.i.d. random variables, the sampling defined in Definition
B.10
effectively approximates the kernel in terms of cut distance. For scalar-valued kernels
w\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathbb{R})
, the following result is commonly referred to as the First Sampling Lemma.

Lemma B.11

(
First Sampling Lemma, scalar-valued
)
.

There exists a priori rates
\epsilon(k),r(k)\to 0
, such that for any kernel
w:[0,1]^{2}\to[-1,1]
and
\xi^{\oplus k}
being uniformly random on
[0,1]^{k}
, as
k\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\|w[\xi^{\oplus k}]\|_{\square}-\|w\|_{\square}\to 0

in probability with such rates.

Proof of Lemma
B.11
.

We refer readers to Lemma 10.6 in
[
28
]
for more details. Although the lemma there is stated specifically for symmetric kernels, the proof itself does not fundamentally rely on the assumption of symmetry and can be readily adapted to non-symmetric kernels.

∎

Next, we will use the technique of approximating kernels by random (oriented) simple graphs. Since the weights will be interpreted as probability densities of discrete random variables, it is convenient to assume non-negativity and that their summation is bounded by
1
. In what follows, we will complete the proof for
\bm{w}:[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1/2d]^{d}
and then extend the result to the general case.

Definition B.12

(
Random oriented simple graph
)
.

Let
\bm{H}
be a
k\times k
matrix with entries
\bm{H}(i,j)\in[0,1/(2d)]^{d}
. Define a random graph where edge weights almost surely take values in the orthonormal basis
\{\bm{0},\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(\bm{H})(i,j)\vcentcolon=\sum_{l\in[d]}\bm{e}_{l}\mathbbm{1}_{[\frac{l-1}{2d},\frac{l-1}{2d}+\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{H}(i,j)\rangle)}(z_{ij}),\quad\forall i,j=1,\dots,k,\ i\neq j

where
(z_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k}
are i.i.d. random variables on
[0,1]
, and
z_{ji}=1-z_{ij}
.

Define the random
k
-induced subgraph for a kernel
\bm{w}:[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1/d]^{d}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})(i,j)\vcentcolon=\mathbb{G}(\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}])(i,j)=\sum_{l\in[d]}\bm{e}_{l}\mathbbm{1}_{[\frac{l-1}{d},\frac{l-1}{d}+\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\rangle)}(z_{ij}),

where
(\xi_{i})_{1\leq i\leq k}
and
(z_{ij})_{1\leq i<j\leq k}
are i.i.d. random variables on
[0,1]
, and
z_{ji}=1-z_{ij}
.

By our definition, at most one of
\mathbb{G}(\bm{H})(i,j)
and
\mathbb{G}(\bm{H})(j,i)
is nonzero, and there is at most one nonzero entry in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma B.13
.

There exists a priori rates
\epsilon(k),r(k)\to 0
, such that for any
k\times k
matrix
\bm{H}_{k}
with entry values in
[0,1/2d]^{d}
, as
k\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{G}(\bm{H}_{k})-\bm{H}_{k}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to 0

in probability with such rates.

A quick consequence of the lemma is the following corollary:

Corollary B.14
.

There exists a priori rates
\epsilon(k),r(k)\to 0
, such that for any kernel
\bm{w}:[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1/2d]^{d}
, as
k\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})-\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}]\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to 0

in probability with such rates.

Proof of Lemma
B.13
.

It suffices to consider
\bm{H}_{k}
with zero diagonal entries. Indeed, let
\bm{H}_{k}^{\setminus\Delta}
denote the matrix with zero diagonals and the same off-diagonal entries as
\bm{H}_{k}
, then

\displaystyle\|\bm{H}_{k}^{\setminus\Delta}-\bm{H}_{k}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\leq\frac{1}{k},

which converges to zero.

Now, let
S,T\subset\{1,\dots,k\}
. By definition,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{G}(\bm{H}_{k})-\bm{H}_{k}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}=\max_{S,T}\bigg\|\sum_{i\in S,j\in T}\frac{\mathbb{G}(\bm{H}_{k})(i,j)-\bm{H}_{k}(i,j)}{k^{2}}\bigg\|_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}.

For fixed
S,T
and
\bm{e}_{l}
chosen from
\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}
the orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, notice that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in S,j\in T}\frac{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\mathbb{G}(\bm{H}_{k})(i,j)-\bm{H}_{k}(i,j)\rangle}{k^{2}}

is the sum of at most
k(k-1)
independent random variables (the pairs
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
are correlated, so we count each such pair as a single variable). The oscillation caused by each variable is
1/k^{2}
.

By applying Azuma-Bernstein-Chernoff-Hoeffding inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sum_{i\in S,j\in T}\frac{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\mathbb{G}(\bm{H}_{k})(i,j)-\bm{H}_{k}(i,j)\rangle}{k^{2}}\leq r\bigg)\geq 1-e^{-k^{2}r^{2}/2}.

Note that there are at most
4^{k}
choices of
S,T\subset\{1,\dots,k\}
and
d
directions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\|\mathbb{G}(\bm{H}_{k})-\bm{H}_{k}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\leq dr\bigg)\geq 1-4^{k}de^{-k^{2}r^{2}/2}.

By optimizing over
r
for fixed
k
and then passing the limit
k\to\infty
, we obtain convergence in probability with a priori rates.
∎

We next extend the Second Sampling Lemma to apply to our new definition.

Lemma B.15

(
Second Sampling Lemma, vector-valued
)
.

There exists a priori rates
\epsilon(k),r(k)
, such that for any kernel
\bm{w}:[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1/2d]^{d}
, as
k\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w}),\bm{w})\to 0

in probability with such rates.

Proof of Lemma
B.15
.

Firstly, notice that

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w}),\bm{w})\leq\|\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})-\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}]\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}+\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}],\bm{w}).

By Corollary
B.14
, we have
\|\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})-\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}]\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to 0
in probability with a priori rates. Therefore, it suffices to investigate
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}],\bm{w})
.

Let
m=\lceil k^{1/4}\rceil
. By Lemma
B.5
, there exists an equipartition
P=\{V_{1},\dots,V_{m}\}
of
[0,1]
into
m
sets such that

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}_{P}-\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\leq\frac{8}{\sqrt{\log k}}.

Then

(B.1)

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}],\bm{w})

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}],\bm{w}_{P})+\|\bm{w}_{P}-\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}+\|\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}]-\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}]\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}},

where the middle term
\|\bm{w}_{P}-\bm{w}\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to 0
by our construction of
P
. Moreover, the last term can be expanded as

\displaystyle\big\|\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}]-\bm{w}[\xi^{\oplus k}]\big\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{d}\big\|\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{P}\rangle[\xi^{\oplus k}]-\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle[\xi^{\oplus k}]\big\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{d}\bigg[\big\|\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{P}\rangle-\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle\big\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}

\displaystyle+\Big(\big\|\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{P}\rangle[\xi^{\oplus k}]-\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle[\xi^{\oplus k}]\big\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\big\|\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{P}\rangle-\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle\big\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big)\bigg].

By our construction of
P
, we have
\|\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{P}\rangle-\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to 0
in probability with a priori rates. In addition,

\displaystyle\big\|\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{P}\rangle[\xi^{\oplus k}]-\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle[\xi^{\oplus k}]\big\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}-\big\|\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{P}\rangle-\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle\big\|_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}\to 0

in probability with a priori rates also holds, by Lemma
B.11
. (Applying Lemma
B.11
is the main reason for decomposing into components.)

It now remains to check the first term in (
B.1
), namely
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}],\bm{w}_{P})
.
Taking the piecewise projection of
\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}]
, we see that
\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}]
and
\bm{w}_{P}
, as kernels on
[0,1]
, are almost identical: both are step functions with
m=\lceil k^{1/4}\rceil
, having the same values on each step. The only difference is that the measure of the
i
-th step in
\bm{w}_{P}
is
1/m
, whereas the measure of the
i
-th step in
\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}]
is

\displaystyle|V_{i}\cap\{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k}\}|/k,

which is expected to be close to
1/m
. Take

\displaystyle r_{i}\vcentcolon=1/m-|V_{i}\cap\{\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k}\}|/k.

It is straightforward to check that

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}],\bm{w}_{P})\leq 4\sqrt{d}\sum_{i=1}^{m}|r_{i}|.

By applying Azuma-Bernstein-Chernoff-Hoeffding inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(|r_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{m\sqrt{\log k}}\bigg)\geq 1-2e^{-2k/m^{2}\log k},

and thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{P}[\xi^{\oplus k}],\bm{w}_{P})\leq\frac{4\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{\log k}}\bigg)\geq 1-2me^{-2k/m^{2}\log k}.

Recalling that
m=\lceil k^{1/4}\rceil
, we obtain convergence in probability with a priori rates.

Combining the analysis of all terms, we conclude convergence in probability with a priori rates (potentially at a slower rate, though this is not a concern here).
∎

The convergence in probability described above can be used to establish deterministic results through the inclusion-exclusion principle.

Lemma B.16
.

Let
\epsilon(k),r(k)
be the a priori rates in Lemma
B.15
. If

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{F}}\Big|\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{u})=\bm{F})-\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})=\bm{F})\Big|<1-2\epsilon(k),

where
\bm{F}
ranges over all graphs with
k
nodes and edge values in
\{\bm{0},\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that for each pair
i\neq j
, at most one of
\bm{F}(i,j)
and
\bm{F}(j,i)
is nonzero, and there is at most one nonzero entry, then

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{u},\bm{w})\leq 2r(k).

Proof of Lemma
B.16
.

The assumption implies that we can couple
\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{u})
and
\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{u})=\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})\bigg)>2\epsilon(k)

By Lemma
B.15
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg(\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{u}),\bm{u})+\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w}),\bm{w})\leq r(k)\bigg)\geq 1-2\epsilon(k).

Thus, there exists a positive probability that these events occur together, which implies

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{u},\bm{w})\leq\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{u}),\bm{u})+\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w}),\bm{w})\leq 2r_{k}.

∎

We are now ready to prove the Inverse Counting Lemma for kernels
\bm{w}:[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1/(2d)]^{d}
.

Lemma B.17

(
Inverse Counting Lemma, positive finite
)
.

If
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\cup\{\bm{w}\}
are
[0,1]^{2}\to[0,1/(2d)]^{d}
kernels and have all tensorized homomorphism densities converging, i.e. for all
F\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=0,

then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0.

Proof of Lemma
B.17
.

Let
k
be an arbitrary integer and let
\bm{F}
be a graph with
k
nodes and edge values in
\{\bm{0},\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that for each pair
i\neq j
, at most one of
\bm{F}(i,j)
and
\bm{F}(j,i)
is nonzero, and there is at most one nonzero entry. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})=\bm{F})

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{v}(F),\bm{F}(i,j)=\bm{F}(j,i)=\bm{0}}\Big(1-\langle\bm{e}_{1}+\dots+\bm{e}_{d},\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})+\bm{w}(\xi_{j},\xi_{i})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big)

\displaystyle\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{v}(F),\bm{F}(i,j)\neq\bm{0}}\langle\bm{F}(i,j),\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

Expanding the products by the distributive property, it is straightforward to verify that each term should be a linear functional applied to some
\bm{t}(F^{\prime},\bm{w})
,
F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
with
|\mathsf{v}(F^{\prime})|=|\mathsf{v}(F)|=k
. Hence, for any fixed
k\geq 1
, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\Big|\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w}_{n})=\bm{F})-\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})=\bm{F})\Big|=0

for all possible choices of
\bm{F}
. Therefore, by definition,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{\bm{F}}\Big|\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w}_{n})=\bm{F})-\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{G}(k,\bm{w})=\bm{F})\Big|

By Lemma
B.16
, we obtain

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})\leq 2r(k),

where
r(k)
is the a priori rates in Lemma
B.15
.
Taking the limit as
k\to\infty
, we conclude the lemma.

∎

Finally, we proceed with the Inverse Counting Lemma on
L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
.

Lemma B.18

(
Inverse Counting lemma on compact subspace
)
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a Banach space compactly embedded into a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
.
If
\{\bm{w}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\cup\{\bm{w}\}\subset L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
satisfy uniform bound that

\displaystyle\sup_{n}\|\bm{w}_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})}<w_{\max}<\infty

and have all tensorized homomorphism densities converging, i.e. for all
F\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=0,

then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0.

Proof of Lemma
B.18
.

We begin by assuming
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The strategy is to make Lemma
B.17
applicable. Define

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{n}(\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{w}_{n}(\xi,\zeta)}{4dw_{\max}}+\frac{1}{4d}\sum_{l\in[d]}\bm{e}_{l},

\displaystyle\forall(\xi,\zeta)\in[0,1]^{2},\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\bm{u}(\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)}{4dw_{\max}}+\frac{1}{4d}\sum_{l\in[d]}\bm{e}_{l},

\displaystyle\forall(\xi,\zeta)\in[0,1]^{2}.

It is straightforward to see that
\bm{u}_{n}
and
\bm{u}
take values in
[0,1/(2d)]^{d}
, and by checking the definition,

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=\frac{1}{4dw_{\max}}\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{u}_{n},\bm{u}).

To verify the convergence
\bm{t}(F,\bm{u}_{n})\to\bm{t}(F,\bm{u})
, note that for any
F\in\mathcal{G}
and any basis element
\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}}
,

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\ \bm{t}(F,\bm{u}_{n})\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\big\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\ \bm{u}_{n}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big\rangle\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bigg(\frac{\big\langle\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\ \bm{w}_{n}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\big\rangle}{4dw_{\max}}+\frac{1}{4d}\bigg)\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

Expanding the products by the distributive property, it is straightforward to verify that each term is a linear functional applied to some
\bm{t}(F^{\prime},\bm{w}_{n})
, where
F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}
with
|\mathsf{v}(F^{\prime})|=|\mathsf{v}(F)|
. Hence,
\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})
implies
\bm{t}(F,\bm{u}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{u})
.

Applying Lemma
B.17
, we obtain
\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{u}_{n},\bm{u})=0
, which in turn implies
\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0
.

Now, consider the general case of a compact embedding
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
.
By compactness, there exists a sequence of finite-dimensional subspaces
\mathcal{H}_{d}
, with
d\to\infty
, such that for any
\bm{u}\in\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
, the projection
P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{u}-\bm{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}}(d)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Consequently, when
\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})}\leq w_{\max}
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w},\bm{w})\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H},w_{\max}}(d).

By contractivity

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(F,P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}

\displaystyle=\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}(\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{t}(F,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}.

This suggests the convergence of the tensorized homomorphism densities, hence the convergence of the unlabeled distance, when projected onto finite-dimensional subspaces.
Notice that

\displaystyle\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}_{d}}(P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}_{n},P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})+\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w}_{n})+\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w},\bm{w})

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{\square;\mathcal{H}_{d}}(P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}_{n},P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})+C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H},w_{\max}}(d).

We conclude by passing the limit first to
n
and then to
d
.

∎

We conclude this section by proving Lemma
2.30
.

Proof of Lemma
2.30
.

The result follows directly by combining Lemma
B.7
and Lemma
B.18
.
∎

Appendix C
Fractional isomorphism and tree homomorphism densities

The objective of this section is to prove Lemma
2.31
. We begin with the finite-dimensional case
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, spanned by the orthonormal basis
\bm{e}_{1},\dots,\bm{e}_{d}
, and follow the roadmap outlined in
[
18
]
. We then generalize the result to
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
.

As in Appendix
B
, probabilistic techniques are used. However, while the primary focus in Appendix
B
was on martingales and their concentration properties, the main tools in this section are Borel algebras and conditional expectations. In this section, we will explicitly specify the Borel algebra and the measure when discussing standard probability spaces. Accordingly, we denote the measures by
\mu
and
\nu
. Note that this usage of
\mu
and
\nu
differs from their usage elsewhere in the article.

C.1.
Invariant subspace and sub-
\sigma
-algebra

We begin with a critical lemma in the
L^{2}
space, which we will later use to construct sub-
\sigma
-algebras on standard probability space
\mathbb{I}
. It is worth noting that this lemma exists in
[
18
]
, but only in the context of symmetric kernels. This extension to the general case is essential for our argument, although it is neither surprising nor particularly difficult.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space. Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and let
V\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{I})
be a closed linear subspace. Then the following are equivalent:

•

For any
\bm{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
V
is
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}
-invariant and
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}^{T}\rangle}
-invariant.

•

For any
\bm{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}
and
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}^{T}\rangle}
commute with the projection
P_{V}
.

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

It suffices to prove the case of scalar-valued
w\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I})
, as the general case can be handled by applying the result for any
\bm{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let us begin by considering
w
symmetric. In this case,
T_{w}
is a self-adjoint operator, and the result follows as a standard application of the compact self-adjoint operator theory.

For the non-symmetric case, define the direct sum space
V\oplus V\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{I})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{I})
. It is straightforward to verify that the projection
P_{V\oplus V}
satisfies
P_{V\oplus V}=P_{V}\oplus P_{V}
.
If
P_{V}
commutes with both
T_{w}
and
T_{w^{T}}
, then the following identity is true:

(C.1)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&T_{w^{T}}\\
T_{w}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}P_{V}&0\\
0&P_{V}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&T_{w^{T}}\circ P_{V}\\
T_{w}\circ P_{V}&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&P_{V}\circ T_{w^{T}}\\
P_{V}\circ T_{w}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}P_{V}&0\\
0&P_{V}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&T_{w^{T}}\\
T_{w}&0\end{pmatrix}.

By the symmetric case, this implies the invariance of
V\oplus V
under the off-diagonal block matrix representation of
T_{w}
and
T_{w^{T}}
, and hence the invariance of
V
under each of
T_{w}
and
T_{w^{T}}
.

Conversely, if
V
is invariant under both
T_{w}
and
T_{w^{T}}
, then
V\oplus V
is invariant under the off-diagonal block matrix representation of
T_{w}
and
T_{w^{T}}
. This gives (
C.1
) and ensures that
P_{V}
commutes with both
T_{w}
and
T_{w^{T}}
.

∎

Next, we define the
\bm{w}
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebra. The motivation, heuristically, is to group points that cannot be distinguished through operations involving
\bm{w}
or
\bm{w}^{T}
. For further discussion, see
[
18
]
.

Definition C.2
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space, and let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. A sub-
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{C}\subset\mathscr{B}
is said to be
\bm{w}
-invariant if, for any
\mathscr{C}
-measurable function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{C})
and any
\bm{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, both
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}(f)
and
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}^{T}\rangle}(f)
belong to
L^{2}(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{C})
. That is, these transforms are not only
\mathscr{B}
-measurable but also
\mathscr{C}
-measurable.

The following supplementary definition ensures the consistency of the concept of null sets:

Definition C.3
.

We say that
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{B}
is a relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathscr{B}
if it is a sub-
\sigma
-algebra and if
Z\in\mathscr{C}
whenever there exists
Z_{0}\in\mathscr{C}
such that
\mu(Z\setminus Z_{0})=\mu(Z_{0}\setminus Z)=0
.

Next, we define canonical projections and injections in the presence of a relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra, which are closely related to conditional expectation.

Definition C.4
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra.
Define the canonical injection and projection

\displaystyle I_{\mathscr{C}}

\displaystyle:L^{2}(\mathbb{I}/\mathscr{C})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{I}),

\displaystyle P_{\mathscr{C}}

\displaystyle:L^{2}(\mathbb{I})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{I}/\mathscr{C}).

It follows that
(P_{\mathscr{C}}\circ I_{\mathscr{C}})
is the identity map on
L^{2}(\mathbb{I}/\mathscr{C})
, and
(I_{\mathscr{C}}\circ P_{\mathscr{C}})
is the conditional expectation operator
\mathbb{E}(\cdot|\mathscr{C})
.

These operations can be applied to
\bm{w}
when it is viewed as a Lebesgue function on
\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
.

Definition C.5
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\bm{w}
-invariant and relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra.
Define

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\mathbb{E}(\bm{w}|\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

\displaystyle\bm{w}/\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=(\bm{w}\circ I_{\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}})\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}/\mathscr{C}\times\mathbb{I}/\mathscr{C};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

However, we do not need to take the conditional expectation on both dimensions. This is stated in the following proposition.

Proposition C.6
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\bm{w}
-invariant and relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra.
The following holds:

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{w}|\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C})=\mathbb{E}(\bm{w}|\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{C}).

Proof of Proposition
C.6
.

The proof is a standard application of conditional expectation in probability theory. We refer to the discussion around Claim 5.7 in
[
18
]
for details.

∎

The next proposition states that taking the conditional expectation with respect to a
\bm{w}
-invariant and relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra preserves certain properties of
\bm{w}
.

Proposition C.7
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space, and let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\bm{w}
-invariant and relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathscr{B}
. Then:

(1)

\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}},\bm{w}/\mathscr{C})=0
.

(2)

T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}/\mathscr{C}\rangle}\circ P_{\mathscr{C}}=P_{\mathscr{C}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}
and
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}^{T}/\mathscr{C}\rangle}\circ P_{\mathscr{C}}=P_{\mathscr{C}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}^{T}\rangle}
for any
\bm{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the proposition essentially replicates the proof of Proposition 5.9 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Proposition
C.7
.

To verify (1), we proceed directly by definition.

For (2), it is straightforward to check that, for example,
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}/\mathscr{C}\rangle}\circ P_{\mathscr{C}}=P_{\mathscr{C}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}\rangle}
. By Lemma
C.1
, the
\bm{w}
-invariance of
\mathscr{C}
implies that the projection operator
\mathbb{E}(\cdot|\mathscr{C}):L^{2}(\mathbb{I})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{I})
commutes with
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}
, so

\displaystyle T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}/\mathscr{C}\rangle}\circ P_{\mathscr{C}}

\displaystyle=P_{\mathscr{C}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}\rangle}

\displaystyle=P_{\mathscr{C}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}\circ\mathbb{E}(\cdot|\mathscr{C})

\displaystyle=P_{\mathscr{C}}\circ\mathbb{E}(\cdot|\mathscr{C})\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}

\displaystyle=P_{\mathscr{C}}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle},

where the second equality follows from Proposition
C.6
.
∎

We now consider the construction of
\bm{w}
-invariant and relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebras. To achieve this, we first introduce the following auxiliary definition.

Definition C.8
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{E}
be relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathscr{B}
. We say that
(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{E})
is a
\bm{w}
-invariant pair if, for all
\bm{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}(L^{2}(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{D}))

\displaystyle\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{E}),

\displaystyle T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}^{T}\rangle}(L^{2}(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{D}))

\displaystyle\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{E}).

Note that
\mathscr{C}
is
\bm{w}
-invariant if and only if
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
is a
\bm{w}
-invariant pair. Given
\mathscr{D}
, define
\Phi
as the collection of all
\mathscr{E}
such that
(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{E})
is a
\bm{w}
-invariant pair. Then
\Phi
is non-empty because
\mathscr{B}\in\Phi
. We define

m(\mathscr{D})\vcentcolon=\{Z\in\mathscr{B}:\forall\mathscr{E}\in\Phi,Z\in\mathscr{E}\}.

It is straightforward to verify that for any relatively complete
\mathscr{D}
, the
\sigma
-algebra
m(\mathscr{D})
is relatively complete and
(\mathscr{D},m(\mathscr{D}))
forms a
\bm{w}
-invariant pair.

Definition C.9

(
Canonical sequence
\{\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
)
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space, and let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Define
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{\bm{w}}\vcentcolon=\langle\{\varnothing,\mathbb{I}\}\rangle
and, inductively,
\mathscr{C}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}\vcentcolon=m(\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}})
. Furthermore, define

\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})\vcentcolon=\left\langle\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}\right\rangle.

The canonical sequence generates the minimum
\bm{w}
-invariant relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra.

Proposition C.10
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space, and let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})
is the minimum
\bm{w}
-invariant relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathscr{B}
.

Proof of Proposition
C.10
.

We refer to the proof of Proposition 5.13 in
[
18
]
.

∎

C.2.
Iterated degree of measures

We next adapt the definition of the iterated degree of measures from
[
18
]
to the multi-dimensional, non-symmetric case.

Definition C.11
.

Let
P^{0}=\{\star\}
denote the one-point space, and define inductively

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{n}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i\leq n}P^{i},\quad\text{and}\quad P^{n+1}=\big(\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{M}_{n})\big)^{2d}

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We set
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{\infty}=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}P^{n}
and denote by
p_{n,k}:\mathbb{M}_{k}\to\mathbb{M}_{n}
the canonical projection, where
n\leq k\leq\infty
.

Define

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}=\left\{\alpha\in\mathbb{M}:\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\forall 1\leq l\leq 2d,\,\alpha(n+1)(l)=(p_{n,n+1})_{\#}\big[\alpha(n+2)(l)\big]\right\},

where
(p_{n,n+1})_{\#}\alpha(n+2)(l)\in(\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{M}_{n}))^{2d}
denotes the push-forward of
\alpha(n+2)(l)
via
p_{n,n+1}
.

It follows from Kolmogorov’s Existence Theorem that for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{P}
, there exists a unique measure
\mu_{\alpha}\in(\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{M}))^{2d}
such that

(p_{n,\infty})_{\ast}\mu_{\alpha}=\alpha(n+1)

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In fact, we have the following uniform version.

Proposition C.12
.

The set
\mathbb{P}
is closed in
\mathbb{M}
, and the map
\alpha\mapsto\mu_{\alpha}
, which satisfies

(p_{n,\infty})_{\ast}\mu_{\alpha}=\alpha(n+1)

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is a continuous map from
\mathbb{P}
to
(\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{M}))^{2d}
.

Hence, we have the following definition, adapted from the notion of distribution on iterated degree measures in
[
18
]
.

Definition C.13
.

We say that
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{M})
is a
2d
-dimensional distribution on iterated degree measures, or a
2d
-DIDM, if:

(1)

\nu(\mathbb{P})=1
,

(2)

\mu_{\alpha}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu
, with the corresponding Radon-Nikodym derivative satisfying
\frac{d\mu_{\alpha}}{d\nu}\in[-1,1]^{2d}
for
\nu
-almost every
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
.

We now construct
\nu_{\bm{w}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{M})
from our kernel
\bm{w}
.

Definition C.14
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space, and let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We define
i_{\bm{w},0}:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{M}_{0}=\{\star\}
as the constant map. Inductively, define

\displaystyle i_{\bm{w},n+1}:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{M}_{n+1}=\textstyle\prod_{j\leq n+1}P^{j}

such that:

(1)

For every
j\leq n
,
i_{\bm{w},n+1}(\xi)(j)=i_{\bm{w},n}(\xi)(j)
.

(2)

When
j=n+1
, for
1\leq l\leq 2d
and any Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{M}_{n}
,

i_{\bm{w},n+1}(\xi)(n+1)(l)(A)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A)}\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)&&\text{if }1\leq l\leq d,\\
&\int_{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A)}\langle\bm{e}_{l-d},\bm{w}^{T}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)&&\text{if }d+1\leq l\leq 2d.\end{aligned}\right.

Define

\displaystyle i_{\bm{w}}:\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{M}=\textstyle\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}P^{n}

as the unique map such that
\forall\xi\in\mathbb{I},n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)(n)=i_{\bm{w},n}(\xi)(n).

Finally, define
\nu_{\bm{w}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{M})
as the pushforward measure of
\mu
by
i_{\bm{w}}
