, i.e.,

\displaystyle\nu_{\bm{w}}(A)=\int_{i_{\bm{w}}^{-1}(A)}\mathrm{d}\mu(\xi).

When constructing
\nu_{\bm{w}}
iteratively through
i_{\bm{w},n}
, the canonical sequence
\{\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
from Definition
C.9
is reproduced. We state this in detail as the following proposition.

Proposition C.15
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space, and let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The maps
i_{\bm{w},n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, are measurable, and

\Big\langle\Big\{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A):A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n})\Big\}\Big\rangle=\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}},

i.e., the minimum relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathscr{B}
that makes the map
i_{\bm{w},n}
measurable is
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the proposition essentially replicates the proof of Proposition 6.6 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Proposition
C.15
.

It is clear that the claim holds for
n=0
because
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{\bm{w}}=\langle\{\varnothing,\mathbb{I}\}\rangle=\langle\{i_{\bm{w},0}^{-1}(\varnothing),i_{\bm{w},0}^{-1}(\{\star\})\}\rangle
.
Suppose that the claim holds for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows from Theorem 17.24 of
[
23
]
and the definition of
\mathbb{M}_{n+1}
that the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n+1})
is generated by the sets
\{p_{n,n+1}^{-1}(A):A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n})\}
and the maps

\mathbb{M}_{n+1}\ni\kappa\mapsto\sum_{l=1}^{2d}\int_{\mathbb{M}_{n}}f_{l}\,\mathrm{d}\kappa(n+1)(l)\in\mathbb{R},

where for each
1\leq l\leq 2d
,
f_{l}:\mathbb{M}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a bounded Borel function.

Let
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n})
. Then, by the inductive hypothesis, we have

i_{\bm{w},n+1}^{-1}(p_{n,n+1}^{-1}(A))=i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A)\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}\subset\mathscr{C}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}.

For each
1\leq l\leq 2d
, let
f_{l}:\mathbb{M}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded Borel function. Then the map

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}\ni\xi

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{l=1}^{2d}\int_{\mathbb{M}_{n}}f_{l}\ \big(i_{\bm{w},n+1}(\xi)(n+1)(l)\big)_{\#}(\mathrm{d}\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{I}}\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle(f_{l}\circ i_{\bm{w},n})(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=d+1}^{2d}\int_{\mathbb{I}}\langle\bm{e}_{l-d},\bm{w}^{T}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle(f_{l}\circ i_{\bm{w},n})(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)

is
\mathscr{C}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}
-measurable by the definition and the inductive hypothesis (that
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}
is the minimum sub-
\sigma
-algebra making
i_{\bm{w},n}
measurable). This shows that
i_{\bm{w},n+1}
is measurable.

Let
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}
denote the minimum relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra that makes
\mathscr{C}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}
measurable; then by definition,
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}\subset\mathscr{C}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}
. It remains to show that
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}=\mathscr{C}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}
.

For
A\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}
, we find
B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n})
such that
\mu(A\setminus i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(B))=\mu(i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(B)\setminus A)=0
by the inductive hypothesis. Then we see that the function

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}

\displaystyle\ni\xi\mapsto\int_{B}\ \big(i_{\bm{w},n+1}(\xi)(n+1)(l)\big)_{\#}(\mathrm{d}\mu)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{I}}\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\mathbbm{1}_{A}(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)=T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle}(\mathbbm{1}_{A})(\xi)&&\text{ if }1\leq l\leq d\\
&\int_{\mathbb{I}}\langle\bm{e}_{l-d},\bm{w}^{T}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\mathbbm{1}_{A}(\zeta)\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)=T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l-d},\bm{w}^{T}\rangle}(\mathbbm{1}_{A})(\xi)&&\text{ if }d+1\leq l\leq 2d\end{aligned}\right.

is
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}
-measurable. It is straightforward to check that
\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}
is the minimum relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra that makes
T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}\rangle}(\mathbbm{1}_{A})
and
T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l-d},\bm{w}^{T}\rangle}(\mathbbm{1}_{A})
measurable, where
1\leq l\leq 2d
and
A\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}
.

Consequently,
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}=\mathscr{C}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}
, and the proof is complete.
∎

The following corollary is then straightforward:

Corollary C.16
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space, and let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The map
i_{\bm{w}}
is measurable, and

\Big\langle\Big\{i_{\bm{w}}^{-1}(A):A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M})\Big\}\Big\rangle=\mathscr{C}^{\bm{w}},

i.e., the minimum relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathscr{B}
that makes the map
i_{\bm{w}}
measurable is
\mathscr{C}^{\bm{w}}
.

We now show that
\nu_{\bm{w}}
is a
2d
-DIDM.

Proposition C.17
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{B},\mu)
be a standard probability space, and let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the measure
\nu_{\bm{w}}
is a
2d
-DIDM, and
i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)\in\mathbb{P}
for every
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the proposition essentially replicates the proof of Proposition 6.8 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Proposition
C.17
.

First, we show that
i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)\in\mathbb{P}
for every
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
. This immediately implies that
\nu_{\bm{w}}(\mathbb{P})=1
. Let
A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n})
and
1\leq l\leq d
. We have, by the definition of
i_{\bm{w}}
,

\displaystyle i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)(n+1)(l)(A)=i_{\bm{w},n+1}(\xi)(n+1)(l)(A)

\displaystyle=\int_{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A)}\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)=\int_{i_{\bm{w},n+1}^{-1}(p_{n,n+1}^{-1}(A))}\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)

\displaystyle=i_{\bm{w},n+2}(\xi)(n+2)(l)(p_{n,n+1}^{-1}(A))=i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)(n+2)(l)(p_{n,n+1}^{-1}(A))

\displaystyle=(p_{n,n+1})_{\#}\big[i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)(n+2)(l)\big](A).

This shows that
i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)\in\mathbb{P}
for any
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
. The argument is essentially the same for
d+1\leq l\leq 2d
, replacing
\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle
with
\langle\bm{e}_{l-d},\bm{w}^{T}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle
.

Let
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
, and write
\mu_{\xi}=\mu_{i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)}\in(\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{M}))^{2d}
. It follows from Corollary
C.16
and Corollary E.2 in
[
18
]
, that there are functions
g_{\xi}
and
g_{\xi}^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{M}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)|\mathscr{C}(\bm{w}))

\displaystyle=g_{\xi}(i_{\bm{w}}(\zeta)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\bm{w}^{T}(\xi,\zeta)|\mathscr{C}(\bm{w}))

\displaystyle=g_{\xi}^{\prime}(i_{\bm{w}}(\zeta)),

holds for
\mu
-a.e.
\zeta\in\mathbb{I}
.

We show that
(g_{\xi},g_{\xi}^{\prime})
is the Radon–Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\xi}}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}}}
(for
\nu_{\bm{w}}
-almost every
i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)\in\mathbb{M}
). To do this, let
A\in\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mu_{\xi}(l)(p_{n,\infty}^{-1}(A))=i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)(n+1)(l)(A)

\displaystyle=\int_{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A)}\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)=\int_{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A)}\Big\langle\bm{e}_{l},\mathbb{E}\big(\bm{w}(\xi,\cdot)|\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}})(\zeta)\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int_{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A)}\Big\langle\bm{e}_{l},\mathbb{E}\big(\bm{w}(\xi,\cdot)|\mathscr{C}(\bm{w}))(\zeta)\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)=\int_{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(A)}\Big\langle\bm{e}_{l},g_{\xi}(i_{\bm{w}}(\zeta))\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int_{i_{\bm{w}}^{-1}(p_{n,\infty}^{-1}(A))}\Big\langle\bm{e}_{l},g_{\xi}(i_{\bm{w}}(\zeta))\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\int_{p_{n,\infty}^{-1}(A)}\Big\langle\bm{e}_{l},g_{\xi}(\cdot)\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}}.

The rest follows from the fact that
\mu_{\xi}
and
\nu_{\bm{w}}
are well-defined and that

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\big\{p_{n,\infty}^{-1}(A):A\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n})\big\}

generates
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M})
.
∎

The next proposition illustrates how to define a kernel from a
2d
-DIDM using the Radon-Nikodym derivative.

Proposition C.18
.

Let
\nu
be a
2d
-DIDM. Then there exists
\bm{U}[\nu]\in L^{\infty}\big((\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M},\nu\times\nu);\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
such that for
1\leq l\leq d
,

\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{U}[\nu](\alpha,\cdot)\rangle=\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha}}{\mathrm{d}\nu}\bigg)(l)

for
\nu
-almost every
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the proposition essentially replicates the proof of Claim 6.9 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Proposition
C.18
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M})
and define
A_{\alpha}=\{\beta\in\mathbb{M}:(\alpha,\beta)\in A\}
. Then the assignment

\mathbb{M}\ni\alpha\mapsto\mu_{\alpha}(l)(A_{\alpha})\in[0,1]

is defined for all
1\leq l\leq 2d
and
\nu
-almost everywhere, and it follows from Proposition
C.12
that this assignment is measurable. This allows us to compute

\Phi(A)=\int_{\mathbb{M}}\mu_{\alpha}(A_{\alpha})\,\mathrm{d}\nu.

It is straightforward to check that
\Phi
is a Borel probability measure on
\mathbb{M}\times\mathbb{M}
that is absolutely continuous with respect to
\nu\times\nu
. Let
\bm{U}[\nu]
be the corresponding Radon-Nikodym derivative. It is then straightforward to verify that
\bm{U}[\nu]
coincides with
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha}}{\mathrm{d}\nu}
for
1\leq l\leq d
and for
\nu
-almost every
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
.
∎

The generated kernel
\bm{U}[\nu]
is a pushforward of
\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}
, as established in the following theorem.

Theorem C.19
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}(\xi,\zeta)=\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}](i_{\bm{w}}(\xi),i_{\bm{w}}(\zeta))

for
\mu\times\mu
-almost every
(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the theorem essentially replicates the proof of Theorem 6.10 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Theorem
C.19
.

Recall that by Proposition
C.17
,
\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}]
is well-defined because
\nu_{\bm{w}}
is a
2d
-DIDM and
i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)\in\mathbb{P}
for every
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
. Consequently,
\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}](i_{\bm{w}}(\xi),\cdot)=\frac{\mathrm{d}i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}}}
for
\mu
-almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
by Proposition
C.18
.

Define an integral kernel
\bm{U}
on
\mathbb{I}
as

\bm{U}(\xi,\zeta)=\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}](i_{\bm{w}}(\xi),i_{\bm{w}}(\zeta)).

It is sufficient to show that
T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}\rangle}=T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{U}\rangle}
for
1\leq l\leq d
.
By the definition of
\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}
and Corollary
C.16
, both
\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}
and
\bm{U}
are
(\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})\times\mathscr{C}(\bm{w}))
-measurable. This implies
T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}\rangle}(f)=T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{U}\rangle}(f)
for
1\leq l\leq d
and
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{I},\mathscr{C}(\bm{w}),\mu)^{\perp}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that

T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}\rangle}(\mathbbm{1}_{A})=T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{U}\rangle}(\mathbbm{1}_{A})

for every
A\in\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}
.

To this end, choose such an
A\in\mathscr{C}_{n}^{\bm{w}}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By Proposition
C.15
, we may assume (up to a
\mu
-null set) that there is
B\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{M}_{n})
such that
A=i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(B)
. Since
i_{\bm{w}}^{-1}(p_{n,\infty}^{-1}(B))=A
, we have

\displaystyle T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}\rangle}(\mathbbm{1}_{A})(\xi)=\int_{A}\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)=\int_{i_{\bm{w},n}^{-1}(B)}\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})}(\xi,\zeta)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)

\displaystyle=i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)(n+1)(l)(B)=\mu_{i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)}(l)(p_{n,\infty}^{-1}(B))

\displaystyle=\int_{p_{n,\infty}^{-1}(B)}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}i_{\bm{w}}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}}}\bigg)(l)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}}=\int_{p_{n,\infty}^{-1}(B)}\Big\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}](i_{\bm{w}}(\xi),\cdot)\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}}

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\Big\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{U}(\xi,\zeta)\Big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu(\zeta)

\displaystyle=T_{\langle\bm{e}_{l},\bm{U}\rangle}(\mathbbm{1}_{A})(\xi),

by definition of
i_{\bm{w}}
,
\mu_{\alpha}
, and
\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}]
for
\mu
-almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
.
∎

Corollary C.20
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
\bm{w}/\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})
is isomorphic to
\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}]
.

C.3.
Tree functions

In this section, we introduce the tensorized graph homomorphism of rooted trees as an alternative to
\nu_{\bm{w}}
to group the points in
\mathbb{I}
.

Recall Definition
2.20
, which we restate here in the context of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
: For any
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and an oriented simple graph
F
, define the tensorized graph homomorphism
\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}
as

\bm{t}(F,\bm{w})\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(F)}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(F)}\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(F)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

We define a rooted oriented tree as a pair
\mathfrak{T}=(T,v)
, where
T\in\mathcal{T}
is an oriented tree and
v\in\mathsf{v}(T)
is a distinguished vertex of
T
. The height
h(\mathfrak{T})
is the maximum number of edges in a path (considering
T
as undirected) that starts at
v
. We denote by
c(\mathfrak{T})
the neighbors of
v
in
T
(also considering
T
as undirected).

Every rooted oriented tree
\mathfrak{T}
can be decomposed into subtrees rooted in the neighbors of
v
. Specifically, there exists a sequence of
(\mathfrak{T}_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
, where each
\mathfrak{T}_{i}
is a rooted, oriented tree with root
i
, and a sequence
(e_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
, where each
e_{i}=(e_{i;1},e_{i;2})
is either
(v,i)
or
(i,v)
, such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}(T)

\displaystyle=\{v\}\cup\bigcup_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\mathsf{v}(T_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}(T)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\big(\mathsf{e}(T_{i})\cup\{e_{i}\}\big).

We call
(\mathfrak{T}_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
and
(e_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
the corresponding decomposition of
\mathfrak{T}
.

Note that if
h(\mathfrak{T})>0
, then
h(\mathfrak{T}_{i})<h(\mathfrak{T})
for every
i\in c(\mathfrak{T})
, and there exists
i\in c(\mathfrak{T})
such that
h(\mathfrak{T}_{i})+1=h(\mathfrak{T})
.

Definition C.21
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathfrak{T}=(T,v)
be a rooted oriented tree. We define
f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}:\mathbb{I}\to(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}
as

\displaystyle f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\xi_{v})=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{c(\mathfrak{T})}}\bigotimes_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\Big[\bm{w}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{i}}(\xi_{i})\Big]\prod_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i},

where
(\mathfrak{T}_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
and
(e_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
are the corresponding decomposition of
\mathfrak{T}
.

This definition is compatible with
\bm{w}
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebras, as stated by the next proposition.

Proposition C.22
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,v)
be a rooted oriented tree and let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\bm{w}
-invariant and relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra. Then
f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\mathscr{C}_{h(\mathfrak{T})}^{\bm{w}}
-measurable, and

\displaystyle f^{\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\xi_{v})=f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\xi_{v})

for
\mu
-almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the proposition essentially replicates the proof of Proposition 7.2 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Proposition
C.22
.

We prove both statements simultaneously by induction. If
h(\mathfrak{T})=0
, then the claim clearly holds. Suppose that
h(\mathfrak{T})=n+1
and that the claim holds for all rooted oriented trees of height at most
n
. Let
(\mathfrak{T}_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
and
(e_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
be the corresponding decomposition of
\mathfrak{T}
.
We have

\displaystyle f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\xi_{v})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{c(\mathfrak{T})}}\bigotimes_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\Big[\bm{w}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{i}}(\xi_{i})\Big]\prod_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}}\bm{w}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{i}}(\xi_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}}\bm{w}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes f^{\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{i}}(\xi_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}}\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes f^{\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{i}}(\xi_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg)

\displaystyle=f^{\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\xi_{v}),

for
\mu
-almost every
\xi\in\mathbb{I}
. By definition, we see that
f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\mathscr{C}_{n+1}^{\bm{w}}
-measurable and that ﬁnishes the proof.
∎

When integrating over
\mathbb{I}
, the function
f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
reduces to
\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})
, as stated in the following proposition.

Proposition C.23
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,v)
be a rooted directed tree, and let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\bm{w}
-invariant and relatively complete sub-
\sigma
-algebra. Then

\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})=\int_{\mathbb{I}}f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\xi_{v})\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{v}.

In particular,
\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})=\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}})=\bm{t}(T,\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}])
for every
T\in\mathcal{T}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the proposition essentially replicates the proof of Proposition 7.3 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Proposition
C.23
.

We proceed by induction on the height
h(\mathfrak{T})
. If
h(\mathfrak{T})=0
, then the claim clearly holds. Suppose that
h(\mathfrak{T})=n+1
and that the claim holds for all rooted oriented trees of height at most
n
. Let
(\mathfrak{T}_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
and
(e_{i})_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}
be the corresponding decomposition of
\mathfrak{T}
.
Then for fixed
\xi_{v}\in\mathbb{I}
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{I}}\bm{w}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{i}}(\xi_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(T_{i})}}\bm{w}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes\bigotimes_{(k,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T_{i})}\bm{w}(\xi_{k},\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(T_{i})}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}.

This gives

\displaystyle\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(T)}}\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{w}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(T)}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}}\bigotimes_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}^{\mathsf{v}(T_{i})}}\bm{w}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes\bigotimes_{(k,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T_{i})}\bm{w}(\xi_{k},\xi_{j})\prod_{i\in\mathsf{v}(T_{i})}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{v}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}}\bigotimes_{i\in c(\mathfrak{T})}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{I}}\bm{w}(\xi_{e_{i;1}},\xi_{e_{i;2}})\otimes f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}_{i}}(\xi_{i})\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\bigg)\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{v}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{I}}f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\xi_{v})\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{v}.

The rest follows from Proposition
C.22
.
∎

C.4.
Stone-Weierstrass Theorem

In this section, we show that
\nu_{\bm{w}}
is determined by the family
f^{\bm{w}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, where
\mathfrak{T}
ranges over all rooted oriented trees.

We define a collection
\mathscr{T}\subset C(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{R})
that is closed under multiplication and contains the constant function
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
.
The construction proceeds recursively on
n\in\mathbb{N}
: in each step
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we construct a subset
\mathscr{T}_{n}\subset C(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{R})
that factors through
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Specifically, for every
f\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
, there exists an
f^{\prime}\in C(\mathbb{M}_{n},\mathbb{R})
such that
f=f^{\prime}\circ p_{n,\infty}
, and
\mathscr{T}_{n}
is uniformly dense in
C(\mathbb{M}_{n},\mathbb{R})\circ p_{n,\infty}
.

The set
\mathscr{T}_{n+1}
is constructed from
\mathscr{T}_{n}
using two operations. Informally, these operations correspond to the following constructions on finite trees, with the correspondence made precise in the proof.

(I)
: Given a rooted tree, add an extra vertex to serve as the new root, with its only neighbor being the original root.

(II)
: Given a collection of finitely many rooted trees
\{\mathfrak{T}^{j}\}
, define a new rooted tree
\mathfrak{T}
as the disjoint union of
\{\mathfrak{T}^{j}\}
whose roots are glued together to form a single new root for
\mathfrak{T}
.

Definition C.24
.

Let
n,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq l\leq 2d
, and
f,f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\in C(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{R})
be such that
f
factors through
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. Then, for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
, define

\displaystyle F(f,n,l)(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{M}_{n}}f^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha(n+1)(l),\quad\text{where }f^{\prime}\in C(\mathbb{M}_{n},\mathbb{R})\text{ and }f=f^{\prime}\circ p_{n,\infty},

and

\displaystyle G(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})(\alpha)=\prod_{j=1}^{k}f_{j}(\alpha).

It is straightforward to see, by the definition of
\mathbb{M}
, that
F(f,n,l)
and
G(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
are elements of
C(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{R})
, where
F(f,n,l)
factors through
\mathbb{M}_{n+1}
and
G(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
factors through
\mathbb{M}_{n}
.

We begin by setting
\mathscr{T}_{0}=\{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}\}
. Suppose
\mathscr{T}_{n}
has been defined. Then we define

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{n+1}=\big\{G(f_{1},\dots,f_{k}):\forall 1\leq i\leq k,

\displaystyle\exists g_{i}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}\,(g_{i}=f_{i}\text{ or }f_{i}=F(g_{i},n,l)\text{ for some }1\leq l\leq 2d)\big\}.

That is, we first apply
(I)
to add new elements, then apply
(II)
to all existing and newly added elements.

Proposition C.25
.

The collection
\mathscr{T}
is closed under multiplication, contains
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
, and separates the points of
\mathbb{M}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the proposition essentially replicates the proof of Proposition 7.5 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Proposition
C.25
.

We need only to show that
\mathscr{T}
separates points. We will demonstrate by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
that
\mathscr{T}_{n}
separates any pair of points
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{M}
whenever there exist
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq l\leq 2d
such that
\alpha(i)(l)\neq\beta(i)(l)
. This is sufficient to prove the claim, since each
\mathscr{T}_{n}
is closed under multiplication and contains
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}}
by
(II)
.

If
n=0
, there is nothing to prove. Suppose that the claim holds for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Now, consider
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{M}
with
\alpha(i)(l)\neq\beta(i)(l)
for some
1\leq i\leq n+1
and
1\leq l\leq 2d
. By the inductive assumption, either there already exists
f\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
such that
f(\alpha)\neq f(\beta)
, or
i=n+1
.

Define
\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\prime}=\{f^{\prime}\in C(\mathbb{M}_{n},\mathbb{R}):\exists f\in\mathscr{T}_{n},\,f=f^{\prime}\circ p_{n,\infty}\}
. By the inductive assumption,
\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\prime}
is closed under multiplication, contains
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{M}_{n}}
, and separates points of
\mathbb{M}_{n}
. By the Stone-Weierstrass theorem, the algebra generated by
\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\prime}
is dense in
C(\mathbb{M}_{n},\mathbb{R})
.

Since
\alpha(n+1)(l),\beta(n+1)(l)\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{M}_{n})
and
\alpha(n+1)(l)\neq\beta(n+1)(l)
, there exists
f^{\prime}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{M}_{n}}f^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha(n+1)(l)\neq\int_{\mathbb{M}_{n}}f^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}\beta(n+1)(l).

Thus, we have
F(f,n,l)(\alpha)\neq F(f,n,l)(\beta)
, where
f\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
is such that
f=f^{\prime}\circ p_{n,\infty}
.
Since
F(f,n,l)\in\mathscr{T}_{n+1}
by
(I)
, the proof is complete.

∎

Proposition C.26
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{T}
. Then there exists a finite rooted tree
\mathfrak{T}=(T,v)
and a sequence
(l_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\in\{1,\dots,d\}^{\mathsf{e}(T)}
such that for every
2d
-DIDM
\nu
, we have

\displaystyle f(\alpha)=\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\,f^{\bm{U}[\nu]}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\alpha)\bigg\rangle

for
\nu
-almost every
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
.

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the proposition essentially replicates the proof of Proposition 7.6 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Proposition
C.26
.

We prove the claim by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It is easy to see that if
f=1_{\mathbb{M}_{1}}
, then a tree
\mathfrak{T}
satisfying
h(\mathfrak{T})=0
works; in other words, the claim holds for
\mathscr{T}_{0}
.

Suppose that the claim holds for
\mathscr{T}_{n}
where
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
f=F(g,n,l)
for some
g\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
and
1\leq l\leq 2d
. Fix a rooted directed tree
\mathfrak{S}=(S,w)
and
(l_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(G)}\in\{1,\dots,d\}^{\mathsf{e}(G)}
, corresponding to
g
, and let
g^{\prime}\in C(\mathbb{M}_{n},\mathbb{R})
such that
g=g^{\prime}\circ p_{n,\infty}
. Define a rooted, directed tree
\mathfrak{T}
such that
c(\mathfrak{T})=1
and
(\mathfrak{S})
and
(e_{w})
are the corresponding decomposition of
\mathfrak{T}
, where
e_{w}=(v,w)
if
1\leq l\leq d
and
e_{w}=(w,v)
if
d+1\leq l\leq 2d
.
We also define
l_{v,w}=l
if
1\leq l\leq d
and
l_{w,v}=l-d
if
d+1\leq l\leq 2d
.
In other words, we add an extra vertex as the new root. The only neighbor of the new root is the old root and the direction of the new edge depends on
l
.

Given a DIDM
\nu
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},\,f^{\bm{U}[\nu]}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\alpha_{v})\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{M}}\bigg\langle\bm{e}_{l_{e_{w;1},e_{w;2}}},\bm{U}[\nu](\alpha_{e_{w;1}},\alpha_{e_{w;2}})\bigg\rangle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(S)}\bm{e}_{l_{i,j}},f_{\mathfrak{S}}^{\bm{U}[\nu]}(\alpha_{w})\bigg\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu(\alpha_{w})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{M}}\bigg\langle\bm{e}_{l_{e_{w;1},e_{w;2}}},\bm{U}[\nu](\alpha_{e_{w;1}},\alpha_{e_{w;2}})\bigg\rangle g(\alpha_{w})\,\mathrm{d}\nu(\alpha_{w})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{M}}g\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha}(l)=\int_{\mathbb{M}}g^{\prime}\circ p_{n,\infty}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha}(l)=\int_{\mathbb{M}_{n}}g^{\prime}\mathrm{d}(p_{n,\infty})_{\#}\mu_{\alpha}(l)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{M}_{n}}g^{\prime}\,d\alpha(n+1)(l)=F(g,n,l)(\alpha)=f(\alpha)

for
\nu
-almost every
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{T}_{n+1}
. By definition, we have
f=G(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
for some
f_{i}
such that either
f_{i}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
or
f_{i}=F(g_{i},n,l_{i})
for some
g_{i}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}
,
1\leq l_{i}\leq 2d
. In both cases, either by the inductive assumption or by the previous paragraph, we find a rooted, directed tree
\mathfrak{T}^{i}=(T^{i},v^{i})
and a labeling
(l_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T^{i})}\in\{1,\dots,d\}^{\mathsf{e}(T^{i})}
that satisfies the claim for
f_{i}
for every
1\leq i\leq k
. Let
\{\mathfrak{T}_{j}^{i}\}_{j=1}^{|c(\mathfrak{T}^{i})|}
and
(e_{j}^{i})_{j=1}^{|c(\mathfrak{T}^{i})|}
be the corresponding decomposition of
\mathfrak{T}^{i}
, where
\mathfrak{T}_{j}^{i}=(T_{j}^{i},v_{j}^{i})
for every
1\leq i\leq k
.

Define the index set
I=\{(i,j):1\leq i\leq k,1\leq j\leq|c(\mathfrak{T}^{i})|\}
and construct
\mathfrak{T}=(T,v)
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}(T)=\{v\}\cup\bigcup_{(i,j)\in I}\mathsf{v}(T_{j}^{i})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{e}(T)=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in I}\{\bar{e}_{j;1}^{i},\bar{e}_{j;2}^{i}\}\cup\mathsf{e}(T_{j}^{i}).

where

\bar{e}_{j}^{i}=(\bar{e}_{j;1}^{i},\bar{e}_{j;2}^{i})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(v,v_{j}^{i})&&\text{ if }e_{j}^{i}=(v^{i},v_{j}^{i}),\\
&(v_{j}^{i},v)&&\text{ if }e_{j}^{i}=(v_{j}^{i},v^{i}).\end{aligned}\right.

Moreover, set
l_{\bar{e}_{j;1}^{i},\bar{e}_{j;2}^{i}}=l_{e_{j;1}^{i},e_{j;2}^{i}}
for all
(i,j)\in I
.

Note that
\{\mathfrak{T}_{j}^{i}\}_{(i,j)\in I}
and
(\bar{e}_{j}^{i})_{(i,j)\in I}
are the corresponding decomposition of
\mathfrak{T}
.
Given a
2d
-DIDM
\nu
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},\,f^{\bm{U}[\nu]}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\alpha_{v})\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{M}^{I}}\prod_{(i,j)\in I}\bigg(\bigg\langle\bm{e}_{l_{\bar{e}_{j;1}^{i},\bar{e}_{j;2}^{i}}},\bm{U}[\nu](\alpha_{\bar{e}_{j;1}^{i}},\alpha_{\bar{e}_{j;2}^{i}})\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T_{j}^{i})}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},f_{\mathfrak{S}}^{\bm{U}[\nu]}(\alpha_{v_{j}^{i}})\bigg\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu(\alpha_{v_{j}^{i}})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bigg[\int_{\mathbb{M}^{c(\mathfrak{T}^{i})}}\prod_{j=1}^{|c(\mathfrak{T}^{i})|}\bigg(\bigg\langle\bm{e}_{l_{\bar{e}_{j;1}^{i},\bar{e}_{j;2}^{i}}},\bm{U}[\nu](\alpha_{\bar{e}_{j;1}^{i}},\alpha_{\bar{e}_{j;2}^{i}})\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T_{j}^{i})}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},f_{\mathfrak{S}}^{\bm{U}[\nu]}(\alpha_{v_{j}^{i}})\bigg\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu(\alpha_{v_{j}^{i}})\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T^{i})}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},\,f^{\bm{U}[\nu]}_{\mathfrak{T}^{i}}(\alpha_{v})\bigg\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}(\alpha_{v})=f(\alpha_{v})

for
\nu
-almost every
\alpha\in\mathbb{M}
, which concludes the proof.

∎

Corollary C.27
.

The map
\bm{w}\mapsto\nu_{\bm{w}}
is continuous when
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}\times\mathbb{I};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is endowed with the cut distance
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{M})
is endowed with the weak-* topology.

Moreover, if
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\bm{u}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\nu_{\bm{u}}\neq\nu_{\bm{w}}
, then there exists an oriented tree
T\in\mathcal{T}
such that

\displaystyle\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\neq\bm{t}(T,\bm{u}).

Aside from adjustments for the multi-dimension, our proof of the corollary essentially replicates the proof of Corollary 7.7 in
[
18
]
.

Proof of Corollary
C.27
.

It follows from the Stone-Weierstrass theorem together with Proposition
C.25
that the algebra generated by
\mathscr{T}
is uniformly dense in
C(\mathbb{M},\mathbb{R})
. Consequently, the weak-* topology on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{M})
is generated by functionals corresponding to elements of
\mathscr{T}
.

Let
\bm{w}_{n}
converge to
\bm{w}
in
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and let
f\in\mathscr{T}
. Fix a (rooted) oriented tree
\mathfrak{T}=(T,v)
and
(l_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}
that corresponds to
f
as in Proposition
C.26
. By Proposition
C.23
and Proposition
C.26
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{M}}f\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}_{n}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},\,\bm{t}(T,\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}_{n}}])\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},\,\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}_{n})\bigg\rangle,

and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{M}}f\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}_{n}}

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},\,\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},\,\bm{t}(T,\bm{U}[\nu_{\bm{w}}])\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{M}}f\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}}.

This proves the continuity.

Suppose that
\nu_{\bm{w}}\neq\nu_{\bm{u}}
. By the Stone-Weierstrass theorem together with Proposition
C.25
, there exists an
f\in\mathscr{T}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{M}}f\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{w}}\neq\int_{\mathbb{M}}f\mathrm{d}\nu_{\bm{u}}.

The rooted oriented tree
\mathfrak{T}=(T,v)
and the assignment
(l_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}
corresponding to
f
then satisfies

\displaystyle\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},\,\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\bigg\rangle\neq\bigg\langle\bigotimes_{(p,q)\in\mathsf{e}(T)}\bm{e}_{l_{p,q}},\,\bm{t}(T,\bm{u})\bigg\rangle

which completes the proof.
∎

C.5.
Summary of results

We are now at the point of summarizing all the results of the section:

Theorem C.28
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\bm{u}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})=\bm{t}(T,\bm{u})
,
\forall T\in\mathcal{T}
.

(2)

\nu_{\bm{w}}=\nu_{\bm{u}}
.

(3)

\bm{w}/\mathscr{C}(\bm{w})
and
\bm{u}/\mathscr{C}(\bm{u})
are isomorphic.

(4)

There is a coupling
\gamma\in\Pi(\mathbb{I}_{1},\mathbb{I}_{2})
such that
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}\circ T_{\gamma}=T_{\gamma}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{u}\rangle}
for all
\bm{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(5)

There is a
\bm{w}
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{C}
and a
\bm{u}
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{D}
such that
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{\mathscr{C}},\bm{u}_{\mathscr{D}})=0
.

What we need is the equivalence of (1) and (4), which we restate as the following corollary:

Corollary C.29
.

Let
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}_{1}\times\mathbb{I}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\bm{u}\in L^{\infty}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{I}_{2}\times\mathbb{I}_{2};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1’)

\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{u})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(T)}}
,
\forall T\in\mathcal{T}

(4’)

\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w},\bm{u})=0
.

Proof of Theorem
C.28
.

The implications (5)
\implies
(1)
\implies
(2)
\implies
(3) follow directly from Proposition
C.23
, Corollary
C.27
, and Corollary
C.20
.

For parts (3)
\implies
(4)
\implies
(5), the proofs are identical to those of Theorem 8.1 in
[
18
]
, so we refer the readers to the original literature and provide only a brief outline.

For (3)
\implies
(4), the adjacency operator
T_{\gamma}:L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{1})
can be indentified from the canonical maps

\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{2}/\mathscr{C}(\bm{u}))\cong L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{1}/\mathscr{C}(\bm{w}))\to L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{1}).

It is straightforward to verify that
\gamma
is a coupling and that the identity
T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{w}\rangle}\circ T_{\gamma}=T_{\gamma}\circ T_{\langle\bm{e},\bm{u}\rangle}
holds.

For (4)
\implies
(5), this follows by a careful yet straightforward application of the Mean Ergodic Theorem to
(T_{\gamma}\circ T_{\gamma^{T}}):L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{1})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{1})
and
T_{\gamma^{T}}\circ T_{\gamma}:L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{I}_{2})
, both of which are Markov operators.

∎

This pointwise equivalence can be extended to the topological equivalence in Lemma
2.31
, following the argument suggested in
[
5
]
.

Proof of Lemma
2.31
.

We begin by assuming
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and prove the lemma by contradiction.

Suppose there exists a sequence
\bm{w}_{n}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})=0
, but there exists a tree
T\in\mathcal{T}
and an
\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}>\epsilon.

By compactness of
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, up to extracting a subsequence (which we still denote by
\bm{w}_{n}
for simplicity), we can assume that
\bm{w}_{n}
converges in
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
(and in
\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
) to some limit
\bm{w}^{\prime}
.
Since the limit in
\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is unique, we have
\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}^{\prime},\bm{w})=0
. Thus, by Corollary
C.29
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}^{\prime})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=0.

Hence, on this extracted subsequence
\bm{w}_{n}
, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}^{\prime})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}\to 0,

where the convergence follows from Lemma
2.30
(Counting Lemma associated with
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
) and the fact that
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w}^{\prime})\to 0
. This leads to a contradiction.

For the converse direction, assume there exists a sequence such that for all
T\in\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=0,

but there exists an
\epsilon>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})>\epsilon.

Again, by the compactness of
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we can extract a subsequence (which we still denote by
\bm{w}_{n}
for simplicity) that converges in
\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}
to some
\bm{w}^{\prime}
.
It is straightforward that for all
T\in\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}^{\prime})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\|_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(F)}}=0,

hence, by Corollary
C.29
,
\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}^{\prime},\bm{w})=0
.
Hence, on this extracted subsequence
\bm{w}_{n}
, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w}^{\prime})\leq\delta_{\square;\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w}^{\prime})\to 0,

leading again to a contradiction.

Finally, consider a general compact embedding
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
.
By compactness, there exists a sequence of finite-dimensional subspaces
\mathcal{H}_{d}
, with
d\to\infty
, such that for any
\bm{u}\in\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
, the projection
P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{u}-\bm{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H}}(d)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathcal{B}}.

Recall the techniques from the proof of Lemma
B.7
and Lemma
B.18
, we have, for any
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})
with
\|\bm{w}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,1]^{2};\mathcal{B})}\leq w_{\max}
,
that

\displaystyle\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w},\bm{w})

\displaystyle\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H},w_{\max}}(d),

\displaystyle\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}_{n},P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{\square;\mathcal{H}}(\bm{w}_{n},\bm{w}),

and, for all
T\in\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(T,P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(T)}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\downarrow}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{H},w_{\max},T}(d),

\displaystyle\|\bm{t}(T,P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(T,P_{\mathcal{H}_{d}}\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(T)}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{t}(T,\bm{w}_{n})-\bm{t}(T,\bm{w})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes\mathsf{e}(T)}}.

This allows extending the finite-dimensional convergence to
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{H}
in both directions by passing to the limit
d\to\infty
.

∎
